Jef’s Handbooks · Handbook 3

Linux for Cloud Engineers

The server under everything, explained

Part I · Finding your way

1

What a Linux server is

A server is just a computer that nobody sits in front of, and most of the ones in the cloud run Linux. This chapter separates the parts that word covers (the kernel, the distribution, the programs around them), follows a machine from power-on to a login prompt, and gives you a calm, read-only way to find out what you've been handed.

By the end you can

  • Explain what the kernel does and what the distribution adds around it.
  • Say which distribution and release a server runs, and until when it gets security updates.
  • Describe the boot sequence from firmware to PID 1, and what changes on a cloud VM or in a container.
  • Explain what a cloud image and cloud-init do at first boot.
  • Inventory an unfamiliar server without changing anything on it.

Before you start

  • A terminal on any Linux machine: a container, WSL on Windows, or a small VM. Appendix C shows how to get one.
  • You can type a command and press Enter. Chapter 2 covers the shell properly.

This chapter

Reading
about 35 minutes
Lab
20 minutes, any Linux terminal, free
Main distribution
Ubuntu, with Debian, the RHEL family and Amazon Linux in 1.7

At Stagedoor

Stagedoor sells tickets for about six hundred music venues in Portugal. Fans buy on the web and in the app; door staff scan the tickets on their phones. Most weeks the traffic is calm. Then a popular band announces a tour, tickets go on sale at 10:00 on a Friday, and for ten minutes everything is busy at once.

On Monday 5 January 2026, Rafaela Costa starts as Stagedoor's first operations engineer. Leonor Ferreira, the CTO, wrote the first version of the platform in 2023 and built its first server herself. She welcomes Rafaela, apologises for the state of things, and gives her three names, door-1, door-2 and door-3, and a pinned message in the team chat's #ops channel: the root password, unchanged since 2023. Nobody has written down what runs where. “It works,” Leonor says. “Mostly. Please don't break it during an on-sale.”

Rafaela's first rule, which she writes at the top of a new page in the wiki: nothing changes until it's written down. Her first job is to find out what she's been handed, without changing anything. Section 1.8 shows what she found.

1.1Kernel, userland, distribution

“Linux” means three different things depending on who says it, and it helps to keep them apart from the start.

Strictly, Linux is a kernel: the one program on the machine that talks to the hardware directly. It starts first, stays in memory until the machine shuts down, and does five jobs for everything else:

  • Processes: it starts programs, gives each a share of the CPUs, and stops them (Chapter 5, Chapter 9).
  • Memory: it hands out memory, keeps each program's memory private from the others, and decides what to do when it runs out (Chapter 10).
  • Files: it turns blocks on a disk into files and directories (Chapter 3, Chapter 11).
  • Network: it sends and receives packets and keeps track of connections (Chapter 13).
  • Devices and permissions: it drives disks, network cards and consoles, and checks who may do what (Chapter 4).

Programs never touch the hardware themselves. When a program wants to open a file, send a packet or start another program, it asks the kernel with a system call: a numbered request such as openat, read, write or execve. The kernel checks the request, does the work and returns a result or an error. Everything outside the kernel, from the shell to your app, is called userland (or user space).

A kernel on its own can't do anything useful for you: there's no shell to type into and no program to start your web server. A distribution (a “distro”) packages the Linux kernel together with a userland (thousands of programs and libraries, mostly from the GNU project and others), a package manager to install and update them (Chapter 8), and a promise to ship security fixes for a stated number of years. Ubuntu, Debian, Red Hat Enterprise Linux and Amazon Linux are distributions. When people say “a Linux server”, they mean a machine running one.

Key idea

The kernel does the work; programs only ask. Almost every problem in this book, from “permission denied” to “too many open files”, is the kernel refusing or running out of something a program asked for.

Kernel space, user spaceThe CPU itself enforces the split: kernel code runs in a privileged mode that can touch hardware and any memory; user programs run in a restricted mode and can't. A bug in a program crashes the program. A bug in the kernel can crash the machine.

1.2Distributions and releases

The distributions you'll meet on cloud servers differ in their package format, their defaults and, most importantly for a server, how long each release is supported. A release is a numbered version of the whole distribution (Ubuntu 24.04, Debian 12) whose packages stay stable: during its support period you get security fixes and bug fixes, not new major versions that might change behaviour under you. When support ends, the fixes stop, and every new vulnerability stays open on that machine for good.

Table 1.1 · Distributions on cloud servers (support periods at the time of writing; check the vendor's page)
DistributionPackagesReleasesSupport per releaseYou'll meet it
Ubuntu LTS.deb, aptevery two years, in April (22.04, 24.04, 26.04)5 years of standard security updates; more with Ubuntu Prothe most common image on AWS, Azure and Google Cloud; this book's main distribution
Debian stable.deb, aptabout every two years (11 “bullseye”, 12 “bookworm”, 13 “trixie”)about 3 years, plus about 2 more from the Debian LTS teamsmall servers, container base images; Ubuntu is built from it
RHEL family (Red Hat Enterprise Linux, Rocky, AlmaLinux).rpm, dnfa major version every three years or so (8, 9, 10)10 yearsenterprises, vendor software that “supports RHEL”
Amazon Linux 2023.rpm, dnfone release, updated in placeuntil 2029the default image on AWS; tuned for EC2

LTS means long-term support: a release meant to be run for years, as servers are. Ubuntu also publishes releases between LTS versions (24.10, 25.04) with nine months of support each; they're fine on a laptop and wrong on a server, because you'd have to upgrade twice a year.

Watch out: end of support is a date, not a feeling

A server past its release's end of support keeps working, which is exactly why it's dangerous: nothing breaks, so nobody notices that security fixes stopped. Write the end-of-support date on every server's card the day you meet it, and plan the upgrade or rebuild a few months before (Chapter 8).

Two files answer “what is this?” on any modern distribution. /etc/os-release names the distribution and release; uname -r prints the running kernel's version. They're different things: Ubuntu 24.04 ships a 6.8 kernel, can be moved to newer ones, and the kernel running can be older than the one installed if nobody has rebooted since the last kernel update.

1.3How a machine boots

Knowing the boot sequence pays off the first time a server doesn't come back after a reboot: each stage hands over to the next, and the question is always which hand-over failed.

  1. Firmware. The machine's built-in firmware (UEFI on modern machines, BIOS on old ones) checks the hardware and looks for something bootable on a disk.
  2. Bootloader. GRUB, on most distributions, loads two files from /boot into memory: the kernel (vmlinuz-…) and the initramfs, a small temporary filesystem with just enough drivers to find the real disk.
  3. Kernel. The kernel starts, detects hardware, uses the initramfs to find and mount the root filesystem (the one mounted at /), and throws the initramfs away.
  4. PID 1. The kernel starts exactly one program itself: /sbin/init, which becomes process number 1. On Ubuntu, Debian, RHEL and Amazon Linux, that's systemd. Every other process on the machine is started by PID 1 or by one of its descendants (Chapter 5).
  5. Services. systemd starts what's configured to run at boot, in dependency order: mounting disks, the network, SSH, then services such as nginx and your app (Chapter 6).

If a program was started by hand (someone logged in and typed a command) rather than as a service, step 5 doesn't know about it, and after a reboot it simply isn't running. That's one of the most common surprises on hand-built servers, and it's waiting for Stagedoor in this chapter's story.

1.4Cloud images and cloud-init

Nobody installs Linux from a USB stick on a cloud server. You pick an image: a ready-made copy of a disk with a distribution already installed (an AMI on AWS, an image on Azure and Google Cloud). Launching a VM copies the image to a new disk and boots it. Ubuntu, Debian, Red Hat and Amazon publish official images for every release and refresh them every few weeks with the latest updates.

An image is the same for everyone, so something must turn it into your server at first boot: set its hostname, create users, install your SSH key, maybe install packages and start your app. On nearly every cloud image, that something is cloud-init. It runs as a set of services early in boot (stage 5 of Figure 1.2), asks the cloud's instance metadata service (a web service only the VM can reach, at 169.254.169.254) for the user data you supplied when you launched the VM, and applies it. User data is usually a shell script or a YAML file that starts with #cloud-config:

user-data.yamlcloud-config (an illustration; Chapter 18 builds a tested one)

#cloud-config
hostname: door-4
users:
  - name: rafaela
    groups: [sudo]
    shell: /bin/bash
    ssh_authorized_keys:
      - ssh-ed25519 AAAAC3Nza…rafaela-laptop
package_update: true
packages: [nginx]
runcmd:
  - [systemctl, enable, --now, nginx]

Read it top to bottom: name the machine, create one personal user who can use sudo and log in with one key, update the package lists, install nginx and start it. cloud-init runs most of this only on the first boot of a new instance; rebooting doesn't repeat it. Its log, /var/log/cloud-init-output.log, is the first place to look when a new server didn't come up the way you described.

Try it locallyMultipass, a free tool for Ubuntu VMs on a laptop, accepts the same file: multipass launch --cloud-init user-data.yaml (Appendix C).

This is the first glimpse of an idea the whole book works towards: a server described in a file can be rebuilt in minutes, while a server built by hand over three years can only be repaired, carefully. Chapter 18 writes a hardened baseline as cloud-init, and Chapter 21 rebuilds one of Stagedoor's servers from a script.

1.5Finding out what you've been handed

The first hour on an unfamiliar server should change nothing. Every question below has a read-only command, and most of the answers come from two places the kernel keeps up to date: /proc and /sys. They look like directories full of files, but nothing in them is stored on a disk; the kernel generates each “file” when you read it (Chapter 3).

Table 1.2 · An inventory in fourteen questions
QuestionCommandNotes
What is it called?hostnamealso in the shell prompt, usually
Which distribution and release?cat /etc/os-releasethen look up its end of support
Which kernel is running?uname -rcompare with ls /boot: a newer kernel there means it's waiting for a reboot
Is a reboot pending?cat /var/run/reboot-requiredUbuntu and Debian; “No such file” means no
How long has it been up?uptimealso the load average (Chapter 9)
What is PID 1?ps -o comm= -p 1systemd on a normal server
How many CPUs?nprocdetails in lscpu
How much memory?free -hread the available column (Chapter 10)
Which disks, how full?lsblk, df -hChapter 11
Which addresses?ip -brief addressChapter 13
What's listening?ss -tlnpwith sudo, -p names the program
Which services run?systemctl list-units --type=service --state=runningonly on machines where PID 1 is systemd
What else runs?ps -ef --forestprograms started by hand show up here but not above
Who logs in?last -n 20, getent passwdChapter 4, Chapter 16

The two rows about running programs are worth comparing on any hand-built server. Something in the ps list that isn't in the systemctl list was started some other way: from a terminal, from cron, from a forgotten script. It won't survive a reboot unless something starts it again.

Watch out: “just a quick restart”

On a server you don't understand yet, a reboot is a change, not a check. If anything was started by hand, it won't come back. Do the inventory first, find out how each running program was started, and reboot only when you know what to expect.

1.6Pets and cattle

Operations people describe two ways of looking after servers. A pet server has a name, was built by hand, and is nursed back to health when it's ill, because nobody knows exactly how to make another one. Cattle are numbered, built from a description (an image, cloud-init, a script), and replaced when they misbehave. The terms are blunt, but the difference is real: a pet holds knowledge that exists nowhere else, and losing it loses the knowledge too.

Almost every company starts with pets, and that's fine. The way out isn't to throw them away but to move the knowledge out of the machine and into writing, one piece at a time:

  1. Inventory: what's on it, what runs, who logs in (this chapter).
  2. Understand: for each running program, how it starts, where its config and data live, where it logs (Chapters 3–8).
  3. Fix in place, writing each fix down as a command you could run again (Chapters 4–16).
  4. Script it: turn the written-down commands into a script or cloud-init (Chapters 17–18).
  5. Rebuild a copy from the script, test it, and replace the pet (Chapter 21).

1.7Across distributions

Everything in this chapter works the same on every mainstream distribution: the kernel, the boot sequence, /proc, systemd and cloud-init. What changes is mostly the package manager and where a few configuration files live, which Appendix B lists side by side.

On the RHEL family

Red Hat Enterprise Linux and its rebuilds (Rocky Linux, AlmaLinux) use .rpm packages and dnf, and turn on SELinux by default, an extra permission layer that Chapter 18 introduces. The release is in /etc/os-release as on Ubuntu, and also in /etc/redhat-release. Ten years of support per major version is why enterprises like it, and why you'll meet very old ones.

On Amazon Linux

Amazon Linux 2023 is based on Fedora, uses dnf, and is updated in place: instead of upgrading to a new release every few years, you move the machine to a newer, versioned snapshot of its package repository. Its older sibling, Amazon Linux 2, is at the end of its support period: check the date before building anything new on it. AWS's own tools come preinstalled, which is handy on EC2 and irrelevant elsewhere.

1.8Lab: a server card

Lab 1 · Describe a machine without touching it

You'll write a small script that prints a one-page card for any Linux machine, run it, and read what it says, including what it says about the machine not being a normal server. You need a terminal on any Linux (Appendix C). Cost: nothing. Clean-up: delete the script; it changes nothing on the machine.

  1. Save the script as servercard.sh in your home directory. Every line is one command from Table 1.2 wrapped in echo; $( … ) means “run this and put its output here”, which Chapter 2 explains along with the pipes (|).

    servercard.shbash

    #!/usr/bin/env bash
# servercard.sh · a one-page description of this machine. It only reads; it changes nothing.

echo "== $(hostname) =="
echo "OS:        $(. /etc/os-release && echo "$PRETTY_NAME")"
echo "Kernel:    $(uname -r) on $(uname -m)"
echo "Up since:  $(uptime -s)"
echo "PID 1:     $(ps -o comm= -p 1)"
echo "CPUs:      $(nproc)"
echo "Memory:    $(free -h | awk '/^Mem:/ {print $2 " total, " $7 " available"}')"
echo "Root disk: $(df -h --output=size,pcent / | tail -1 | awk '{print $1 ", " $2 " used"}')"
echo "Addresses:"
ip -brief address | sed 's/^/  /'
echo "Listening on TCP:"
ss -tlnH | awk '{print "  " $4}' | sort -u
  2. Make it executable and run it: 
    chmod +x servercard.sh
./servercard.sh
    Here's what it printed on the machine this book's labs were tested on. Yours will differ, and that's the point: 
    == vm ==
OS:        Ubuntu 24.04.5 LTS
Kernel:    6.18.44-fc-v880 on x86_64
Up since:  2026-11-27 07:48:25
PID 1:     process_api
CPUs:      4
Memory:    15Gi total, 15Gi available
Root disk: 252G, 14% used
Addresses:
  lo               UNKNOWN        127.0.0.1/8 
  ifb0             DOWN           
  ifb1             DOWN           
  eth0             UP             192.0.2.2/24 
Listening on TCP:
  0.0.0.0:2024
  127.0.0.1:41381
  127.0.0.1:44513
  3. Read the card. This one has some surprises, and spotting them is the skill. The kernel's version string ends in -fc-…, not Ubuntu's usual -generic, and PID 1 is not systemd: it's a program called process_api. This machine is a lightweight virtual machine (a Firecracker microVM) whose provider boots its own kernel and its own small init program; Ubuntu supplies only the userland. That's a perfectly good Linux machine for most of this book's labs, but systemctl won't work on it, so the systemd chapters use a full VM instead. On a container you'd see something similar: PID 1 would be bash or your app. On a normal Ubuntu server you'd see systemd and a kernel such as 6.8.0-…-generic or …-aws.
  4. Compare running and installed kernels. Run uname -r and ls /boot. On a normal server, if /boot holds a vmlinuz- file with a newer version than uname -r, security fixes for the kernel are installed but not running. On a microVM or container, /boot may be empty or missing: the kernel comes from outside.
  5. Break it on purpose. Remove the execute permission and run the script again: 
    chmod -x servercard.sh
./servercard.sh; echo "exit status: $?"
    bash: ./servercard.sh: Permission denied
exit status: 126
    The kernel refused to run the file because its permissions don't allow it (Chapter 4), and the shell reports that with exit status 126 (Chapter 2). bash servercard.sh still works, because then the kernel runs bash, and bash merely reads the file. Put the permission back with chmod +x servercard.sh.

Stretch goals. (1) Add a line for “reboot pending” that prints yes if /var/run/reboot-required exists and no otherwise (hint: test -e, Chapter 2). (2) Add the number of running processes (ps -e --no-headers | wc -l). (3) Run the card on a second machine, or in a container (docker run --rm -it ubuntu:24.04), and list three differences.

At Stagedoor: three cards

By the end of her first week, Rafaela has a card for each server in the wiki, made with a script much like this one, plus a few notes per machine written by hand:

Table 1.3 · Stagedoor's servers on 9 January 2026
HostReleaseSizeRunsNotes
door-1Debian 11, built March 20238 vCPU, 32 GBPostgreSQL 13, the nightly jobsup 401 days; two newer kernels installed but not running; Debian 11's security support ends 31 August 2026
door-2Ubuntu 22.04, April 20254 vCPU, 8 GBnginx, the appthe app isn't a service: it was started by hand, as root, with nohup, from /root/stagedoor
door-3Ubuntu 22.04, April 20254 vCPU, 8 GBnginx, the appsame as door-2; standard support for 22.04 ends in April 2027

She notices what the cards mean together. If door-2 or door-3 rebooted (a kernel update, a host failure at Atlantica Hosting), nginx would start and the app wouldn't: fans would see errors until someone logged in and remembered the command. Everyone logs in as root with the same password, so nobody can tell who changed what. And door-1, which holds every ticket ever sold, is the oldest machine, on the release with the least support left. Rafaela doesn't fix anything yet. She writes the three problems at the top of the page, with the chapters of this book that fix them: the app as a service (Chapter 6), personal accounts and no shared root password (Chapter 4), and a plan for door-1 (Chapter 8).

1.9Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Running a release past its end of supportNew vulnerabilities are never fixed on that machine; nothing visibly breaks.Record the end-of-support date on day one; plan the move months ahead.
Choosing a non-LTS release for a serverSupport ends after nine months; you're forced into upgrades twice a year.Use an LTS (or the RHEL family's long releases) for servers.
Starting programs by hand on a serverThey don't come back after a reboot, and nobody knows how they were started.Run them as services (Chapter 6).
Installing kernel updates and never rebootingThe fixes are on disk but not running; months of exposure.Schedule reboots; check /var/run/reboot-required (Chapter 8).
Rebooting a server you don't understand to “see if it helps”Hand-started programs vanish; an outage becomes a mystery.Inventory first; reboot when you know what will start.
Changing things during the first lookYou can't tell your changes from what was there before.Read-only commands first, and write down what you find.

✓Chapter summary

  • The kernel is the only program that touches hardware; everything else asks it through system calls.
  • A distribution packages the kernel with a userland and a package manager, and supports each release for a stated number of years. Know the date for every server.
  • Boot goes firmware → bootloader → kernel → PID 1 (systemd) → services. Anything not started as a service won't come back after a reboot.
  • Cloud servers start from an image, and cloud-init turns it into your server at first boot from the user data you supply.
  • Inventory an unfamiliar server with read-only commands first; much of the answer comes from /proc and /sys.
  • Pets hold knowledge nobody wrote down. Move it into writing, then into scripts, then rebuild.

Key terms

Kernel
The program that runs the hardware and provides processes, memory, files, networking and permissions to everything else.
System call
A request from a program to the kernel, such as opening a file or starting a program.
Userland
Everything outside the kernel: init, shells, utilities, libraries and applications.
Distribution
A kernel, a userland and a package manager released and supported together, such as Ubuntu or Debian.
Release
A numbered version of a distribution whose packages receive fixes, not major changes, until its end of support.
LTS
Long-term support: a release meant to be run for years, with security updates for all of that time.
UEFI
The firmware on modern machines that checks hardware and starts the bootloader.
initramfs
A small temporary filesystem the kernel uses at boot to find and mount the real root filesystem.
systemd
The init system (PID 1) on most distributions; it starts and supervises services.
Image
A ready-made disk with an operating system installed, from which cloud VMs are launched.
cloud-init
The program on cloud images that configures a new instance at first boot from its user data.
Pet
A server built and repaired by hand, which can't easily be replaced; the opposite of cattle, built from a description.

Check your understanding

  1. Which of these is the kernel's job, and which is the distribution's: scheduling processes on CPUs, shipping a security fix for OpenSSL, deciding whether a program may open a file, providing ls? recall

    Show answer

    Kernel: scheduling processes and checking file permissions. Distribution: shipping the OpenSSL fix (as an updated package) and providing ls (part of the userland, from GNU coreutils). The distribution also ships the kernel itself as a package.

  2. uname -r prints 5.15.0-91-generic, and ls /boot shows vmlinuz-5.15.0-91-generic and vmlinuz-5.15.0-131-generic. What does that tell you, and what would you do? apply

    Show answer

    A newer kernel (131) is installed but the machine is still running the old one (91), so the kernel's security fixes since then aren't active. Plan a reboot, after checking that everything that should run is started as a service and will come back.

  3. Why does a program started by hand with nohup not come back after a reboot, while nginx does? explain

    Show answer

    After a reboot, the only programs that start are the ones PID 1 (systemd) is configured to start, plus their descendants. nginx is installed as a systemd service enabled at boot; the hand-started program was started by a person's shell, which no longer exists, and nothing records that it should run.

  4. A new VM launched from an Ubuntu image with your user data has no user rafaela. Where do you look first, and what might have gone wrong? debug

    Show answer

    Look at cloud-init's logs: /var/log/cloud-init-output.log and /var/log/cloud-init.log (and cloud-init status --long). Common causes: the file didn't start with #cloud-config, so it wasn't read as cloud-config; a YAML indentation error; or the user data was added to an instance that had already booted, and cloud-init only applies it at first boot.

  5. You've inherited a server nobody understands, and you've been asked to “tidy it up”. Write the first five things you'd do, in order. design

    Show answer

    One reasonable order: (1) record the release and its end of support, the kernel and uptime; (2) list running services and all running processes and find what was started outside systemd; (3) list listening ports and who logs in; (4) for each running program, find its config, data and logs; (5) write it all down, with the problems found, before changing anything. Only then start fixing, one written-down change at a time.

  6. Compare an Ubuntu LTS release with a RHEL major version as a choice for a server you expect to run for six years. compare

    Show answer

    Ubuntu LTS gives five years of standard security updates (more with Ubuntu Pro), so a six-year server either needs Pro or an upgrade along the way. A RHEL major version gets ten years, covering the whole period, at the cost of a subscription (or using a rebuild such as Rocky or AlmaLinux) and older package versions near the end. Either works; what matters is knowing the date and planning for it.

Going further

  • Ubuntu: The Ubuntu lifecycle and release cadence.
  • Debian: Debian releases and Debian Long Term Support.
  • The Linux kernel documentation: The Linux kernel user's and administrator's guide.
  • cloud-init: documentation, especially the cloud-config examples.
  • man 7 boot and man 2 intro (system calls) on any Linux machine.

Part I · Finding your way

2

The shell, properly

You can type commands; this chapter shows what happens to them before they run. Once you know how the shell splits, expands and connects what you type, pipelines stop being magic spells copied from the internet and become sentences you can read, write and trust with a real question.

By the end you can

  • Describe what the shell does with a line before any program runs.
  • Redirect standard output and standard error, and connect commands with pipes.
  • Answer a question from a log file with a pipeline of small tools, and check the answer.
  • Quote correctly, and predict what *, $var and $( … ) turn into.
  • Use exit codes to tell success from failure, including inside a pipeline.

Before you start

  • A terminal on any Linux (Appendix C). Chapter 1 helps but isn't required.
  • Python 3, only to generate the lab's sample log.

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, any Linux terminal, free
Shell
bash, the default on Ubuntu, Debian, RHEL and Amazon Linux

At Stagedoor

Tiago Neves starts at Stagedoor on Monday 2 February 2026, his first job after university. On Wednesday, Joana Matos, the CEO, stops by his desk. Sala Ribeira, a venue in Porto, says some fans got an error at checkout when Os Lumes went on sale last Friday at 10:00. How many?

Tiago has seen this done in a chat thread. He logs in to door-2, finds nginx's access log for that morning and types:

grep 500 access.log | wc -l

It prints 381. Joana goes pale: that's a lot of fans to apologise to. Rafaela, reading over his shoulder, asks one question: “What did 500 match?” Section 2.9 answers it.

2.1What the shell does with a line

The shell is an ordinary program (on servers, usually bash) whose job is to read a line of text, turn it into one or more programs with arguments, run them, and wait. Most surprises come from not knowing that the shell rewrites the line before anything runs.

Two consequences follow. First, a program can't tell whether you typed *.log or the list of names the shell turned it into. Second, when a command does something odd, the question is usually “what arguments did it actually get?”, and echo is the quickest way to find out: put echo in front of the command, and the shell prints the expanded line instead of running it.

bash, sh, dashYour login shell on a server is usually bash. /bin/sh is a simpler shell for scripts: on Ubuntu and Debian it's dash, which lacks some bash features. A script that starts with #!/bin/sh but uses bash syntax works on one distribution and fails on another.

2.2Commands, options and arguments

After splitting, the first word is the command and the rest are its arguments. By convention, arguments starting with - are options: short ones are a single letter and can be combined (ls -l -a is ls -la); long ones start with -- (ls --all). Some options take a value: head -n 5, sort --key=2. A lone -- means “no more options”, which matters when a file name starts with a dash: rm -- -oops.txt.

The command itself is one of three things, and type tells you which:

  • A builtin, part of the shell itself: cd, echo, export, type. It has to be: cd changes the shell's own current directory, which a separate program couldn't do.
  • An alias or function defined in your shell's startup files, such as Ubuntu's ll for ls -alF.
  • A program: a file somewhere on disk. The shell looks for it in each directory listed in the PATH variable, in order, and runs the first match.
type cd ll grep         # cd is a shell builtin · ll is aliased to `ls -alF' · grep is /usr/bin/grep
echo "$PATH"            # /usr/local/sbin:/usr/local/bin:/usr/sbin:/usr/bin:/sbin:/bin…

Watch out: PATH is not the same everywhere

Your interactive shell's PATH is set by its startup files. Programs started by cron or as services get a much shorter one. A command that works when you type it can fail with “command not found” at 03:00. Stagedoor meets exactly this in Chapter 6; the fix is to use full paths, or set PATH explicitly, in anything that runs unattended.

2.3Standard streams and redirection

Every program starts with three open channels, called standard streams, each identified by a small number, its file descriptor:

  • 0, standard input (stdin): where it reads input from. By default, your keyboard.
  • 1, standard output (stdout): where it writes its results. By default, your terminal.
  • 2, standard error (stderr): where it writes errors and progress messages. Also your terminal, which is why the two look the same until you separate them.

The split between 1 and 2 is what makes pipelines work: results flow on to the next program, while errors still reach you. Redirection tells the shell to connect a stream to a file instead:

Table 2.1 · Redirection, the forms you'll use every day
You writeIt means
cmd > out.txtstdout to out.txt, replacing the file if it exists
cmd >> out.txtstdout appended to out.txt
cmd 2> err.txtstderr to err.txt; stdout still to the terminal
cmd > all.txt 2>&1stdout to all.txt, then stderr to wherever stdout now goes. Order matters: 2>&1 > all.txt sends stderr to the terminal
cmd 2> /dev/nullthrow stderr away. /dev/null is a device that discards whatever is written to it
cmd < in.txtstdin from in.txt
cmd1 | cmd2stdout of cmd1 becomes stdin of cmd2
cmd | tee out.txtshow the output and save a copy

One more form is common in documentation: sudo cmd > /etc/file fails with “Permission denied” even though cmd runs as root. The redirection is done by your shell, as you, before sudo even starts. Write cmd | sudo tee /etc/file > /dev/null instead.

2.4Pipes and the small tools

A pipe connects one program's standard output to the next one's standard input. All the programs in a pipeline run at the same time; each reads what the previous one writes, as it's written. That's why grep error huge.log | head -5 returns at once: when head has its five lines it exits, and grep is stopped the next time it writes.

The Unix habit is to combine small programs that each do one thing. A handful cover most questions you'll ask of a server:

Table 2.2 · Small tools for text
ToolDoesTypical use
grepkeeps lines matching a patterngrep -i error app.log, grep -v healthcheck (lines not matching), grep -c (count)
awksplits each line into fields $1, $2…, filters and printsawk '$9 >= 500 {print $7}'
sortsorts linessort -n (numbers), -r (reverse), -u (unique)
uniq -ccounts runs of identical lines; needs sorted inputsort | uniq -c | sort -rn: a frequency table
wc -lcounts lineshow many matched?
head, tailfirst or last linestail -f app.log follows a growing file
cutpicks fields by delimitercut -d: -f1 /etc/passwd
lesspages through text/ to search, q to quit

Build pipelines one step at a time, and look at the output after each step (add | head while you work). A pipeline you built and checked step by step is one you can trust; a pipeline you pasted is a guess with a confident number at the end.

Key idea

Before you trust a count, look at what you counted. Replace | wc -l with | head and read the lines.

2.5Expansion and quoting

Step 3 of Figure 2.1 is where most shell bugs live. Before running anything, bash performs several expansions on each word:

  • Tilde: ~ becomes your home directory.
  • Variables: $name or ${name} becomes the variable's value. $? (last exit status), $$ (the shell's process ID) and $1 (a script's first argument) are special variables.
  • Command substitution: $(cmd) becomes cmd's output, as in Lab 1's server card.
  • Arithmetic: $((2 * 60)) becomes 120.
  • Word splitting: the result of an unquoted expansion is split again at spaces, tabs and newlines.
  • Globbing (pathname expansion): an unquoted word containing *, ? or […] becomes the list of matching file names. If nothing matches, bash leaves the word as it is.

Quoting switches expansions off:

  • Single quotes '…' switch off everything. What you write is what the program gets.
  • Double quotes "…" allow variables, $( ) and arithmetic, but switch off word splitting and globbing. The result stays one argument, spaces and all.
  • A backslash switches off the special meaning of the next character.

In a directory containing only access.log, with event=os-lumes:

echo *.log; echo "*.log"; echo $event "$event" '$event'
access.log
*.log
os-lumes os-lumes $event

The dangerous case is a variable holding a space. Unquoted, it becomes two arguments:

f="on-sale report.txt"; touch "$f"; ls $f; ls "$f"
ls: cannot access 'on-sale': No such file or directory
ls: cannot access 'report.txt': No such file or directory
on-sale report.txt

The unquoted ls $f looked for two files, on-sale and report.txt. With ls that's an error message. With rm it deletes the wrong files, and with rm -rf $dir/ and an empty $dir, it's the classic way to delete far more than intended.

Key idea

Put double quotes around every variable and every $( ), unless you can say why not. Use single quotes for text that must reach the program untouched, such as awk and grep patterns.

2.6Exit codes

Every program finishes with an exit status, a number from 0 to 255. 0 means success; anything else means some kind of failure, and the program's manual page says which. The shell keeps the last one in $?. grep is a good example, because it distinguishes “no match” from “something went wrong”:

grep -q '" 504 ' access.log; echo $?     # a line with status 504 exists
grep -q '" 503 ' access.log; echo $?     # none with 503
grep -q 504 nosuch.log; echo $?          # the file doesn't exist
0
1
grep: nosuch.log: No such file or directory
2

A few codes are set by the shell itself: 126 means the file exists but can't be executed (Lab 1), 127 means command not found, and 128 + n means the program was killed by signal n (so 137 is 128 + 9, SIGKILL, which you'll meet in Chapter 5 and Chapter 10).

Exit codes drive the shell's logic. a && b runs b only if a succeeded; a || b runs b only if a failed; a; b runs both regardless. if tests an exit status, not a true/false value: if grep -q ERROR app.log; then ….

A pipeline's exit status is, by default, the status of its last command. That hides failures:

grep 500 nosuch.log | wc -l; echo "status: $?"
set -o pipefail
grep 500 nosuch.log | wc -l; echo "status: $?"
grep: nosuch.log: No such file or directory
0
status: 0
grep: nosuch.log: No such file or directory
0
status: 2

Without pipefail, the pipeline “succeeded” and printed a count of 0, because wc happily counted nothing. In a script, that zero would be reported as a fact. With set -o pipefail, the pipeline fails if any part fails. Chapter 17 makes this and two other settings the start of every script.

2.7Getting help and moving fast

Nobody remembers every option. What you need is to know where the answers are:

  • man grep opens the manual page. Pages are grouped in numbered sections: 1 for commands, 5 for file formats (man 5 crontab), 8 for administration commands. Search inside with /; quit with q. man -k word searches page titles.
  • grep --help prints a shorter summary for most programs; help cd does the same for bash builtins.
  • Examples are often the fastest help: the EXAMPLES section near the end of many man pages, or a community tool such as tldr.
Minimal imagesCloud and container images often leave out manual pages to save space. On Ubuntu, sudo unminimize puts them back; or read them on your own machine, or at manpages.ubuntu.com.

And a few keys save hours: Tab completes commands and file names (press it twice to see choices); Ctrl+R searches your command history; Ctrl+C interrupts the running program; Ctrl+D ends input (and logs you out of an empty prompt); Ctrl+A and Ctrl+E jump to the start and end of the line. history lists what you typed, which, on a shared server, is also a way to learn what the last person did.

Watch out: secrets in your history

Anything you type is saved to ~/.bash_history when the shell exits, including a password passed as an argument (mysql -p'secret', curl -u user:secret). Prefer tools that prompt for secrets or read them from a file or environment variable. Arguments are also visible to other users in ps while the program runs.

2.8Across distributions

bash and the core tools behave the same on every distribution in this book: Ubuntu, Debian, the RHEL family and Amazon Linux all ship GNU bash, GNU coreutils, GNU grep and an awk. The differences you'll notice are small:

On the RHEL family and Amazon Linux

/bin/sh is bash itself (running in a compatibility mode), not dash. Scripts that start with #!/bin/sh but use bash features work there and break on Ubuntu and Debian, so write #!/usr/bin/env bash when you use bash features. awk is GNU awk (gawk) here, while Ubuntu and Debian install the smaller mawk by default; both handle everything in this chapter.

On your Mac

macOS uses zsh as the default shell and BSD versions of the core tools, whose options sometimes differ (sed -i, date, base64 -D). When a command from this book behaves strangely on a Mac, run it on the server or in a Linux container instead.

2.9Lab: how many fans got an error?

Lab 2 · Answer Joana's question, and check the answer

You'll generate a sample of the access log from Stagedoor's on-sale, reproduce Tiago's wrong count, find out why it's wrong, and build the right answer one pipe at a time. You need a Linux terminal and Python 3. Cost: nothing. Clean-up: delete the lab directory.

  1. Make the log. Create a directory, save this script in it as makelog.py, and run it. It writes 2,000 lines in nginx's default (“combined”) format; its random choices are seeded, so you get exactly the same file as everyone else.

    makelog.pyPython 3.8+

    # makelog.py · a sample nginx access log for the 10:00 on-sale on Friday 30 January 2026
import random

rng = random.Random(2026)
NETS = ["198.51.100", "203.0.113", "192.0.2"]
PAGES = [("GET", "/events/os-lumes-porto"), ("GET", "/static/app.js"),
         ("GET", "/api/seats?event=4500"), ("POST", "/api/hold"), ("POST", "/api/checkout")]

for i in range(2000):
    t = 9 * 3600 + 58 * 60 + int(i * 0.36)          # 09:58:00 onwards, about 3 requests a second
    stamp = f"30/Jan/2026:{t // 3600:02d}:{t // 60 % 60:02d}:{t % 60:02d} +0000"
    ip = f"{rng.choice(NETS)}.{rng.randint(1, 254)}"
    method, path = rng.choices(PAGES, weights=[30, 25, 20, 15, 10])[0]
    status = 200
    if path == "/static/app.js" and rng.random() < 0.5:
        status = 304
    elif path == "/api/hold" and rng.random() < 0.2:
        status = 409                                   # someone else got the seat first
    elif path == "/api/checkout" and "10:01" <= stamp[12:17] <= "10:04" and rng.random() < 0.3:
        status = rng.choice([500, 502, 504])
    size = 0 if status == 304 else rng.randint(150, 9000)
    agent = rng.choice(["Mozilla/5.0 (iPhone)", "Mozilla/5.0 (Android)", "Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0)"])
    print(f'{ip} - - [{stamp}] "{method} {path} HTTP/1.1" {status} {size} "-" "{agent}"')
    mkdir -p ~/lab2 && cd ~/lab2       # save makelog.py here first
python3 makelog.py > access.log
wc -l access.log
head -3 access.log
    2000 access.log
198.51.100.82 - - [30/Jan/2026:09:58:00 +0000] "GET /static/app.js HTTP/1.1" 200 1831 "-" "Mozilla/5.0 (iPhone)"
192.0.2.160 - - [30/Jan/2026:09:58:00 +0000] "GET /api/seats?event=4500 HTTP/1.1" 200 8192 "-" "Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0)"
203.0.113.62 - - [30/Jan/2026:09:58:00 +0000] "GET /events/os-lumes-porto HTTP/1.1" 200 1472 "-" "Mozilla/5.0 (iPhone)"
    Read one line field by field, as awk will see it (fields are separated by spaces): $1 is the client's address, $4 the time (with a leading [), $6 the method (with a leading "), $7 the path, $9 the status code, $10 the response size in bytes.
  2. Reproduce Tiago's count, then look at what it counted: 
    grep 500 access.log | wc -l
grep 500 access.log | head -4
    381
192.0.2.160 - - [30/Jan/2026:09:58:00 +0000] "GET /api/seats?event=4500 HTTP/1.1" 200 8192 "-" "Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0)"
203.0.113.23 - - [30/Jan/2026:09:58:02 +0000] "GET /api/seats?event=4500 HTTP/1.1" 200 4936 "-" "Mozilla/5.0 (Android)"
192.0.2.117 - - [30/Jan/2026:09:58:05 +0000] "GET /api/seats?event=4500 HTTP/1.1" 200 4262 "-" "Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0)"
192.0.2.150 - - [30/Jan/2026:09:58:10 +0000] "GET /api/seats?event=4500 HTTP/1.1" 200 8210 "-" "Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0)"
    grep 500 matches any line containing those three characters. Every request for the seat map has event=4500 in its path, and two responses happen to be 8,500 and 5,009 bytes long. None of the first four matches is an error.
  3. Count the status field instead. A frequency table of field 9: 
    awk '{print $9}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn
       1662 200
    253 304
     58 409
     11 502
      9 500
      7 504
    Now the picture is clear: 27 server errors in total (500, 502, 504). The 409s are fans who lost a race for a seat; the app answered correctly, so they aren't errors for this question.
  4. Narrow it to checkout, and to people rather than requests. A fan who retried counts once: 
    awk '$7 == "/api/checkout" && $9 >= 500' access.log | wc -l
awk '$7 == "/api/checkout" && $9 >= 500 {print $1}' access.log | sort -u | wc -l
    27
26
    Twenty-seven failed checkouts, from 26 addresses. Check the difference rather than trusting it: 
    awk '$7 == "/api/checkout" && $9 >= 500 {print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -3
          2 198.51.100.138
      1 203.0.113.59
      1 203.0.113.48
    One address failed twice. (An address isn't quite a person: several fans behind one mobile network can share one, and one fan can switch from Wi-Fi to 4G. For an apology email, Stagedoor would use the order attempts in its database instead. The log gives the right order of magnitude, quickly.)
  5. When did it happen? Characters 14–18 of field 4 are the hour and minute: 
    awk '$9 >= 500 {print substr($4, 14, 5)}' access.log | sort | uniq -c
          4 10:01
     11 10:02
      7 10:03
      5 10:04
    All errors fell between 10:01 and 10:04, the first minutes of the on-sale.
  6. Break it on purpose: quoting. Count checkout requests, first without quotes: 
    grep POST /api/checkout access.log | head -2
grep "POST /api/checkout" access.log | wc -l
    grep: /api/checkout: No such file or directory
access.log:203.0.113.37 - - [30/Jan/2026:09:58:02 +0000] "POST /api/hold HTTP/1.1" 200 5212 "-" "Mozilla/5.0 (iPhone)"
204
    Unquoted, the shell split the pattern into two words: grep searched for POST in two files, /api/checkout (which doesn't exist, hence the error on stderr) and access.log (and, with two files, prefixed each line with its file name). Piped into wc -l, the error would scroll past and you'd get 497, every POST, holds included. Quoted, it's one argument and the right answer: 204 checkout requests.
  7. Break it on purpose: exit codes. Run the pipefail example from 2.6 in your lab directory and confirm the two different statuses.

Stretch goals. (1) Print the top five paths by number of requests. (2) Count 409s per minute: does losing a seat race peak at the same time as the server errors? (3) Use awk to sum field 10 and report how many megabytes the server sent in these twelve minutes.

At Stagedoor: the real answer

The 381 lines were mostly fans looking at the seat map for event 4500. Tiago rebuilds the answer the way the lab does, one pipe at a time, and checks each step with head. On door-2: 27 failed checkout requests, from 26 fans, all between 10:01 and 10:04; door-3's log shows much the same. Joana sends Sala Ribeira the numbers within the hour, and Sasha's team asks for the four minutes of app logs to find out why checkout failed.

Rafaela adds one line to the team wiki, under “Answering questions from logs”: before you send a number, show the lines you counted. Tiago pins it.

2.10Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
grep for a number anywhere on the lineMatches sizes, paths and times; the count is meaningless.Match the field: awk '$9 == 500'.
Counting without lookingA confident wrong number.Swap | wc -l for | head and read the lines first.
Unquoted variablesValues with spaces become several arguments; empty ones vanish."$var", always.
Double quotes around an awk programThe shell expands $9 (usually to nothing) before awk sees it.Single quotes around awk and grep patterns.
uniq -c on unsorted inputEqual lines that aren't adjacent are counted separately.sort | uniq -c.
Trusting a pipeline's exit statusA failed first step is hidden by a successful last one.set -o pipefail in scripts (Chapter 17).
sudo cmd > /etc/file“Permission denied”: the redirection runs as you.cmd | sudo tee /etc/file.

✓Chapter summary

  • The shell reads a line, splits it, expands it, finds each program and runs it. Programs see only the final arguments.
  • Programs have three standard streams: input (0), output (1) and error (2). Redirection connects them to files; pipes connect one program's output to the next one's input.
  • Small tools (grep, awk, sort, uniq, wc, head) answer most questions about a server, built one step at a time.
  • Quote every variable with double quotes; use single quotes for patterns and programs.
  • Exit status 0 means success. A pipeline returns its last command's status unless pipefail is set.
  • Look at what you counted before you trust the count.

Key terms

Shell
The program that reads commands, expands them and runs programs; usually bash on servers.
PATH
The list of directories the shell searches, in order, to find a program by name.
Standard streams
The three channels every program starts with: standard input (0), standard output (1) and standard error (2).
File descriptor
A small number a process uses to refer to an open file, pipe or socket.
Redirection
Connecting a program's standard stream to a file instead of the terminal, with >, >>, 2> or <.
Pipe
A connection from one program's standard output to another's standard input, written |.
Expansion
The shell's rewriting of a word before running a command: variables, command substitution, arithmetic, word splitting and globbing.
Globbing
Turning a word containing *, ? or […] into the list of matching file names.
Quoting
Switching off expansions: single quotes switch off all of them, double quotes all but variables and command substitution.
Exit status
The number a program returns when it finishes; 0 means success.

Check your understanding

  1. Which file descriptor does a program write error messages to, and why does it matter for pipelines? recall

    Show answer

    Standard error, file descriptor 2. Pipes only carry standard output (1), so error messages reach the terminal instead of being mixed into the data the next program processes.

  2. Write a pipeline that lists the five client addresses with the most requests in access.log, most first. apply

    Show answer

    awk '{print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -5. The first sort groups equal addresses so uniq -c can count them; the second sorts by count, numerically and in reverse.

  3. dir= is accidentally empty. What does rm -rf $dir/* do, and how would quoting and a check have helped? explain

    Show answer

    The shell expands it to rm -rf /*: every top-level directory on the machine. Quoting alone ("$dir"/*) gives the same result with an empty variable; what helps is refusing to run with an empty value: rm -rf "${dir:?}"/* stops with an error if dir is empty or unset, and set -u (Chapter 17) catches unset variables in scripts.

  4. A script runs grep ERROR /var/log/app.log | wc -l and reports 0 errors every night. The log file was renamed a month ago. Why didn't anyone notice, and what's the fix? debug

    Show answer

    grep failed (exit 2, “No such file or directory” on stderr, which nobody reads in a cron job), but the pipeline's status was wc's, which succeeded and counted zero lines. Fix: set -o pipefail (or check that the file exists) so the failure is reported, and send the script's stderr somewhere someone reads.

  5. Joana now asks for the number of fans who lost a seat race (status 409) and later completed a checkout. Sketch how you'd answer it from the log, and say what the log can't tell you. design

    Show answer

    Collect the addresses with a 409 on /api/hold (awk '$9 == 409 {print $1}' | sort -u > lost.txt), the addresses with a 200 on /api/checkout (… | sort -u > bought.txt), and count the lines in both (comm -12 lost.txt bought.txt | wc -l). The log can't tell you about people: addresses are shared and change, and it doesn't know which order belongs to whom. The order database can.

  6. Compare single and double quotes: what does each switch off, and when do you use each? compare

    Show answer

    Single quotes switch off every expansion; use them for text that must reach a program unchanged, such as awk programs and grep patterns containing $. Double quotes keep variable and command substitution but switch off word splitting and globbing; use them around variables and $( ) so the value stays one argument.

Going further

  • The GNU Bash Reference Manual, especially “Shell Expansions” and “Redirections”.
  • Greg's Bash Guide and its list of Bash pitfalls.
  • The GNU Awk User's Guide; the first chapter is enough for most log work.
  • man bash, man grep, man 7 pipe.

Part I · Finding your way

3

Files and the filesystem layout

On a Linux server, nearly everything you'll configure, read or fix is a file in one big tree, and the tree has conventions: configuration here, data there, logs somewhere else. This chapter teaches the map, what a file really is underneath its name, and how links let you switch a whole application to a new version in one step.

By the end you can

  • Say where configuration, programs, data, logs and temporary files belong, and why.
  • Read every column of ls -l, and explain what an inode is.
  • Choose between a hard link and a symbolic link, and switch a release atomically with a symlink.
  • Find files by name, type, size and age with find.
  • Design a filesystem layout for your own application.

Before you start

  • Chapter 2: commands, quoting, pipes.
  • A Linux terminal as a normal (non-root) user (Appendix C).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, any Linux terminal, free
Main distribution
Ubuntu, with other distributions in 3.8

At Stagedoor

In the week of 9 February 2026, Rafaela and Tiago set out to answer a simple question about door-2: where does the app live? nginx's configuration points at /srv/app. That turns out to be a link to /root/stagedoor, a Git checkout in the root user's home directory. In it: the code, a .env file with the database password, a venv directory, and nohup.out, 6.1 GB of everything the app has printed since it was last started. Deploying means logging in as root, running git pull in that directory, and restarting the app by hand.

Tiago asks the obvious question: what happens to a request that arrives while git pull is halfway through replacing files? Nobody knows. Section 3.7 is the answer they agree on.

3.1One tree

Linux has no drive letters. Every file on the machine sits somewhere in one tree that starts at /, the root directory. Other disks, network shares and special filesystems are attached to the tree at a directory, called a mount point (Chapter 11), and from then on look like ordinary directories.

Where things go in that tree isn't arbitrary. The Filesystem Hierarchy Standard (FHS) describes the layout every mainstream distribution follows, and once you know it you can find your way around any server, including one somebody else built.

3.2What goes where

Table 3.1 · The directories you'll use, and what they hold on a server
DirectoryHoldsOn a server like Stagedoor's
/etcsystem-wide configuration, as text/etc/nginx/, /etc/ssh/sshd_config, /etc/passwd
/usrprograms and libraries from packages/usr/sbin/nginx, /usr/bin/python3
/usr/localsoftware installed by hand, outside the package managera tool compiled from source
/optself-contained application bundlesa vendor's agent; your own app's releases
/var/libstate that programs keep/var/lib/postgresql/ (the database's files)
/var/loglogs/var/log/nginx/access.log (Chapter 7)
/var/cachedata that can be regenerateddownloaded packages
/srvdata the server publishesa static website; often unused
/home, /rootusers' own files; /root is the root user's homepersonal notes and keys, not running services
/tmptemporary files, for anyoneoften emptied at boot; never for anything you need
/runruntime state since boot: PID files, sockets/run/nginx.pid
/proc, /systhe kernel's view of processes and devices/proc/meminfo, /proc/<pid>/
/devdevices as files/dev/vda (a disk), /dev/null

The reason this matters is practical. A layout that follows the convention tells everyone, including tools, what each file is: backups include /etc and /var/lib; log rotation looks in /var/log; a security scanner expects /usr to change only when packages do. An app living in /root, with its secrets, data and logs in one directory, defeats all of them at once.

The /usr mergeUbuntu, Debian 12+, Fedora, RHEL and Amazon Linux have merged /bin, /sbin and /lib into /usr: the old names are symbolic links (3.5) to /usr/bin and so on. Both paths work.

3.3Everything is a file

“Everything is a file” is the oldest Unix slogan, and it's nearly true: the kernel presents many different things through the same interface of names in the tree that you can open, read and write. The first character of ls -l says which kind you're looking at:

Table 3.2 · File types
ls -lTypeExample
-regular file: bytes on a disk/etc/hostname, a program, a log
ddirectory: a list of names/etc
lsymbolic link: a name pointing to another path/bin → usr/bin
c, bcharacter and block devices: hardware, or kernel features, as files/dev/null, /dev/vda
ssocket: a connection point between programs/run/postgresql/.s.PGSQL.5432
pnamed pipe: a pipe (Chapter 2) with a namerare on servers

The files under /proc and /sys are a special case: they're regular files whose contents the kernel generates at the moment you read them. That's why ls -l /proc/loadavg shows a size of 0, and file calls it “empty”, while cat /proc/loadavg prints the current load. They're the kernel's dashboard, and many tools you'll meet (ps, free, top) are mostly readers of /proc.

3.4Inodes, names and ls -l

A file's name is not the file. On Linux filesystems, a file is an inode: a numbered record that holds everything about the file (its type, owner, permissions, size, timestamps and where its data blocks are on the disk) except its name. A directory is just a table that maps names to inode numbers. ls -i shows the inode number next to each name.

With that picture, every column of ls -l makes sense:

599380 -rw-r--r-- 2 tiago tiago 13 Nov 27 08:02 a.txt
│      │          │ │     │     │  │            └ the name, from the directory
│      │          │ │     │     │  └ modification time
│      │          │ │     │     └ size in bytes
│      │          │ │     └ group
│      │          │ └ owner
│      │          └ link count: how many names this inode has
│      └ type, then permissions (Chapter 4)
└ inode number (shown with ls -i)

stat shows the rest of the inode, including three timestamps: modify (the contents changed), change (the inode changed: permissions, owner, link count) and access (last read, often updated lazily to save writes). Note what's missing: there's no “creation time” you can rely on across filesystems, and none of the timestamps records who made the change. Accountability comes from logs (Chapter 7, Chapter 18), not from the filesystem.

3.5Hard links and symbolic links

Because names and inodes are separate, one file can have several names. There are two ways to do it, and they behave very differently:

Table 3.3 · Hard links and symbolic links compared
Hard link (ln target name)Symbolic link (ln -s target name)
What it isanother directory entry for the same inodea separate tiny file whose contents are a path
Delete the original namethe file lives on under the other namethe link now points nowhere (“dangling”)
Across filesystemsno: inode numbers are per filesystemyes: it's only a path
To a directorynot allowedallowed, and common
You can tell it's a linkonly from the link countls -l shows name -> target
Typical usebackup tools, package managers, saving space“current version”, compatibility names, config enabled from a library of files

Symbolic links (symlinks) are the ones you'll create on purpose. A relative target (releases/2026.02.1) is resolved from the directory the link is in, not from where you are; an absolute one (/opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.02.1) from /. Prefer relative targets inside one application's directory, so the whole tree can be moved or copied.

Switching versions with one rename

Symlinks make a clean deployment pattern possible. Install each version of an application into its own directory, never touching it again, and point a symlink called current at the live one. Everything else (the service, nginx, cron jobs) refers only to …/current. Deploying is: unpack the new version next to the old one, then point current at it. Rolling back is pointing it back.

The switch must be atomic: at every instant, current must point to one complete version, never to nothing and never to half of each. The kernel guarantees exactly that for one operation: renaming a name over an existing one, within one filesystem, replaces it in a single step. So the safe way is to create the new link under a temporary name and rename it over the old one:

ln -s releases/2026.02.2 current.new
mv -T current.new current            # -T: treat "current" as the name to replace, not a directory to move into

You'll often see ln -sfn releases/2026.02.2 current instead. Whether that's atomic depends on the version: the one on Ubuntu 24.04 creates a temporary link and renames it, as the lab shows with strace, while older versions removed the old link first and created the new one after, leaving a moment with no current at all. mv -T is atomic everywhere, so deploy scripts should use it.

Key idea

Never change a running application's files in place. Put the new version beside the old one, and switch with a single rename.

Running programs that already loaded the old code keep using it until they restart; that's a feature, and Chapter 5 shows how to restart them without dropping requests.

3.6Finding things

find walks a tree and tests every entry. Its arguments read as a sentence: where to start, then tests, then optionally an action (the default is to print the path).

find /etc -name '*.conf'                    # by name; quote the pattern so the shell doesn't expand it
find /var/log -type f -size +100M           # regular files over 100 MB
find /var/log -type f -mtime +30            # modified more than 30 days ago
find . -newer deploy.marker                 # changed since a reference file was touched
find / -xdev -type d -perm -0002            # world-writable directories on the root filesystem (Chapter 4)
find /tmp -user tiago -type f -delete       # an action: delete what matched. Run without -delete first!

Searching from / touches /proc and network mounts unless you add -xdev (stay on one filesystem), and prints “Permission denied” for directories you can't read unless you add 2>/dev/null or use sudo. For the other common questions: type cmd or command -v cmd finds a program on PATH; grep -rl 'pattern' /etc/nginx finds files containing a text; dpkg -S /usr/sbin/nginx says which package installed a file (Chapter 8); and readlink -f path follows every symlink to the real file.

Watch out: deleted is not freed

Deleting a file removes a name. If a running program still has the file open, the inode and its data stay on the disk until the program closes it or exits, even though no name points to it any more. Deleting a huge log that nginx or your app is still writing frees nothing, and the disk stays full. The lab shows how to see such files; Chapter 7 and Chapter 11 show how to free the space.

3.7A layout for your own application

Put the convention to work. For an application you deploy yourself (not from a distribution package), a layout that follows the FHS looks like this:

Table 3.4 · Stagedoor's app layout, agreed in February 2026
WhatWhereWhy there
Each release's code and its Python environment/opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.02.1/self-contained, never modified after it's unpacked
The live release/opt/stagedoor/current → releases/…one atomic rename to deploy or roll back
Configuration and secrets/etc/stagedoor/stagedoor.envoutside the code, so a release can't overwrite it; readable only by the app (Chapter 4)
Data the app keeps/var/lib/stagedoor/ (uploads in …/uploads)survives every deploy; included in backups
Logs/var/log/stagedoor/where log rotation and log shipping look (Chapter 7)
Runtime files/run/stagedoor/sockets and PID files; cleared at boot

Notice what this buys. A deploy can't destroy uploads or overwrite configuration, because they aren't in the release directory. Old releases stay on disk for instant rollback (keep the last few; delete older ones). Backups need only /etc/stagedoor and /var/lib/stagedoor. And nothing lives in a person's home directory, so nobody's account is load-bearing.

At Stagedoor: the new layout

Rafaela and Tiago write Table 3.4 into the wiki and try it on a copy of door-2's app first. The git pull question answers itself: a request arriving mid-deploy is served entirely by the old release or entirely by the new one, never by a mixture. Leonor likes the rollback most: “so going back is one command, not a git revert at 10:02 on a Friday?”

Two things have to happen before the app can move out of /root: it needs its own user, so the files can belong to something other than root (Chapter 4), and it needs to be a service, so something other than a person's terminal starts it (Chapter 6). The 6.1 GB nohup.out stays for now, with a note: still open by the app; deleting it frees nothing.

3.8Across distributions

The FHS is shared, so the big directories are the same everywhere. The differences are in where particular programs put their files:

Table 3.5 · Same idea, different paths
Ubuntu, DebianRHEL family, Amazon Linux
Defaults for a service's options/etc/default/<name>/etc/sysconfig/<name>
nginx sites/etc/nginx/sites-available/, enabled by a symlink in sites-enabled//etc/nginx/conf.d/*.conf
nginx's default web root/var/www/html/usr/share/nginx/html
PostgreSQL's data/var/lib/postgresql/<version>/main/var/lib/pgsql/data
System log file/var/log/syslog/var/log/messages

On the RHEL family

SELinux labels every file with a security context, and a file in an unexpected place can be refused even when its permissions are right. Moving an app to /opt or /srv on RHEL may need semanage fcontext and restorecon so nginx may read it; ls -Z shows the labels. Chapter 18 explains why this extra layer is worth having.

3.9Lab: names, inodes and a release switch

Lab 3 · Take a file apart, then deploy with a symlink

You'll look at real system files, watch a hard link outlive its original name and a symlink dangle, build Table 3.4's release layout in your home directory and switch it atomically, and catch a deleted file that still uses disk space. You need a Linux terminal as a normal user, Python 3, and optionally strace (sudo apt install strace). Cost: nothing. Clean-up: rm -r ~/lab3. The outputs below came from a user called tiago; yours will show your name, other inode numbers and times.

  1. Look around, read-only. 
    mkdir -p ~/lab3 && cd ~/lab3
ls -ld /etc /var/log /tmp /proc
file /usr/bin/ls /etc/hostname /dev/null /proc/loadavg
    drwxr-xr-x 82 root root 4096 Nov 27 08:01 /etc
dr-xr-xr-x 94 root root    0 Nov 25 01:08 /proc
drwxrwxrwt  6 root root 4096 Nov 27 08:01 /tmp
drwxr-xr-x  7 root root 4096 Nov 21 05:14 /var/log
/usr/bin/ls:   ELF 64-bit LSB pie executable, x86-64, version 1 (SYSV), dynamically linked, interpreter /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2, BuildID[sha1]=44900829b39271d878615772bb2a6ffa422f0891, for GNU/Linux 3.2.0, stripped
/etc/hostname: ASCII text
/dev/null:     character special (1/3)
/proc/loadavg: empty
    /proc has size 0 and an odd link count because nothing in it is on a disk; /proc/loadavg is “empty” until you read it (try cat /proc/loadavg). /tmp ends in t, which Chapter 4 explains. /dev/null is a character device with device numbers 1 and 3.
  2. A hard link. Make a file, give it a second name, and compare: 
    echo "door-2 notes" > a.txt
ln a.txt b.txt
ls -li a.txt b.txt
    599380 -rw-r--r-- 2 tiago tiago 13 Nov 27 08:02 a.txt
599380 -rw-r--r-- 2 tiago tiago 13 Nov 27 08:02 b.txt
    Same inode number, link count 2: one file, two names. Write through one name, read through the other, then delete the original name: 
    echo "app runs as root" >> b.txt
cat a.txt
rm a.txt
ls -li b.txt
    door-2 notes
app runs as root
599380 -rw-r--r-- 1 tiago tiago 30 Nov 27 08:02 b.txt
    The text written through b.txt was there in a.txt, and removing a.txt only dropped the link count to 1. The data is untouched.
  3. A symbolic link, and how it breaks. 
    ln -s b.txt c.txt
ls -l c.txt
cat c.txt
mv b.txt notes.txt
cat c.txt; echo "exit status: $?"
    lrwxrwxrwx 1 tiago tiago 5 Nov 27 08:02 c.txt -> b.txt
door-2 notes
app runs as root
cat: c.txt: No such file or directory
exit status: 1
    The symlink stores the name b.txt. Renaming the target left it pointing at nothing. (A hard link would have followed, because it refers to the inode, not the name.)
  4. Build the release layout. Two releases and a current link, as in Table 3.4: 
    mkdir -p opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.02.1 opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.02.2
echo 2026.02.1 > opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.02.1/VERSION
echo 2026.02.2 > opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.02.2/VERSION
ln -s releases/2026.02.1 opt/stagedoor/current
ls -l opt/stagedoor
cat opt/stagedoor/current/VERSION
    total 4
lrwxrwxrwx 1 tiago tiago   18 Nov 27 08:02 current -> releases/2026.02.1
drwxr-xr-x 4 tiago tiago 4096 Nov 27 08:02 releases
2026.02.1
  5. Deploy atomically: create the new link under another name, then rename it over the old one. 
    cd opt/stagedoor
ln -s releases/2026.02.2 current.new
mv -T current.new current
readlink current
cat current/VERSION
    releases/2026.02.2
2026.02.2
    Rolling back is the same two commands with the old version.
  6. Optional: watch what ln -sfn does. strace prints the system calls a program makes (Chapter 1). Roll back with ln -sfn and watch: 
    strace -e trace=symlinkat,unlinkat,renameat,renameat2 ln -sfn releases/2026.02.1 current
readlink current
    symlinkat("releases/2026.02.1", AT_FDCWD, "current") = -1 EEXIST (File exists)
symlinkat("releases/2026.02.1", AT_FDCWD, "Cuojojq4") = 0
renameat(AT_FDCWD, "Cuojojq4", AT_FDCWD, "current") = 0
+++ exited with 0 +++
releases/2026.02.1
    This version tried to create current, found it existed, made the link under a random temporary name, and renamed it over current: atomic. On an older system (Ubuntu 22.04, for example) run the same command and look for an unlinkat of current before the symlinkat: that gap is the moment with no link.
  7. Find things. Every symlink under the lab, and every VERSION file newer than notes.txt: 
    cd ~/lab3
find . -type l
find . -name VERSION -newer notes.txt
    ./opt/stagedoor/current
./c.txt
./opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.02.1/VERSION
./opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.02.2/VERSION
    The broken c.txt still shows up: it's a symlink, whether or not its target exists. find . -xtype l lists only broken ones.
  8. Break it on purpose: delete a file that's still open. Start a small program that writes 10 MB to app.log and keeps the file open, the way an app keeps its log open. Then delete the file and look at the program's open files in /proc: 
    python3 -c 'import time; f = open("app.log", "a"); f.write("x" * 10_000_000); f.flush(); time.sleep(600)' &
sleep 1
ls -l app.log
rm app.log
ls -l /proc/$!/fd | grep deleted
kill $!
    -rw-r--r-- 1 tiago tiago 10000000 Nov 27 08:02 app.log
l-wx------ 1 tiago tiago 64 Nov 27 08:02 3 -> /home/tiago/lab3/app.log (deleted)
    $! is the process ID of the last program started in the background. Its file descriptor 3 (Chapter 2) still points at the file, marked (deleted): the 10 MB are still on the disk, and stay there until the kill ends the program. That's Stagedoor's nohup.out, in miniature.

Stretch goals. (1) Run stat notes.txt, then chmod o-r notes.txt and stat again: which timestamp changed, and why not the other? (2) Write a three-line script deploy.sh VERSION that switches current atomically and refuses to run if releases/VERSION doesn't exist. (3) Find the five largest files in your home directory: find ~ -type f -printf '%s %p\n' | sort -rn | head -5.

3.10Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Running an app from a home directoryThe app depends on a person's account; secrets, code and data mix./opt for code, /etc for config, /var/lib and /var/log for data and logs.
Deploying with git pull or cp into the live directoryRequests during the deploy see half-old, half-new code.Unpack beside the old release; switch a symlink with mv -T.
Keeping anything important in /tmpCleaned at boot or by a timer; readable by others if careless./var/lib/<app>; for scratch files, mktemp (Chapter 17).
Deleting a big log to free spaceThe app keeps it open; the space isn't freed.Truncate it (: > file) or rotate and signal the app (Chapter 7).
Absolute symlinks inside an app's treeCopying or moving the tree leaves links pointing at the old place.Relative targets within the app's directory.
find … -delete or -exec rm without a dry runDeletes more than you meant; there's no undo.Run the same find without the action first and read the list.

✓Chapter summary

  • Everything is in one tree from /. Programs live in /usr, configuration in /etc, changing data in /var; /proc, /sys, /dev and /run are made by the kernel.
  • Follow the convention for your own app: code in /opt, config in /etc, data in /var/lib, logs in /var/log.
  • A file is an inode; names are directory entries pointing at it. ls -l shows the inode's type, permissions, link count, owner, size and time.
  • A hard link is another name for the same inode; a symbolic link is a small file holding a path, and can dangle.
  • Switch releases atomically: new symlink under a temporary name, then mv -T over the old one.
  • Deleting a file that a program has open frees nothing until the program closes it.

Key terms

Root directory
The top of the single filesystem tree, written /.
FHS
The Filesystem Hierarchy Standard: the conventional layout of directories on Linux.
Mount point
The directory where another filesystem is attached to the tree.
Inode
The record of a file's type, owner, permissions, size, times and data location; everything except its name.
Hard link
An additional directory entry pointing to the same inode.
Symbolic link
A small file containing a path to another file or directory; also called a symlink.
Atomic operation
A change that happens completely or not at all, with no in-between state visible to others; renaming within a filesystem is atomic.
/proc
A filesystem generated by the kernel that shows processes and kernel state as files.

Check your understanding

  1. Where would you expect to find: nginx's configuration, PostgreSQL's data files, the python3 program, and a process's list of open files? recall

    Show answer

    /etc/nginx/; /var/lib/postgresql/… (on Ubuntu and Debian); /usr/bin/python3; /proc/<pid>/fd/.

  2. ls -l shows -rw-r----- 3 postgres postgres 8192 Feb 9 10:02 0001. What does the 3 tell you, and what doesn't ls tell you about it? apply

    Show answer

    The inode has three names (hard links), somewhere on the same filesystem. ls doesn't say where the other two names are; ls -i gives the inode number, and find / -xdev -inum <number> finds them.

  3. Explain why mv -T current.new current is safe during a deploy, and why rm current; ln -s releases/new current isn't. explain

    Show answer

    A rename within one filesystem replaces the old name in a single step, so any lookup of current finds either the old link or the new one. With rm then ln, there's a moment when current doesn't exist, and a request or a service restart in that moment fails.

  4. The disk on a server is full. You delete a 6 GB log file, but df still says 100%. Why, and how do you confirm it? debug

    Show answer

    A running process still has the file open, so its inode and data aren't freed. Confirm with ls -l /proc/*/fd 2>/dev/null | grep deleted or lsof +L1, which list open files with no names left. The space comes back when that process closes the file or is restarted; next time, truncate or rotate instead of deleting (Chapter 7).

  5. Design the layout for a small Go service called scanner that keeps a local cache of ticket barcodes and writes a log. Say where each part goes. design

    Show answer

    Binary per release in /opt/scanner/releases/<version>/ with a current symlink; configuration in /etc/scanner/; the barcode cache in /var/cache/scanner/ if it can be rebuilt from the server, otherwise /var/lib/scanner/; logs in /var/log/scanner/ (or the journal, Chapter 7); a socket or PID file in /run/scanner/.

  6. Compare a hard link and a symbolic link for keeping a “latest backup” name that always points to the newest backup file. compare

    Show answer

    A symlink suits it: it's visibly a pointer, can be switched atomically, and works across filesystems. A hard link would work only on the same filesystem, wouldn't show which backup it is without comparing inode numbers, and would keep an old backup's data alive after the backup itself was “deleted”.

Going further

  • The Filesystem Hierarchy Standard 3.0, and man 7 hier on your machine.
  • man 7 inode, man 7 symlink, man 2 rename (the atomicity guarantee).
  • man find, especially the EXAMPLES section.
  • The systemd view of the same layout: man 7 file-hierarchy.

Part I · Finding your way

4

Users, groups and permissions

Every file on a Linux server belongs to someone, and every process runs as someone. This chapter explains the small, strict rules the kernel uses to decide who may read, write and run what, how to grant exactly the access a program needs, and how to replace a shared root password with personal accounts and sudo.

By the end you can

  • Explain users, groups and IDs, and where Linux stores them.
  • Read and set permission bits on files and directories, in symbolic and octal form.
  • Predict the kernel's decision for any user, file and action.
  • Set up a shared directory where one program writes and another only reads, with setgid and umask.
  • Give people administrative access through sudo, and narrow it to single commands.

Before you start

  • Chapter 3: inodes and ls -l.
  • A Linux machine where you can use sudo: a VM or a container (Appendix C). The lab creates two users and removes them at the end.

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
35 minutes, a Linux machine with sudo, free
Main distribution
Ubuntu, with other distributions in 4.9

At Stagedoor

On Tuesday 17 February 2026, Rafaela runs one find command on each server: every directory that anyone on the machine may write to. Apart from /tmp, which is meant to be, one comes back on door-2 and door-3: /srv/uploads, where venues' posters and seating plans live. Its permissions are drwxrwxrwx, “777”. Leonor remembers why: in 2024 an upload failed with “Permission denied”, and chmod 777 made the error go away.

Among the posters, Rafaela finds a dozen files owned by marco. Marco Antunes was a contractor who built the door-scanning API in 2024 and left that November; his account still exists. The files are harmless test images. But nginx serves this directory on Stagedoor's own domain, so anything on the machine, any user or any compromised program, could replace a venue's poster or drop in a web page that fans' browsers would trust. And everyone, Rafaela included, still logs in as root with the password pinned in the chat.

4.1Users, groups and IDs

To the kernel, a user is a number: the user ID (UID). Names are for people. Every process runs with a UID, every file has an owner UID, and the permission check (4.3) compares the two.

A group is also a number, the GID, with a list of member users. Every user has one primary group (new files get it) and can belong to any number of supplementary groups. Groups are how you give several users, or several programs, the same access without giving each one separately.

Three kinds of user exist on every server:

  • root, UID 0. The kernel skips most permission checks for UID 0. It's not a person; it's a level of power.
  • System users for services: www-data (nginx on Ubuntu), postgres, sshd. They have low UIDs (below 1000 on Ubuntu), no password and usually no login shell (/usr/sbin/nologin). A service running as its own user can only damage what that user owns.
  • People, with UIDs from 1000 up, a home directory and a shell.

The databases are plain text files in /etc:

Table 4.1 · Where users and groups are stored
FileOne line perExample
