/etc/passwduser: name, UID, primary GID, description, home, shell (readable by everyone; despite the name, no passwords)www-data:x:33:33:www-data:/var/www:/usr/sbin/nologin
/etc/shadowuser's password hash and ageing rules; readable only by rootmarco:$y$j9T$…:20057:0:99999:7:::
/etc/groupgroup: name, GID, memberssudo:x:27:leonor,rafaela

Read them with getent passwd name and getent group name rather than cat, because getent also asks any directory service the machine is connected to. id shows who you are right now: your UID, primary group and every group you're in.

Group changes and sessionsGroups are read when you log in. After adding yourself to a group, log out and in again (or run newgrp) before the new membership counts. Running services must be restarted for the same reason.

4.2Permission bits

Every inode carries nine permission bits: read, write and execute, for three classes of user. ls -l shows them after the file type:

The same three letters mean different things on a file and on a directory, and the directory meanings cause most surprises:

Table 4.2 · What r, w and x allow
BitOn a fileOn a directory
rread its contentslist the names in it
wchange its contentscreate, delete and rename entries in it, including other people's files
xrun it as a programenter it and reach things inside by name (“traverse”)

Two consequences are worth saying twice. Deleting a file is a change to the directory, not the file: if you can write to a directory, you can delete any file in it, whoever owns the file and whatever its own permissions say. That's what made Stagedoor's 777 directory dangerous. And to reach /var/lib/stagedoor/uploads/poster.jpg, a user needs x on every directory along the path, not only read on the file.

4.3How the kernel decides

When a process opens a file, the kernel compares the process's UID and groups with the file's owner and group, picks exactly one class, and checks only that class's bits:

Key idea

To debug “Permission denied”, ask three questions: which user and groups is the process running as, which class does that put it in for this file, and does every directory on the path allow it in?

namei -l /var/lib/stagedoor/uploads/poster.jpg answers the third question in one go: it lists the owner and permissions of every directory on the path, so a missing x stands out.

4.4Changing owners and permissions

chown user:group file changes the owner and group (only root may give a file away); chgrp group file changes only the group; chmod changes the bits, in either of two notations:

  • Symbolic: who (u, g, o, a), how (+, -, =), what (r, w, x). chmod g+r file, chmod o= file, chmod u=rwx,g=rx,o= dir. Good for small changes, because it leaves the other bits alone.
  • Octal: three digits, as in Figure 4.1. chmod 640 file. Good for setting everything at once, as scripts do.
Table 4.3 · Modes you'll use again and again
ModeSymbolicFor
644rw-r--r--ordinary files everyone may read: most of /etc
640rw-r-----files one service writes and one group reads
600rw-------secrets: private keys, .env files with passwords
755rwxr-xr-xprograms and directories everyone may enter
750rwxr-x---directories for one service and one group
2750rwxr-s---the same, and new files inherit the directory's group (4.5)
1777rwxrwxrwt/tmp: everyone may create, only owners may delete (4.5)
777rwxrwxrwxalmost never. If it “fixes” something, the real problem is ownership

chmod -R and chown -R change a whole tree. Be careful with chmod -R and octal modes: chmod -R 640 dir removes x from every directory inside, and nobody, not even the owner, can enter them any more. Use symbolic modes with a capital X (“execute only on directories, or where some x is already set”): chmod -R u=rwX,g=rX,o= dir.

4.5The special bits: setuid, setgid and sticky

Three more bits sit in front of the nine, as a fourth octal digit:

  • setuid (4000, shown as s in the owner's x) on a program makes it run as the file's owner, whoever starts it. /usr/bin/passwd is setuid root, so ordinary users can change their own password in /etc/shadow. Every setuid-root program is a door into root if it has a bug; you'll audit them in Chapter 18. Never set it on your own scripts.
  • setgid (2000, s in the group's x) on a directory makes new files and subdirectories inside it inherit the directory's group, instead of the creator's primary group. This is the tool for a directory shared between a writer and a reader, as the lab shows.
  • sticky bit (1000, t in the other's x) on a directory means that only a file's owner (or root) may delete or rename it, even though others may write to the directory. /tmp is 1777: everyone can create files there, nobody can delete anyone else's.

Stagedoor's /srv/uploads was 777, without even the sticky bit: any user on the machine could delete or replace any poster.

4.6umask: the default for new files

When a program creates a file, it asks for a mode (usually 666 for files, 777 for directories), and the kernel removes the bits set in the process's umask. A umask of 022 removes write for group and other: new files are 644, directories 755. A umask of 027 also removes everything for other: files 640, directories 750.

The umask belongs to the process and is inherited by its children, so a service's umask decides the permissions of every file it creates. umask with no argument shows your shell's; systemd sets a service's with UMask= (Chapter 6). For an app that writes files other programs shouldn't see, 027 is a good default.

4.7root and sudo

Logging in as root, especially with a shared password, has every problem a shared account has: nobody knows who did what, a leaver keeps the password, and every typo runs with unlimited power. The standard answer is sudo: people log in with their own accounts, and run individual commands as root when they need to, after proving who they are.

  • Who may do what is in /etc/sudoers and, better, in small files under /etc/sudoers.d/. On Ubuntu, members of the group sudo may run anything: %sudo ALL=(ALL:ALL) ALL.
  • Narrow rules name the exact command: tiago ALL=(root) /usr/bin/systemctl restart stagedoor lets Tiago restart the app and nothing else. Use full paths: the rule matches the program, not the name you type.
  • Edit with visudo (or check a file with visudo -cf file), never with a plain editor. A syntax error in sudoers can lock everybody out of sudo, and on a cloud VM without a root password that can mean rebuilding the machine.
  • Every use is logged, with the person's name, to /var/log/auth.log on Ubuntu (and the journal, Chapter 7). That's the accountability the shared root password couldn't give.
  • sudo -l shows what you may run; sudo -i opens a root shell when you really need several commands (prefer single commands, so each is logged).

Watch out: sudo rules that are root in disguise

A rule that allows an editor (sudo vim /etc/nginx/nginx.conf), a pager, tar, find or a script the user can modify gives full root, because each of these can start a shell or write any file. Allow specific, non-interactive commands; for editing files as root, use sudoedit, which edits a copy as you and writes it back.

4.8Access control lists

Owner, group and other is sometimes too coarse: you want one more user, or a second group, to read a directory without changing its owner or group. ACLs (access control lists) add named entries to an inode: setfacl -m u:tiago:rx dir gives Tiago read and enter on one directory. getfacl dir shows every entry; ls -l marks files that have an ACL with a + after the permission bits.

ACLs have a mask entry, which caps what the named entries and the group may have; when an ACL seems not to work, check the mask first. A default ACL on a directory (setfacl -d -m …) is copied to new files created in it.

Use ACLs sparingly. They're invisible in a normal ls -l apart from the +, some backup and copy tools drop them, and a server whose access only makes sense with getfacl is hard to review. When a group would do, use a group.

4.9Across distributions

Table 4.4 · Same ideas, different names
Ubuntu, DebianRHEL family, Amazon Linux
Administrators' groupsudowheel
nginx runs aswww-datanginx
Authentication log/var/log/auth.log/var/log/secure
First UID for people10001000
Friendly user creationadduser (asks questions); useradd for scriptsuseradd
Cloud image's default userubuntu, admin (Debian)ec2-user (Amazon Linux, RHEL on AWS)

On the RHEL family

SELinux checks every access a second time, after the permission bits, against rules about what each kind of program may touch. nginx may read files labelled as web content and not, say, a user's home directory, even if the bits allow it. A “Permission denied” that survives a correct ls -l on RHEL is usually SELinux; ausearch -m avc -ts recent shows the denial. Chapter 18 covers it.

4.10Lab: fix the uploads directory

Lab 4 · From 777 to exactly enough

You'll recreate Stagedoor's world-writable uploads directory, abuse it as another user, then fix it so the app writes, nginx reads and nobody else gets in; then grant one extra reader with an ACL and one narrow sudo rule. You need a Linux VM or container where you can use sudo (Appendix C); on Ubuntu, the user www-data already exists. For the ACL step: sudo apt install acl. Cost: nothing. Clean-up: the last step removes everything. Outputs come from a user called tiago; your IDs, names and times will differ.

  1. Who are you? 
    id
id www-data
getent group sudo
    uid=30033(tiago) gid=30033(tiago) groups=30033(tiago)
uid=33(www-data) gid=33(www-data) groups=33(www-data)
sudo:x:27:ubuntu
    On this lab machine the first person's UID happened to be 30033 (usually it's 1000 or 1001). www-data is a system user with UID 33. The last line lists who may use sudo through the sudo group.
  2. Recreate the problem. Create a system user for the app, a second person (marco), and a 777 uploads directory; then write one poster as the app and one file as Marco: 
    sudo useradd --system --user-group --no-create-home --shell /usr/sbin/nologin stagedoor
sudo useradd --create-home --shell /bin/bash marco
sudo mkdir -p /srv/lab4/uploads
sudo chmod 777 /srv/lab4/uploads
sudo -u stagedoor sh -c "echo poster > /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-101.jpg"
sudo -u marco sh -c "echo not-a-poster > /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-102.jpg"
ls -la /srv/lab4/uploads
    total 16
drwxrwxrwx 2 root      root      4096 Nov 27 08:05 .
drwxr-xr-x 3 root      root      4096 Nov 27 08:05 ..
-rw-rw-r-- 1 stagedoor stagedoor    7 Nov 27 08:05 poster-101.jpg
-rw-rw-r-- 1 marco     marco       13 Nov 27 08:05 poster-102.jpg
    sudo -u name runs one command as another user: here it stands in for the app writing a file, and for Marco logging in.
  3. Abuse it. As Marco, delete the app's poster and put your own in its place: 
    sudo -u marco rm /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-101.jpg
sudo -u marco sh -c "echo replaced > /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-101.jpg"
ls -l /srv/lab4/uploads
    total 8
-rw-rw-r-- 1 marco marco  9 Nov 27 08:05 poster-101.jpg
-rw-rw-r-- 1 marco marco 13 Nov 27 08:05 poster-102.jpg
    No error. Marco can't write to the app's file (it's rw-rw-r--, and he's “other”), but he can write to the directory, so he can delete the file and create a new one with the same name (Table 4.2). nginx would now serve his file as venue 101's poster.
  4. Fix it. Remove both files, then give the directory to the app, with nginx's group, setgid, and nothing for anyone else; write a poster as the app with umask 027: 
    sudo rm /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-101.jpg /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-102.jpg
sudo chown stagedoor:www-data /srv/lab4/uploads
sudo chmod 2750 /srv/lab4/uploads
sudo -u stagedoor sh -c "umask 027; echo poster > /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-101.jpg"
ls -ld /srv/lab4/uploads
sudo ls -l /srv/lab4/uploads
    drwxr-s--- 2 stagedoor www-data 4096 Nov 27 08:05 /srv/lab4/uploads
total 4
-rw-r----- 1 stagedoor www-data 7 Nov 27 08:05 poster-101.jpg
    The directory is rwxr-s---: the app may do anything, the group www-data may list and enter, others nothing; the s is setgid. The new file got the group www-data from the directory, and mode 640 from the umask. (You needed sudo for the second ls: you're now “other” for this directory, too.)
  5. Test every user against Figure 4.2. nginx reads, nginx can't write, Marco can't even look: 
    sudo -u www-data cat /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-101.jpg
sudo -u www-data touch /srv/lab4/uploads/evil.jpg
sudo -u marco ls /srv/lab4/uploads
sudo -u marco cat /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-101.jpg
    poster
touch: cannot touch '/srv/lab4/uploads/evil.jpg': Permission denied
ls: cannot open directory '/srv/lab4/uploads': Permission denied
cat: /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-101.jpg: Permission denied
    For each line, say which class the user falls into and which bit decided. (Marco's cat fails on the directory's missing x for other, before the file's own bits are even considered.)
  6. Break it on purpose: without setgid. Remove the setgid bit and write another poster: 
    sudo chmod g-s /srv/lab4/uploads
sudo -u stagedoor sh -c "umask 027; echo poster > /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-102.jpg"
sudo ls -l /srv/lab4/uploads
sudo -u www-data cat /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-102.jpg
    total 8
-rw-r----- 1 stagedoor www-data  7 Nov 27 08:05 poster-101.jpg
-rw-r----- 1 stagedoor stagedoor 7 Nov 27 08:05 poster-102.jpg
cat: /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-102.jpg: Permission denied
    The new file took the app's own primary group, stagedoor, so nginx is “other” for it and can't serve it: a broken poster on the website, and the kind of failure that tempts people to type chmod 777. Put it right: sudo chmod g+s /srv/lab4/uploads and sudo chgrp www-data /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-102.jpg.
  7. Break it on purpose: the wrong umask. 
    sudo -u stagedoor sh -c "umask 022; echo poster > /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-103.jpg"
sudo ls -l /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-103.jpg
    -rw-r--r-- 1 stagedoor www-data 7 Nov 27 08:05 /srv/lab4/uploads/poster-103.jpg
    Readable by everyone. Here the directory still keeps others out, but copy or move that file anywhere else and it's open. The umask is the app's responsibility; set it in the service (Chapter 6).
  8. One extra reader with an ACL. Let tiago (use your own name) list the uploads without joining www-data: 
    sudo setfacl -m u:tiago:rx /srv/lab4/uploads
ls -ld /srv/lab4/uploads
getfacl -p /srv/lab4/uploads
ls /srv/lab4/uploads
    drwxr-s---+ 2 stagedoor www-data 4096 Nov 27 08:05 /srv/lab4/uploads
# file: /srv/lab4/uploads
# owner: stagedoor
# group: www-data
# flags: -s-
user::rwx
user:tiago:r-x
group::r-x
mask::r-x
other::---

poster-101.jpg
poster-102.jpg
poster-103.jpg
    The + after the bits says “there's more: run getfacl”.
  9. One narrow sudo rule. Allow tiago to restart the app and nothing else, check the file's syntax, and list what the rule grants: 
    echo "tiago ALL=(root) /usr/bin/systemctl restart stagedoor" | sudo tee /etc/sudoers.d/lab4 > /dev/null
sudo chmod 440 /etc/sudoers.d/lab4
sudo visudo -cf /etc/sudoers.d/lab4
sudo -l -U tiago
    /etc/sudoers.d/lab4: parsed OK
Matching Defaults entries for tiago on vm:
    env_reset, mail_badpass,
    secure_path=/usr/local/sbin\:/usr/local/bin\:/usr/sbin\:/usr/bin\:/sbin\:/bin\:/snap/bin,
    use_pty

User tiago may run the following commands on vm:
    (root) /usr/bin/systemctl restart stagedoor
    (On a machine where you're in the sudo group, the list also shows (ALL : ALL) ALL.) Notice secure_path: sudo replaces your PATH with a fixed, safe one, another reason commands can behave differently under sudo. Now see what visudo catches; check a file with a stray parenthesis, without installing it: 
    echo "tiago ALL=(root)) /usr/bin/systemctl" | sudo tee /tmp/bad > /dev/null

    /tmp/bad:1:17: syntax error

exit status: 1
  10. Clean up. 
    sudo rm /etc/sudoers.d/lab4 /tmp/bad
sudo rm -r /srv/lab4

sudo userdel stagedoor
    userdel may warn that Marco's mail spool wasn't found; that's harmless.

Stretch goals. (1) Find every world-writable directory on your machine that lacks the sticky bit: sudo find / -xdev -type d -perm -0002 ! -perm -1000. (2) Find every setuid program: sudo find / -xdev -perm -4000 -type f, and look up why each one needs it. (3) Use namei -l on a file deep in /var/lib and spot the directory that would stop an ordinary user.

The uploads move to /var/lib/stagedoor/uploads, the place Chapter 3's layout gave them: owned by stagedoor:www-data, mode 2750, every existing file 640 and in the group www-data. nginx's configuration points there; the five-minute rsync to door-3 keeps the same ownership with rsync -a. Two things remain on her list. The app itself still runs as root, from a terminal, so it can still write anywhere: that ends when it becomes a service running as stagedoor with UMask=0027 (Chapter 6). And locking an account's password doesn't stop someone who logs in with an SSH key, which is why Chapter 16 goes looking for keys.

4.11Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
chmod 777 to fix “Permission denied”The error goes away, and so does all protection: anyone on the machine can replace or delete files.Find which user the process runs as (Figure 4.2), and fix ownership or group.
Testing access with sudoRoot skips the checks; it “works” and proves nothing.Test as the real user: sudo -u www-data cat file.
Running services as rootAny bug in the service is a bug with root's power.One system user per service, owning only what it needs.
A shared root passwordNo accountability; leavers keep access; changing it breaks everyone.Personal accounts with sudo; root's password in a vault for emergencies.
chmod -R 640 on a treeDirectories lose x; nobody can enter them.chmod -R u=rwX,g=rX,o= dir.
Editing sudoers with a plain editorOne typo and sudo stops working for everyone.visudo, or visudo -cf on a drop-in file.
Locking a leaver's password and calling it doneSSH keys, cron jobs and running processes still work.Lock, expire, then check keys, crontabs and processes (Chapter 16).

✓Chapter summary

  • Users and groups are numbers (UID, GID) with names in /etc/passwd and /etc/group. Services get their own system users.
  • Nine permission bits give read, write and execute to the owner, the group and everyone else. On directories, w means create and delete entries, and x means enter.
  • The kernel picks exactly one class for a process (owner, group or other) and checks only that class. Root skips the check.
  • setgid on a directory makes new files inherit its group; the sticky bit stops people deleting each other's files; the umask sets the default mode of new files.
  • A shared directory for a writer and a reader: owner = writer, group = reader, mode 2750, writer's umask 027.
  • Personal accounts and sudo replace a shared root password; narrow rules name exact commands; every use is logged.
  • ACLs add named users or groups when a single group isn't enough; use them sparingly.

Key terms

User
An identity the kernel knows by its user ID (UID); processes run as a user and files are owned by one.
UID
The number that identifies a user to the kernel; 0 is root.
Group
A numbered set of users that can be given access together.
Permission bits
Read, write and execute for a file's owner, its group and everyone else.
setgid (directory)
A directory bit that makes new entries inherit the directory's group.
Sticky bit
A directory bit that lets only a file's owner (or root) delete or rename it.
umask
A per-process mask of permission bits removed from every new file and directory.
sudo
A program that lets permitted users run commands as root (or another user), with logging, according to rules in sudoers.
ACL
An access control list: extra permission entries for named users and groups on a file or directory.

Check your understanding

  1. Write rwxr-x--- and rw------- in octal, and say what each is typically used for. recall

    Show answer

    750: a directory (or program) for its owner, readable and enterable by one group, closed to everyone else. 600: a secret only its owner may read and write, such as a private key or a file with a password.

  2. A file is -rw----r-- 1 rafaela ops. Rafaela tries to read it. Is she allowed, and why? apply

    Show answer

    Yes: she's the owner, so only the owner bits count, and they include r. (If the owner bits had been ---, she would be denied even though “other” has r, because only one class is checked.)

  3. Why can a user delete a file they don't own and can't write to? explain

    Show answer

    Deleting removes a name from the directory, so it needs write (and execute) permission on the directory, not on the file. Without the sticky bit, anyone who can write to the directory can delete any entry in it.

  4. nginx returns 403 for /var/lib/stagedoor/uploads/plan-7.pdf, but sudo cat of the file works. List what you'd check, in order. debug

    Show answer

    sudo cat proves nothing, because root skips the checks. Check which user nginx's workers run as (ps -o user= -C nginx: the master is root, the workers that open files are www-data); run namei -l on the path to see that www-data can traverse every directory; check the file's group and mode (probably created without the setgid directory, so its group is stagedoor, or with a bad umask); test with sudo -u www-data cat. On RHEL, also check SELinux denials.

  5. Design permissions for /etc/stagedoor/stagedoor.env, which holds the database password: the app must read it, the deploy process (running as a person with sudo) must update it, nginx must not see it. design

    Show answer

    Owner root, group stagedoor, mode 640; the directory /etc/stagedoor owned root:stagedoor, mode 750. The app (user stagedoor) reads through the group but can't change it; deploys update it with sudo; nginx (www-data) is “other” and gets nothing. (Chapter 6 loads it with systemd's EnvironmentFile=.)

  6. Compare giving Tiago read access to the uploads by adding him to www-data with giving it through an ACL. compare

    Show answer

    Joining www-data gives him everything nginx's group can read, everywhere on the machine, which is broader than needed but visible in /etc/group. An ACL gives exactly this directory and nothing else, but it's less visible (only a + in ls -l) and easy to forget. For one person and one directory, the ACL is the narrower choice; for a role several people share, make a dedicated group.

Going further

  • man 7 credentials, man 7 path_resolution (exactly how the kernel checks a path), man chmod.
  • man sudoers, especially the examples, and sudo's documentation.
  • man 5 acl and man setfacl.
  • The Ubuntu Server documentation, section “User management”.

Part II · Processes and services

5

Processes and signals

Everything that runs on a server is a process: started by another process, identified by a number, and stopped by a signal. This chapter shows how processes are born and how they die, how to read ps and top without guessing, and how to stop a server so that the requests it's handling finish instead of vanishing.

By the end you can

  • Explain how fork, exec, exit and wait create and end processes.
  • Read a process tree and the state column of ps, including zombies.
  • Choose the right signal (TERM, KILL, HUP, INT) and predict what it does.
  • Write a server that shuts down gracefully, and prove the difference with a test.
  • Read exit statuses such as 137 and 143.

Before you start

  • Chapter 2: exit codes, background jobs with &.
  • A Linux terminal with Python 3 and curl (Appendix C); two terminal windows help.

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, any Linux terminal, free
Main distribution
any; the tools are the same everywhere

At Stagedoor

On Thursday 26 February 2026 at 15:10, Leonor deploys a small fix to door-2 and door-3. Stagedoor's deploy script has always ended with the same two lines: pkill -9 -f uvicorn, then start the app again with nohup. It takes a second, and nobody has ever complained.

This time, a small venue's tickets are on sale. On Friday, three fans write in: their cards were charged at 15:10, and they have no tickets. The payment provider confirmed each payment, but the app process that was waiting for that confirmation no longer existed when it arrived, so no order was saved. Joana refunds them and apologises. Rafaela wants to know exactly what -9 did to those three requests, and Tiago, who has used kill -9 for years “because it always works”, is about to find out.

5.1What a process is

A process is a running program: the program's code plus everything the kernel tracks while it runs. A program is a file on disk; you can run it ten times at once and get ten processes.

For each process, the kernel keeps:

  • a PID (process ID), unique while the process exists, and the PPID, its parent's PID;
  • the user and groups it runs as (Chapter 4), its current directory, its environment variables and its umask;
  • its memory (Chapter 10), its open file descriptors (Chapter 2, Chapter 12), and its resource limits;
  • its state (running, sleeping, stopped…) and how much CPU time it has used.

All of it is visible under /proc/<pid>/: status for a readable summary, cmdline for the command, environ for the environment, fd/ for open files, limits for limits. /proc/self is always the process reading it.

5.2How processes are born, and how they end

Linux creates processes in two steps, and the split explains a lot of behaviour you'll see:

  1. fork() makes an almost exact copy of the calling process, with a new PID. The copy has the same memory (copied lazily, only when either side writes), the same open files, user, environment and current directory.
  2. exec() replaces the program running in a process with another one. The PID, open files, user and environment stay.
  3. exit(status) ends a process. Its memory and files are released at once, but the kernel keeps a tiny record, the exit status, for the parent.
  4. wait() is how the parent collects that status. Until it does, the finished child is a zombie: not running, holding no memory, but still listed, with state Z.

Zombies themselves are harmless; a parent that never waits for its children is a bug, and thousands of zombies can exhaust the PID space. If a parent dies first, its children become orphans and are adopted by PID 1, whose job includes waiting for them. That's one reason PID 1 is special, and why what runs as PID 1 inside a container matters (Chapter 19).

5.3Seeing processes

Every process except PID 1 has a parent, so all processes form a tree with PID 1 at the root. ps shows a snapshot; top (or the friendlier htop) a live view:

ps -ef --forest                          # every process, as a tree, with its full command
ps -o pid,ppid,user,stat,etime,cmd -C nginx   # chosen columns, for one program
pgrep -a uvicorn                         # PIDs and commands matching a name
pstree -p                                # the tree, compactly
top                                      # live; press P (CPU), M (memory), q to quit

The STAT column of ps (S in top) is the process's state. The first letter matters most:

Table 5.1 · Process states
StateMeansTypical cause
Rrunning, or ready to run and waiting for a CPUdoing work (Chapter 9)
Ssleeping, interruptibly: waiting for somethinga network request, a timer, input; most processes, most of the time
Dsleeping, uninterruptibly: waiting for the kernel, usually disk I/Oa slow or stuck disk or network filesystem; can't be killed until the I/O finishes
TstoppedCtrl+Z, or a debugger
Zzombie: finished, waiting for its parent to collect the statusa parent that doesn't call wait

Extra letters follow: s a session leader, l multithreaded, + in the terminal's foreground, < and N high and low priority (Chapter 9).

5.4Signals

A signal is a small numbered message the kernel delivers to a process: “stop”, “hang up”, “your child died”. A process can catch most signals and run its own code (a handler), ignore them, or leave the default action, which for most signals is to terminate. Two signals can't be caught or ignored: SIGKILL and SIGSTOP. The kernel carries them out itself.

Table 5.2 · The signals you'll send and receive
SignalNo.DefaultUsed for
SIGTERM15terminate“please shut down”. The default of kill, and what systemd and Docker send first. Well-written servers catch it and shut down gracefully.
SIGINT2terminateCtrl+C in a terminal; Python raises KeyboardInterrupt.
SIGHUP1terminateoriginally “the terminal hung up”; by convention, daemons such as nginx catch it to reload configuration.
SIGKILL9terminate, can't be caughtthe last resort: the process gets no chance to finish anything.
SIGQUIT3terminate and dump coreCtrl+\; nginx uses it for a graceful stop.
SIGUSR1, SIGUSR210, 12terminatedefined by each program: nginx reopens its log files on USR1 (Chapter 7).
SIGCHLD17ignoresent to a parent when a child exits, so it can wait.
SIGPIPE13terminatewriting to a pipe nobody reads: why grep | head stops grep (Chapter 2).
SIGSTOP, SIGCONT19, 18stop, continuepause and resume; Ctrl+Z sends the catchable SIGTSTP.

Send signals with kill -s TERM 2187 (or kill -TERM, or kill -15), to every process with a name with pkill, and list them all with kill -l. Prefer names to numbers: they're the same on every machine and say what you mean. And be careful with pkill -f: it matches the whole command line, so pkill -f uvicorn also matches an editor with a file called uvicorn.conf open.

When a process dies from a signal, the shell reports exit status 128 + the signal number (Chapter 2): 143 is SIGTERM, 137 is SIGKILL, 130 is SIGINT. When a container or service exits with 137, ask who sent SIGKILL: a person, a stop timeout, or the kernel's out-of-memory killer (Chapter 10).

nohup and hangupsWhen you log out, your terminal's processes get SIGHUP and, by default, die. nohup cmd & makes cmd ignore it, which is why Stagedoor's app survived logouts. It doesn't make it a service: nothing restarts it, and it doesn't come back after a reboot (Chapter 6).

5.5Graceful shutdown

A server stopping is a normal event: every deploy, every reboot, every scale-down stops one. What matters is what happens to the work in progress. Compare the two ways Stagedoor's app can stop:

A graceful shutdown has four parts, and the lab builds each one:

  1. Catch SIGTERM. If the program doesn't, the default action kills it as abruptly as SIGKILL would. Many frameworks do this for you (uvicorn, gunicorn, nginx, PostgreSQL); check, don't assume.
  2. Stop taking new work: close the listening socket, or fail the health check, so new requests go to another instance.
  3. Finish the work in progress, within a time limit.
  4. Exit with status 0. Whoever sent SIGTERM waits for that, up to a deadline, and only then sends SIGKILL.

Key idea

Send SIGTERM, wait, and send SIGKILL only when the deadline passes. kill -9 first is not “stopping a server”; it's crashing it on purpose.

On a server you'll rarely send these signals by hand. systemd does exactly this sequence on systemctl stop (KillSignal= and TimeoutStopSec=, Chapter 6), Docker on docker stop, and Kubernetes when it removes a pod. Your job is to make sure the program handles SIGTERM well and that the deadline is longer than your slowest legitimate request.

Watch out: signals don't pass through shell wrappers

If a service is started by a shell script (start.sh that runs python3 app.py), the signal goes to the shell, which by default doesn't forward it, and the app never hears it. End such scripts with exec python3 app.py, so the app replaces the shell (Figure 5.1) and receives signals directly. The same trap catches container entrypoints (Chapter 19).

5.6Jobs, foreground and background

In an interactive shell, cmd & starts a command in the background: the shell doesn't wait, and $! holds its PID. jobs lists the shell's background jobs; Ctrl+Z stops the foreground one; bg resumes it in the background, fg brings it back. These are conveniences for a terminal session, and they die with it (or survive with nohup). For anything that must keep running, use a service; for long interactive work over SSH, use tmux so a dropped connection doesn't take your session with it.

5.7Across distributions

Processes, signals and /proc are the kernel's, so they're identical on every distribution. The tools come from the procps package everywhere (ps, top, pgrep, pkill, free), with pstree from psmisc. Two differences appear on small or unusual systems:

On minimal images and containers

Container base images often leave out ps entirely (apt install procps or dnf install procps-ng), and Alpine-based images use BusyBox versions with fewer options. You can always fall back to /proc: ls /proc | grep -E '^[0-9]+$' lists PIDs, and cat /proc/<pid>/cmdline | tr '\0' ' ' shows a command.

5.8Lab: stop a server without losing a checkout

Lab 5 · Three fans, three signals

You'll run a tiny checkout server, send it three slow requests, and stop it mid-request with SIGTERM and SIGKILL; then add a graceful handler and repeat. Finally you'll make a zombie and watch who cleans it up. You need a Linux terminal (two windows are easiest), Python 3 and curl. Cost: nothing. Clean-up: delete the lab directory. PIDs in the outputs will differ on your machine.

  1. Save the server as checkout.py in a new directory ~/lab5. Each request sleeps 3 seconds, standing in for a payment provider; --graceful switches on a SIGTERM handler.

    checkout.pyPython 3.8+

    # checkout.py · a tiny web server whose checkout takes 3 seconds, like a call to a payment provider
import http.server, os, signal, sys, threading, time

class Checkout(http.server.BaseHTTPRequestHandler):
    def do_GET(self):
        time.sleep(3)                                   # waiting for the payment provider
        body = f"order confirmed by pid {os.getpid()}\n".encode()
        self.send_response(200)
        self.send_header("Content-Length", str(len(body)))
        self.end_headers()
        self.wfile.write(body)

    def log_message(self, *args):                       # keep the terminal quiet
        pass

server = http.server.ThreadingHTTPServer(("127.0.0.1", 8080), Checkout)
server.daemon_threads = False                           # at shutdown, wait for requests in progress

def graceful(signum, frame):
    print(f"got {signal.Signals(signum).name}: no new requests, finishing the ones in progress", flush=True)
    threading.Thread(target=server.shutdown).start()    # stop the accept loop

if "--graceful" in sys.argv:
    signal.signal(signal.SIGTERM, graceful)
print(f"pid {os.getpid()} listening on 127.0.0.1:8080", flush=True)
server.serve_forever()
server.server_close()                                   # waits for the request threads
print("all requests finished; exiting", flush=True)
  2. Save the fans as fans.sh and make it executable (chmod +x fans.sh). It starts three requests in the background, waits a second, and signals the server while they're in progress:

    fans.shbash

    #!/usr/bin/env bash
# fans.sh SIGNAL · start three checkouts, then send SIGNAL to the server while they're in progress
pid=$(pgrep -f '^python3 checkout.py')
for fan in 1 2 3; do
  ( curl -s -m 10 http://127.0.0.1:8080/checkout || echo "fan $fan: failed, curl exit status $?" ) &
done
sleep 1
echo "sending SIG$1 to pid $pid"
kill -s "$1" "$pid"
wait
    pgrep -f '^python3 checkout.py' finds the server by its command line; the ^ anchors the match at the start, so it can't match some other command that merely mentions the file.
  3. Stop it with SIGTERM, no handler. In window 1: cd ~/lab5 && python3 checkout.py. In window 2: cd ~/lab5 && ./fans.sh TERM. 
    sending SIGTERM to pid 6658
fan 2: failed, curl exit status 52
fan 3: failed, curl exit status 52
fan 1: failed, curl exit status 52
    In window 1 the server printed only its first line and stopped. Run echo $? there: 143, 128 + 15. The program didn't catch SIGTERM, so the default action terminated it mid-request, and the kernel closed all three connections (curl's status 52 means “empty reply from server”).
  4. Stop it with SIGKILL. Start the server again in window 1, then ./fans.sh KILL in window 2: 
    sending SIGKILL to pid 6675
fan 1: failed, curl exit status 52
fan 3: failed, curl exit status 52
fan 2: failed, curl exit status 52
    The same three failures, and echo $? in window 1 says 137. For this program TERM and KILL did the same damage, because it had no handler: that's Stagedoor's deploy on 26 February.
  5. Now gracefully. In window 1: python3 checkout.py --graceful. In window 2: ./fans.sh TERM. Window 2 shows: 
    sending SIGTERM to pid 6692
order confirmed by pid 6692
order confirmed by pid 6692
order confirmed by pid 6692
    and window 1: 
    pid 6692 listening on 127.0.0.1:8080
got SIGTERM: no new requests, finishing the ones in progress
all requests finished; exiting
    All three checkouts completed, about two seconds after the signal, and echo $? says 0. While the server was finishing, it had already stopped accepting: a fourth curl http://127.0.0.1:8080/ sent a fraction of a second after the signal fails to connect (curl's exit status 7) instead of starting a checkout that might not finish.
  6. Break it on purpose: a deadline that's too short. Start python3 checkout.py --graceful again, and in window 2 send SIGKILL half a second after the SIGTERM: 
    ./fans.sh TERM & sleep 1.5; pkill -KILL -f '^python3 checkout.py'
    sending SIGTERM to pid 6840
fan 2: failed, curl exit status 52
fan 1: failed, curl exit status 52
fan 3: failed, curl exit status 52
    Window 1 shows that the handler ran, and then nothing more: 
    pid 6840 listening on 127.0.0.1:8080
got SIGTERM: no new requests, finishing the ones in progress
    Nobody waited for it. A stop timeout shorter than your slowest request turns a graceful shutdown back into a crash.
  7. Make a zombie. Save this as zombie.py:

    zombie.pyPython 3.8+

    # zombie.py · a parent that never collects its child's exit status
import os, time

if os.fork() == 0:      # fork() returns 0 in the child
    os._exit(0)         # the child exits at once
time.sleep(60)          # the parent sleeps and never calls os.wait()
    Run it in the background and look at it, then kill the parent and look again: 
    python3 zombie.py &
sleep 1; ps -o pid,ppid,stat,cmd -C python3
kill $!
sleep 0.5; ps -o pid,ppid,stat,cmd -C python3
    On a normal Ubuntu server (PID 1 is systemd), the first ps shows the parent and its zombie child, Z and <defunct>: 
      PID  PPID STAT CMD
 6736  6735 S    python3 zombie.py
 6738  6736 Z    [python3] <defunct>
    and the second shows nothing but the header: when the parent died, the zombie was handed to systemd, which collected its status at once. 
        PID    PPID STAT CMD
    On the small VM where this book's labs were first tested, PID 1 isn't systemd (Lab 1), and the second ps said this instead: 
      PID  PPID STAT CMD
 6738     1 Z    [python3] <defunct>
    The zombie was adopted by PID 1 (its PPID is now 1), and that PID 1 never calls wait, so the zombie stays until the machine restarts. That's exactly the problem a container whose PID 1 is an ordinary app has, and Chapter 19 shows the fix.

Stretch goals. (1) Add a SIGHUP handler that prints “reloading” and keeps running; send it with kill -HUP. (2) Read /proc/<pid>/status for the running server and find its state, its threads and its signal masks (SigCgt lists the signals it catches, as a bitmask). (3) Put the server behind a wrapper script start.sh that runs python3 checkout.py --graceful without exec, signal the script's PID, and watch the requests fail; then add exec.

At Stagedoor: a deploy that waits

Tiago reproduces 26 February in the lab and adds the result to the incident note. uvicorn already handles SIGTERM gracefully; the deploy script never gave it the chance. The new script does three things on each server in turn, door-2 first: it fails the app's health check, so Atlantica's load balancer stops sending new fans there; it sends SIGTERM and waits up to 30 seconds (checkout's slowest normal request takes about 8); and only if the app is still running after that does it send SIGKILL, and say so loudly. Then it starts the new version, waits for the health check to pass, and moves on to door-3.

Rafaela points out that this is exactly what systemd does on systemctl restart, with less code to get wrong. Turning the app into a service is the next chapter, and the deploy script shrinks accordingly.

5.9Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
kill -9 as the first resortRequests in progress fail; files and transactions are left half-written.SIGTERM, wait, then SIGKILL after a deadline.
A server that doesn't handle SIGTERMSIGTERM is as abrupt as SIGKILL.Handle it, or use a framework that does; test it as in the lab.
A shell wrapper without execThe signal reaches the shell; the app never hears it and is killed at the deadline.exec the app as the script's last line.
pkill -f name with a loose patternKills unrelated processes whose command line contains the word.Use the PID, a precise anchored pattern, or the service manager.
Stop timeouts shorter than the slowest requestGraceful shutdown is cut off and becomes a crash.Measure; set the deadline above the slowest legitimate request.
Running servers in a terminal with nohupNo restart on crash, no start at boot, signals sent by hand.A systemd service (Chapter 6).

✓Chapter summary

  • A process is a running program with a PID, a parent, a user, open files and a state; /proc/<pid>/ shows all of it.
  • fork copies a process, exec replaces its program, exit ends it, and the parent's wait collects the status. Uncollected children are zombies; orphans are adopted by PID 1.
  • States: R running, S sleeping, D waiting on I/O and unkillable, T stopped, Z zombie.
  • Signals are caught, ignored or take their default action. SIGTERM asks; SIGKILL can't be refused. Death by signal n gives exit status 128 + n.
  • Graceful shutdown: catch SIGTERM, stop accepting, finish in-flight work, exit 0; whoever stops it waits for a deadline before SIGKILL.

Key terms

Process
A running instance of a program, with its own PID, memory, open files and state.
PID
The number the kernel uses to identify a process; PID 1 is the first process, the init system.
fork and exec
The two steps that start a program: copy the current process, then replace the copy's program.
Zombie
A finished process whose exit status hasn't yet been collected by its parent.
Orphan
A process whose parent has exited; it is adopted by PID 1.
Signal
A numbered notification the kernel delivers to a process, such as SIGTERM or SIGKILL.
Signal handler
Code a program registers to run when it receives a particular signal.
Graceful shutdown
Stopping a server so it accepts no new work, finishes work in progress, and exits cleanly.

Check your understanding

  1. A process shows state D and doesn't die after kill -9. Why? recall

    Show answer

    D is uninterruptible sleep: the process is inside the kernel waiting for I/O, usually a disk or a network filesystem. Signals, even SIGKILL, are delivered only when it returns from that wait. Find out what the I/O is stuck on (a failing disk, an unreachable NFS server); the process dies when the I/O completes or fails.

  2. A container exits with status 137, and nobody ran docker kill. Give two likely explanations. apply

    Show answer

    137 = 128 + 9, death by SIGKILL. Either the kernel's out-of-memory killer killed it (docker inspect shows OOMKilled: true; Chapter 10), or it was asked to stop and didn't exit within the stop timeout, so the runtime sent SIGKILL (for example because it never handles SIGTERM, or its PID 1 is a shell that doesn't forward it).

  3. Why does a redirection like python3 app.py > app.log still apply after the shell execs Python? explain

    Show answer

    The shell forks, and in the child it opens app.log as file descriptor 1 before calling exec. exec replaces the program but keeps the process's open file descriptors, so Python starts with its standard output already pointing at the file.

  4. systemctl stop myapp takes exactly 90 seconds every time, and the logs show no shutdown messages from the app. What's going on, and how do you fix it? debug

    Show answer

    The app isn't reacting to SIGTERM, so systemd waits for its default stop timeout (90 s) and then sends SIGKILL. Common causes: the service starts a shell script that doesn't exec the app; the app ignores or blocks SIGTERM; or the main process exits but leaves children behind. Fix the signal handling (or add exec), then set TimeoutStopSec= to a deadline that fits the app.

  5. Design a deploy for an app on two servers behind a load balancer so that no request in progress is lost. design

    Show answer

    One server at a time: take it out of the load balancer (fail its health check or deregister it) and wait for the balancer to notice; send SIGTERM and wait up to a deadline longer than the slowest request; SIGKILL only if it's still running, and alert; start the new version; wait until its health check passes and the balancer sends it traffic; then repeat on the second server.

  6. Compare SIGHUP and SIGTERM for a daemon such as nginx. compare

    Show answer

    By convention, SIGHUP asks a daemon to reload its configuration without stopping, and nginx starts new workers with the new configuration while old workers finish their requests. SIGTERM asks it to shut down. For a program that doesn't handle SIGHUP, the default action is to terminate, so check before sending it to an unknown process.

Going further

  • man 7 signal (every signal and its default action), man 2 fork, man 2 execve, man 2 wait, man 5 proc.
  • nginx: Controlling nginx (which signal does what).
  • uvicorn: Deployment, including graceful shutdown and timeouts.
  • Python: the signal module.

Part II · Processes and services

6

systemd: services, units and timers

A program you started by hand runs until something stops it. A service runs because the machine was told it should: started at boot, restarted when it crashes, stopped gracefully, logged, and limited. This chapter turns a hand-started app into a systemd service, explains the unit files behind every service on the machine, and replaces a fragile cron job with a timer.

By the end you can

  • Write a service unit that runs an app as its own user, with its configuration, umask and restart policy.
  • Start, stop, enable and inspect services with systemctl, and find out why one failed.
  • Override a packaged unit safely with a drop-in file.
  • Explain the difference between ordering (After=) and requirement (Wants=, Requires=).
  • Replace a cron job with a timer, and avoid the PATH and time-zone traps of scheduled jobs.

Before you start

  • Chapter 5: processes, signals, graceful shutdown. Chapter 4: users, umask.
  • A Linux machine where PID 1 is systemd: a VM or cloud server, not a plain container (Appendix C). Check with ps -o comm= -p 1.

This chapter

Reading
about 50 minutes
Lab
40 minutes, a VM with systemd and sudo, free
Main distribution
Ubuntu 24.04 (systemd 255), with others in 6.8

At Stagedoor

On Monday 2 March 2026, three venues ask Joana the same question: where is last week's payout file? Every night at 03:00, a cron job on door-1 runs the payouts report, which tells the bank how much to transfer to each venue. It hasn't produced anything since Wednesday.

Rafaela runs the job by hand. It works. She checks the crontab: unchanged since 2023. Then she finds root's local mailbox, /var/mail/root, which nobody has ever read: five messages from cron, one a night since Wednesday, each saying payouts-report: command not found. On Tuesday 24 February, Leonor had upgraded the report tool by reinstalling it with pip, which put it in /usr/local/bin instead of /usr/bin. In her shell, /usr/local/bin is on the PATH. In cron's, on Debian 11, it isn't.

The same week, Rafaela has a list from Chapters 1, 4 and 5 that ends in one word: services. The app still runs from a terminal, as root, stopped with signals by a script. Both problems have the same fix.

6.1What systemd does

systemd is PID 1 on almost every Linux server (Chapter 1). It starts everything else, and it keeps doing a job for every service afterwards: a service manager, or supervisor.

Everything systemd manages is a unit, described by a small text file. The unit's type is its file extension: .service (a program), .timer (a schedule), .target (a group of units, like a milestone in the boot), .socket, .mount, .path. Unit files live in three places, and the order matters:

Table 6.1 · Where unit files live
DirectoryWho writes itWins over
/etc/systemd/system/you, the administratoreverything below
/run/systemd/system/programs, at runtime; gone at rebootpackages
/usr/lib/systemd/system/ (also /lib/…)packages; overwritten on upgradenothing

Never edit a file under /usr/lib: the next package upgrade replaces it. Put your own units in /etc/systemd/system/, and change a packaged one with a drop-in (6.4).

6.2A service unit, line by line

Here is the unit Rafaela and Tiago wrote for Stagedoor's app. It's the same one the lab installs, with a stand-in app:

/etc/systemd/system/stagedoor.servicesystemd unit

[Unit]
Description=Stagedoor ticketing app
After=network-online.target
Wants=network-online.target

[Service]
Type=exec
User=stagedoor
Group=stagedoor
UMask=0027
EnvironmentFile=/etc/stagedoor/stagedoor.env
WorkingDirectory=/opt/stagedoor/current
ExecStart=/usr/bin/python3 /opt/stagedoor/current/app.py
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=2
TimeoutStopSec=30

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
Table 6.2 · What each line does
LineMeans
[Unit] Description=the name shown in status and logs
After=network-online.target
Wants=network-online.target		start after the network is configured, and ask for that milestone to be reached (6.5)
Type=execthe service is “started” once the program has been executed successfully. simple (the default) doesn't even wait for that; notify waits for the app to say it's ready; oneshot is for jobs that run and finish
User=, Group=run as this user, never root (Chapter 4)
UMask=0027new files: 640, directories 750 (Chapter 4)
EnvironmentFile=read NAME=value lines into the environment; keeps secrets out of the unit file, which everyone may read
WorkingDirectory=the current directory; through the current symlink (Chapter 3)
ExecStart=the command, with a full path: it's not run through a shell, so no |, > or &&
Restart=on-failure, RestartSec=2restart if it exits with an error or is killed by a signal, after 2 seconds; not after a clean stop
TimeoutStopSec=30on stop, wait 30 s after SIGTERM before SIGKILL (Chapter 5)
[Install] WantedBy=multi-user.targetwhat systemctl enable hooks it into: start it whenever the normal multi-user system starts, at every boot

Notice what isn't there: no nohup, no &, no PID file, no redirection to a log. The app runs in the foreground and writes to standard output; systemd does the rest. That's the right shape for any program you write for a server, and for containers too.

Key idea

A server program should run in the foreground, log to standard output, and exit cleanly on SIGTERM. The service manager handles starting, restarting, stopping and logging.

6.3Driving services with systemctl

Table 6.3 · The systemctl commands you'll use every day
CommandDoes
systemctl status stagedoorstate, main PID, memory, the last log lines
sudo systemctl start / stop / restartnow, this boot only
sudo systemctl reloadask the service to re-read its configuration (for nginx, SIGHUP); only if the unit supports it
sudo systemctl enable / disablestart (or not) at every boot; enable --now also starts it now
sudo systemctl daemon-reloadre-read unit files after you change them
systemctl cat stagedoorthe unit file and all its drop-ins, as systemd sees them
systemctl show stagedoor -p User -p Restartindividual settings, including defaults you didn't write
systemctl list-units --type=service --failedwhat has failed
sudo systemctl reset-failed stagedoorclear a failed state and the start-rate counter
journalctl -u stagedoorthe service's log (Chapter 7); add -f to follow, -b for this boot

Two states are easy to confuse. Enabled means “starts at boot”; active means “running now”. A service can be either without the other, and after a reboot only enabled services come back. On a server you've inherited, compare systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled with what's actually running.

Watch out: restart loops

A service that fails immediately and has Restart=on-failure is restarted, fails, is restarted again: a restart loop. systemd stops it if it's started more than five times in ten seconds (StartLimitBurst=, StartLimitIntervalSec=), and then refuses to start it again with “Start request repeated too quickly” until you run systemctl reset-failed. With a long enough RestartSec=, the limit is never reached and the loop goes on for ever, filling the journal. Watch the restart counter in the log.

6.4Changing a unit: drop-ins

To change one setting of a unit, especially one a package installed, don't copy the whole file. Add a drop-in: a file in /etc/systemd/system/<unit>.d/ ending in .conf, containing just the sections and lines you want to change. sudo systemctl edit nginx opens an editor on /etc/systemd/system/nginx.service.d/override.conf and runs daemon-reload for you; or write the file and reload yourself.

# /etc/systemd/system/nginx.service.d/limits.conf: raise nginx's file-descriptor limit (Chapter 12)
[Service]
LimitNOFILE=65536

Drop-ins survive package upgrades, and systemctl cat shows the original and every drop-in together, so the next person can see exactly what you changed. Most settings in a drop-in replace the original's; list settings such as ExecStart= add to it, so to replace a command you first clear it with an empty line, ExecStart=, then give the new one.

6.5Dependencies and the boot

systemd starts units in parallel wherever it can, so the order has to be stated. Two separate kinds of relationship do that, and mixing them up is the most common unit-file mistake:

Boot milestones are targets. multi-user.target is “the normal server is up” (what WantedBy= usually names); network-online.target is “the network is configured”, which matters for an app that connects to a database on another machine at start-up. systemctl list-dependencies multi-user.target shows the tree, and systemd-analyze blame shows what made the last boot slow.

Even with the right order, a dependency on another machine can't be guaranteed: the database on door-1 might be restarting when door-2 boots. Apps should retry their connections for a while rather than exit on the first failure; Restart=on-failure is a safety net, not a substitute.

6.6Scheduled jobs: cron and timers

cron has run scheduled jobs on Unix since the 1970s, and you'll find it on every server. A line in a crontab (crontab -e for your user; files in /etc/cron.d/ for the system) has five time fields and a command: 0 3 * * * /opt/jobs/payouts.sh means 03:00 every day. It's simple, and it has three traps that all bit Stagedoor at once:

  • A minimal environment. cron doesn't read your shell's startup files. On Debian 11 and older releases, cron's PATH is only /usr/bin:/bin; newer versions, such as Ubuntu 24.04's, inherit a fuller one, which is exactly why a job can work on one server and not on the next. Your ~/.bashrc variables aren't there either.
  • Output goes to mail. Anything a job prints is emailed to the crontab's owner, which on most servers means a local mailbox nobody reads, or nowhere at all. A failing job fails silently.
  • No memory. If the machine was off or rebooting at 03:00, the job simply doesn't run that night. And two runs can overlap if one takes longer than the interval.

A systemd timer is a unit that starts another unit on a schedule. The job itself becomes an ordinary oneshot service, so it gets everything a service gets: a user, an environment, limits, its output in the journal with a timestamp and an exit status, and systemctl start to run it now, exactly as the schedule would.

Table 6.4 · cron and timers compared
cronsystemd timer
Schedule0 3 * * *OnCalendar=*-*-* 03:00:00 Europe/Lisbon
Environmentminimal, varies by versiondefined in the service unit; same every time
Output and failuresmailed, often nowherein the journal; failed state visible in systemctl --failed
Missed runsskippedPersistent=true runs a missed job at the next boot
Overlapping runspossibleimpossible: a running service isn't started twice
Run it now, the same waycopy the command and hope the environment matchessystemctl start payouts.service
Next runwork it outsystemctl list-timers

Watch out: which time zone?

Cloud servers usually run in UTC, and both cron and timers use the machine's time zone unless told otherwise. Portugal is UTC in winter and UTC+1 in summer, so “03:00” on a UTC server is 03:00 in Lisbon for half the year and 04:00 for the other half. Write the zone into the timer (OnCalendar=… Europe/Lisbon) when the time matters to people, and check it with systemd-analyze calendar.

6.7A first look at sandboxing

Because systemd starts the process, it can also take things away from it before it starts. A few lines in the [Service] section shrink what a compromised app could do:

NoNewPrivileges=yes          # can't gain privileges, e.g. through setuid programs (Chapter 4)
ProtectSystem=strict         # the whole filesystem is read-only to it…
ReadWritePaths=/var/lib/stagedoor /var/log/stagedoor   # …except these
ProtectHome=yes              # /home and /root are invisible
PrivateTmp=yes               # its own empty /tmp

systemd-analyze security stagedoor scores a unit and lists what else could be restricted. Chapter 18 hardens Stagedoor's units this way; add these lines one at a time and test after each, because an app that writes somewhere unexpected will fail with “Read-only file system”.

6.8Across distributions

Ubuntu, Debian, the RHEL family and Amazon Linux all use systemd, so units, systemctl and timers are the same. Versions differ (Ubuntu 24.04 has systemd 255, Debian 11 has 247, RHEL 9 has 252), and a few newer settings don't exist on older ones: systemd-analyze verify on the target machine catches that. Package-provided services differ in name: ssh on Ubuntu is sshd on RHEL, cron is crond.

On containers and minimal systems

Containers normally don't run systemd: the container's main process is PID 1, and the container runtime or Kubernetes does the supervising, restarting, stopping and logging (Chapter 19). The ideas carry over directly: run in the foreground, log to standard output, handle SIGTERM. Alpine Linux uses OpenRC instead of systemd, and a few minimal distributions use others; their service files look different, but do the same jobs.

6.9Lab: from terminal to service

Lab 6 · A service, a crash, a stop, and a timer

You'll install a stand-in for Stagedoor's app as a service in the layout from Chapter 3, crash it and watch systemd restart it, stop it gracefully, break it on purpose, reproduce the cron PATH failure, and replace the job with a timer. You need an Ubuntu 24.04 VM with sudo where PID 1 is systemd (Multipass is easiest, Appendix C), plus curl. Cost: nothing. Clean-up: the last step. These outputs came from an Ubuntu 24.04 system container on the test machine, user tiago, host lab; times and PIDs will differ, and on a VM the CGroup: line reads /system.slice/stagedoor.service.

  1. Get the files. In a directory ~/lab6, save the unit from 6.2 as stagedoor.service, and these three files:

    app.pyPython 3.8+

    # app.py · a stand-in for Stagedoor's app: says who it runs as, and stops gracefully on SIGTERM
import http.server, os, pwd, signal, threading

class App(http.server.BaseHTTPRequestHandler):
    def do_GET(self):
        umask = os.umask(0); os.umask(umask)            # read the umask without changing it
        body = (f"stagedoor {os.environ.get('STAGEDOOR_VERSION', '?')} as {pwd.getpwuid(os.getuid()).pw_name}, "
                f"umask {umask:04o}, database {os.environ.get('STAGEDOOR_DB_HOST', 'not set')}\n").encode()
        self.send_response(200)
        self.send_header("Content-Length", str(len(body)))
        self.end_headers()
        self.wfile.write(body)

server = http.server.ThreadingHTTPServer(("127.0.0.1", 8000), App)
server.daemon_threads = False

def graceful(signum, frame):
    print("SIGTERM: finishing requests in progress", flush=True)
    threading.Thread(target=server.shutdown).start()

signal.signal(signal.SIGTERM, graceful)
print(f"listening on 127.0.0.1:8000 as pid {os.getpid()}", flush=True)
server.serve_forever()
server.server_close()
print("stopped cleanly", flush=True)

    stagedoor.envenvironment file

    STAGEDOOR_VERSION=2026.03.1
STAGEDOOR_DB_HOST=10.40.0.11
  2. Install it the way Chapters 3 and 4 laid it out: a system user, a release directory with a current link, configuration readable only by root and the app's group. install copies a file and sets its owner and mode in one step. Then check the unit: 
    sudo useradd --system --user-group --no-create-home --shell /usr/sbin/nologin stagedoor
sudo install -d -o root -g root -m 755 /opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.03.1
sudo install -o root -g root -m 644 app.py /opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.03.1/app.py
sudo ln -s releases/2026.03.1 /opt/stagedoor/current
sudo install -d -o root -g stagedoor -m 750 /etc/stagedoor
sudo install -o root -g stagedoor -m 640 stagedoor.env /etc/stagedoor/stagedoor.env
sudo install -o root -g root -m 644 stagedoor.service /etc/systemd/system/stagedoor.service
systemd-analyze verify /etc/systemd/system/stagedoor.service && echo "unit file OK"
    unit file OK
    The app's code belongs to root, not to stagedoor: the app can read its code but not change it.
  3. Start it, enable it, look at it. 
    sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl enable --now stagedoor
systemctl status stagedoor --no-pager
    ● stagedoor.service - Stagedoor ticketing app
     Loaded: loaded (/etc/systemd/system/stagedoor.service; enabled; preset: enabled)
     Active: active (running) since Fri 2026-11-27 08:23:26 UTC; 5s ago
   Main PID: 230 (python3)
      Tasks: 1 (limit: 19281)
     Memory: 11.0M ()
     CGroup: /docker/16df4c906790d65db95b829c7aaa291ec22255662bc963de5f2dfc74f8b4a196/system.slice/stagedoor.service
             └─230 /usr/bin/python3 /opt/stagedoor/current/app.py
    enabled: it will start at boot. active (running): it's running now. Ask it who it is: 
    curl -s http://127.0.0.1:8000/
    stagedoor 2026.03.1 as stagedoor, umask 0027, database 10.40.0.11
    User, umask and configuration all came from the unit, not from whoever started it.
  4. Crash it. Send SIGKILL to the app through systemd, wait, and read the log: 
    sudo systemctl kill --signal=SIGKILL stagedoor
sleep 3
sudo journalctl -u stagedoor --no-pager -n 6 -o short
    Nov 27 08:23:41 lab systemd[1]: stagedoor.service: Main process exited, code=killed, status=9/KILL
Nov 27 08:23:41 lab systemd[1]: stagedoor.service: Failed with result 'signal'.
Nov 27 08:23:43 lab systemd[1]: stagedoor.service: Scheduled restart job, restart counter is at 1.
Nov 27 08:23:43 lab systemd[1]: Starting stagedoor.service - Stagedoor ticketing app...
Nov 27 08:23:43 lab systemd[1]: Started stagedoor.service - Stagedoor ticketing app.
Nov 27 08:23:43 lab python3[296]: listening on 127.0.0.1:8000 as pid 296
    systemd saw the main process die from signal 9, waited RestartSec, and started a new one: no person, no terminal, no nohup.
  5. Stop it gracefully. 
    sudo systemctl stop stagedoor
sudo journalctl -u stagedoor --no-pager -n 5 -o short
    Nov 27 08:23:51 lab python3[316]: SIGTERM: finishing requests in progress
Nov 27 08:23:51 lab systemd[1]: Stopping stagedoor.service - Stagedoor ticketing app...
Nov 27 08:23:52 lab python3[316]: stopped cleanly
Nov 27 08:23:52 lab systemd[1]: stagedoor.service: Deactivated successfully.
Nov 27 08:23:52 lab systemd[1]: Stopped stagedoor.service - Stagedoor ticketing app.
    systemd sent SIGTERM; the app's handler (from Chapter 5) finished cleanly, well inside the 30 seconds. Start it again: sudo systemctl start stagedoor.
  6. Override one setting with a drop-in, and check what systemd now uses: 
    sudo mkdir -p /etc/systemd/system/stagedoor.service.d
printf "[Service]\nTimeoutStopSec=45\n" | sudo tee /etc/systemd/system/stagedoor.service.d/override.conf > /dev/null
sudo systemctl daemon-reload
systemctl cat stagedoor --no-pager | tail -4
systemctl show stagedoor -p TimeoutStopUSec
    
# /etc/systemd/system/stagedoor.service.d/override.conf
[Service]
TimeoutStopSec=45
TimeoutStopUSec=45s
  7. Break it on purpose: a typo in ExecStart=. Change app.py to ap.py in the unit file, reload, start, and look: 
    sudo sed -i "s|/opt/stagedoor/current/app.py|/opt/stagedoor/current/ap.py|" /etc/systemd/system/stagedoor.service
sudo systemctl daemon-reload; sudo systemctl start stagedoor; sleep 7
systemctl is-active stagedoor; sudo journalctl -u stagedoor --no-pager -n 3 -o cat
    activating
/usr/bin/python3: can't open file '/opt/stagedoor/current/ap.py': [Errno 2] No such file or directory
stagedoor.service: Main process exited, code=exited, status=2/INVALIDARGUMENT
stagedoor.service: Failed with result 'exit-code'.
    activating, because it's between restarts. Leave it for a minute and the counter keeps climbing (sudo journalctl -u stagedoor -o cat | grep "restart counter" | tail -1): 
    stagedoor.service: Scheduled restart job, restart counter is at 13.
    This is the restart loop from 6.3: with RestartSec=2, each attempt is just slow enough not to hit the start limit. Put app.py back (the same sed, the other way round), daemon-reload and sudo systemctl restart stagedoor. On the test machine that restart was refused: 
    Job for stagedoor.service failed because the control process exited with error code.
See "systemctl status stagedoor.service" and "journalctl -xeu stagedoor.service" for details.
    and the status explained why: 
    Nov 27 08:31:31 lab systemd[1]: Stopped stagedoor.service - Stagedoor ticketing app.
Nov 27 08:31:31 lab systemd[1]: stagedoor.service: Start request repeated too quickly.
Nov 27 08:31:31 lab systemd[1]: stagedoor.service: Failed with result 'exit-code'.
Nov 27 08:31:31 lab systemd[1]: Failed to start stagedoor.service - Stagedoor ticketing app.
    The restart, on top of the loop's attempts, crossed the limit of five starts in ten seconds. Clear the counter and start again: 
    sudo systemctl reset-failed stagedoor; sudo systemctl start stagedoor; sleep 1; systemctl is-active stagedoor
    active
  8. The cron job that works by hand. Save the stand-in report tool and the job script:

    payouts-reportbash

    #!/usr/bin/env bash
# payouts-report · stands in for the tool that writes the venues' payout file
echo "$(date '+%F %T') payouts written for 612 venues"

    payouts.shbash

    #!/usr/bin/env bash
# payouts.sh · the nightly job: run the report tool, by name, as it worked by hand
payouts-report
    Install the tool where pip put Leonor's, and run the job by hand: 
    sudo install -m 755 payouts-report /usr/local/bin/payouts-report
sudo install -d /opt/jobs
sudo install -m 755 payouts.sh /opt/jobs/payouts.sh
/opt/jobs/payouts.sh; echo "exit status: $?"
    2026-11-27 08:23:52 payouts written for 612 venues
exit status: 0
    Now schedule it every minute with cron. Ubuntu 24.04's cron would inherit a PATH that includes /usr/local/bin, so to reproduce door-1 (Debian 11), set cron's old default explicitly in the crontab: 
    printf "PATH=/usr/bin:/bin\n* * * * * /opt/jobs/payouts.sh >> /tmp/payouts.log 2>&1\n" | crontab -
crontab -l
    PATH=/usr/bin:/bin
* * * * * /opt/jobs/payouts.sh >> /tmp/payouts.log 2>&1
    After a minute, cat /tmp/payouts.log: 
    /opt/jobs/payouts.sh: line 3: payouts-report: command not found
    Exactly door-1's five nights. (Without >> /tmp/payouts.log 2>&1, that line would have gone to local mail.) Remove the crontab: crontab -r.
  9. The same job as a timer. Save these two units:

    payouts.servicesystemd unit

    [Unit]
Description=Nightly venue payouts report

[Service]
Type=oneshot
User=stagedoor
ExecStart=/opt/jobs/payouts.sh

    payouts.timersystemd unit

    [Unit]
Description=Run the payouts report every night at 03:00 Lisbon time

[Timer]
OnCalendar=*-*-* 03:00:00 Europe/Lisbon
Persistent=true

[Install]
WantedBy=timers.target
    Install them, and run the job now, exactly as the timer will: 
    sudo install -m 644 payouts.service payouts.timer /etc/systemd/system/
sudo systemctl daemon-reload
sudo systemctl start payouts.service
sudo journalctl -u payouts.service --no-pager -n 4 -o short
    Nov 27 08:30:25 lab systemd[1]: Starting payouts.service - Nightly venue payouts report...
Nov 27 08:30:25 lab payouts.sh[465]: 2026-11-27 08:30:25 payouts written for 612 venues
Nov 27 08:30:25 lab systemd[1]: payouts.service: Deactivated successfully.
Nov 27 08:30:25 lab systemd[1]: Finished payouts.service - Nightly venue payouts report.
    It worked, and its output is in the journal with the service's name. (systemd's default PATH for services includes /usr/local/bin; even so, the real fix for a job like this is a full path in the script, so it doesn't depend on anyone's default.) Enable the timer and see when it fires: 
    sudo systemctl enable --now payouts.timer
systemctl list-timers payouts.timer --no-pager
    Created symlink /etc/systemd/system/timers.target.wants/payouts.timer → /etc/systemd/system/payouts.timer.
NEXT                        LEFT LAST PASSED UNIT          ACTIVATES
Sat 2026-11-28 03:00:00 UTC  18h -         - payouts.timer payouts.service

1 timers listed.
Pass --all to see loaded but inactive timers, too.
  10. Check the time zone. The test machine runs in UTC. In November, Lisbon is on UTC too: 
    systemd-analyze calendar --iterations=2 "*-*-* 03:00:00 Europe/Lisbon"
    Normalized form: *-*-* 03:00:00 Europe/Lisbon

    Normalized form: *-*-* 03:00:00 Europe/Lisbon

    sudo systemctl disable --now payouts.timer stagedoor

At Stagedoor: Wednesday 4 March

On door-1, the payouts job becomes payouts.service and payouts.timer, at 03:00 Lisbon time, with Persistent=true and the tool's full path in the script. Rafaela runs it with systemctl start on Monday afternoon; the missing files reach the bank that evening. She also adds an alert for any unit in the failed state, so a job that fails is noticed the same morning rather than after five nights.

On Wednesday 4 March, between on-sales, the app moves on each web server in turn: into /opt/stagedoor/releases/2026.03.1, running as stagedoor under stagedoor.service, with UMask=0027 and its settings in /etc/stagedoor/stagedoor.env. The old hand-started process is stopped with SIGTERM, the new service started, the health check confirmed, then the next server. Once nothing has nohup.out open any more, Tiago deletes it, and this time the space comes back (Chapter 3). The deploy script shrinks to three steps per server: unpack the release, switch current with mv -T, systemctl restart stagedoor.

One thing comes along unchanged: the app's own log file, /var/log/stagedoor/stagedoor.log, still at the debug level Sasha's team switched on in February. Nobody looks at its size. Chapter 7 does.

6.10Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Editing a unit under /usr/lib/systemdThe next package upgrade silently undoes it.A drop-in in /etc/systemd/system/<unit>.d/.
Forgetting daemon-reloadsystemd keeps using the old unit; it warns in status.Reload after every change (or use systemctl edit).
start without enableRuns now, gone after the next reboot.enable --now.
Shell syntax in ExecStart=Pipes, redirections and && are passed as arguments, not interpreted.A script with exec, or ExecStart=/bin/sh -c '…' if you must.
After= without Wants= (or the reverse)No order, or no dependency, and a race at boot.Both, when you mean both (Figure 6.2).
Relying on a default PATH in scheduled jobsWorks by hand, fails at 03:00, silently.Full paths in the job; journal output; alert on failed units.
Schedules without a time zone on a UTC serverJobs move by an hour twice a year.OnCalendar=… Europe/Lisbon, checked with systemd-analyze calendar.

✓Chapter summary

  • systemd starts, supervises, stops, logs and limits services. Everything it manages is a unit.
  • Your units go in /etc/systemd/system/; change packaged ones with drop-ins; daemon-reload after every change.
  • A service unit names the user, environment, working directory, command (full path), restart policy and stop timeout. The app stays in the foreground and logs to standard output.
  • Enabled means it starts at boot; active means it runs now.
  • Wants=/Requires= pull units in; After=/Before= order them. Use both when you need both.
  • A timer runs a oneshot service on a schedule, with a known environment, logs, missed-run catch-up and a time zone; cron has none of these by default.

Key terms

Service manager
The program (systemd on most servers) that starts, supervises, restarts and stops services.
Unit
Anything systemd manages, described by a unit file: a service, timer, target, socket, mount or path.
Unit file
The text file that describes a unit, in sections such as [Unit], [Service] and [Install].
Drop-in
A small .conf file in a unit's .d/ directory that overrides or adds settings.
Target
A unit that groups other units into a milestone, such as multi-user.target.
Enabled
Hooked into a target so that the unit starts automatically at boot.
Restart loop
A service that fails at start and is restarted repeatedly by its restart policy.
Timer
A systemd unit that starts another unit on a schedule or after a delay.
cron
The traditional scheduler that runs commands from crontab files at given times.

Check your understanding

  1. A service is enabled but inactive (dead). What do those two words tell you? recall

    Show answer

    It will be started at the next boot (enabled), but it isn't running now (inactive): someone stopped it, or it finished or failed without a restart policy. systemctl status and journalctl -u show which.

  2. Write a drop-in that makes nginx restart automatically if it crashes, without touching the packaged unit. Where does the file go? apply

    Show answer

    /etc/systemd/system/nginx.service.d/restart.conf containing [Service], Restart=on-failure and, say, RestartSec=2; then sudo systemctl daemon-reload. systemctl cat nginx confirms it's applied.

  3. Explain why a unit with only After=postgresql.service may start before PostgreSQL on one machine and never start PostgreSQL at all on another. explain

    Show answer

    After= only orders: if both are being started in the same transaction, this unit waits. If PostgreSQL isn't enabled, nothing pulls it in, so it never starts. Add Wants= (or Requires=) to pull it in, and keep After= for the order.

  4. After you fix a typo in a unit and run systemctl restart, you get “Start request repeated too quickly”. What happened, and what do you run? debug

    Show answer

    The broken unit had been in a restart loop; with your restart, it crossed the start limit (by default five starts in ten seconds), so systemd refuses further starts. Run sudo systemctl reset-failed <unit>, then start it. Make sure you ran daemon-reload after fixing the file.

  5. Design a timer for a backup that must run once a night at 02:30 Lisbon time, must never run twice at once, and must run after a reboot if the machine was off at 02:30. design

    Show answer

    A backup.service with Type=oneshot, its own user and full paths, and a backup.timer with OnCalendar=*-*-* 02:30:00 Europe/Lisbon and Persistent=true, enabled with systemctl enable --now backup.timer. systemd never starts a service that's already running, so runs can't overlap; check with systemd-analyze calendar and test with systemctl start backup.service.

  6. Compare running an app with nohup … & and as a systemd service, for a reboot, a crash, a deploy and a question about last night's logs. compare

    Show answer

    Reboot: nohup doesn't come back; an enabled service does. Crash: nohup stays dead; the service is restarted. Deploy: with nohup someone signals and restarts by hand; the service gets systemctl restart with SIGTERM and a timeout. Logs: nohup.out grows without timestamps or limits; the journal stores timestamped, per-service logs with retention (Chapter 7).

Going further

  • man systemd.service, man systemd.unit, man systemd.exec (users, environment, sandboxing), man systemd.timer, man systemd.time (calendar syntax).
  • The systemd project's documentation.
  • Ubuntu Server documentation on services and systemd.
  • man 5 crontab on the server you're using: it states that version's environment and PATH.

Part II · Processes and services

7

Logs

Logs are how a server tells you what happened while you weren't looking. This chapter shows where Linux keeps them, how to find the five lines that matter among five million, and how to keep logs from doing the one thing they must never do: fill the disk on the busiest night of the year.

By the end you can

  • Say where a given program's logs go: the journal, a syslog file or its own file.
  • Filter the journal by unit, priority, time and boot.
  • Free space taken by a deleted log that's still open, without restarting anything.
  • Configure logrotate, and choose between create, copytruncate and a signal.
  • Set limits so logs can't fill a disk, and decide what to ship off the machine.

Before you start

  • Chapter 6: services and the journal. Chapter 3: deleted-but-open files.
  • A VM where PID 1 is systemd, with sudo (Appendix C).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, a VM with systemd and sudo, free
Main distribution
Ubuntu 24.04, with others in 7.7

At Stagedoor

Friday 20 March 2026, 10:00: a popular indie band's spring tour goes on sale. At 10:04, checkout starts failing on door-2. The app can't write the temporary file it uses to build each fan's PDF ticket: No space left on device. df -h says the root filesystem is 100% full. door-3 is at 97%.

The culprit takes Tiago thirty seconds to find: /var/log/stagedoor/stagedoor.log, 131 GB. Sasha's team switched the app's log level to DEBUG in February while investigating Chapter 2's checkout errors, and nobody switched it back. Every request writes dozens of lines, and nothing ever rotates or deletes the file. Rafaela deletes it. df still says 100%. Tiago says what they're both thinking: “It's still open.”

7.1Where logs go

A Linux server has three kinds of log, and a given program uses one or more of them. The first question about any log is which one you're looking at.

  • The journal. systemd's logging service, journald, collects the standard output and error of every service, messages sent through the old syslog interface, and the kernel's messages. It stores them in a structured, indexed format in /var/log/journal/, with limits on its own size. You read it with journalctl.
  • Syslog files. Syslog is the traditional Unix logging interface. On Ubuntu, rsyslog receives a copy of everything from journald and writes the familiar text files: /var/log/syslog (everything), /var/log/auth.log (logins, sudo), /var/log/kern.log. Handy for grep and for old tools.
  • Programs' own files. nginx writes /var/log/nginx/access.log and error.log itself; PostgreSQL, some apps and many vendor agents do the same. Nothing limits these files except log rotation (7.4), if somebody set it up.

Every log line carries a priority (also called severity or level), from 0 to 7: emerg, alert, crit, err, warning, notice, info, debug.
