Application frameworks use the same idea (Python's DEBUG, INFO, WARNING, ERROR). The level is the cheapest volume control you have: DEBUG can easily produce a hundred times what INFO does.

7.2Reading the journal

journalctl with no arguments shows everything, oldest first, which is rarely what you want. Filters combine, and a handful cover nearly every question:

Table 7.1 · journalctl filters
You wantCommand
one servicejournalctl -u stagedoor (several -u are allowed)
follow livejournalctl -u stagedoor -f
the last lines, newest lastjournalctl -u stagedoor -n 50; -r for newest first
a time window--since "2026-03-20 09:55" --until "10:10", or --since "-15min"
warnings and worse-p warning; a range: -p err..crit
this boot, the previous one-b, -b -1; --list-boots
the kernel (out-of-memory kills, disk errors)journalctl -k, like dmesg
a program by name or PID-t stagedoor-lab, _PID=2187, _UID=1001
just the messages, or every field-o cat, -o json-pretty, -o verbose
how much space it usesjournalctl --disk-usage

Each journal entry is a set of fields, not just a line of text: who wrote it (_UID, _PID, _SYSTEMD_UNIT), when, on which boot, at which priority. Fields starting with _ are added by journald itself and can't be faked by the program, which makes them trustworthy for questions like “which user ran this?”. Reading other users' and system entries needs sudo, or membership of the adm or systemd-journal group.

Is the journal kept?On Ubuntu, the journal is stored on disk in /var/log/journal and survives reboots. On some minimal images it's kept only in memory, in /run/log/journal, and lost at every reboot. If journalctl --list-boots shows only one boot, check Storage= in journald.conf.

7.3When logs fill the disk

A full filesystem breaks things that seem to have nothing to do with logs: uploads, temporary files, database writes, package installs, even logins on some systems. When it happens, the order of work matters:

  1. Find what's big. df -h shows which filesystem is full; sudo du -xh /var --max-depth=2 | sort -h | tail walks down to the culprit (Chapter 11 does this properly).
  2. Free space without losing what you need. If a running program has the file open, deleting the name frees nothing (Chapter 3). Truncate it instead: : > /var/log/stagedoor/stagedoor.log (or truncate -s 0) sets its length to zero, and the space comes back at once. If you need the contents, copy the last part somewhere else first.
  3. If you already deleted it, find the open file through /proc (ls -l /proc/*/fd 2>/dev/null | grep deleted, or sudo lsof +L1) and truncate it through its descriptor: : > /proc/<pid>/fd/<n>. Or restart the program, which closes it.
  4. Then stop it happening again: rotation, limits, a sensible log level and an alert on disk usage.

Watch out: don't free space by deleting the wrong thing

Under pressure, it's tempting to delete whatever is big. Never delete files under /var/lib (database files, package state) or a database's write-ahead log to make room: that's data, not logs, and the damage outlasts the incident. Logs, caches (/var/cache, apt clean) and old releases are the safe candidates.

7.4Rotation with logrotate

Log rotation means: every so often (or when a file gets too big), move the current log aside, start a new one, compress the older ones, and delete the oldest. On Ubuntu, logrotate does this once a day, started by logrotate.timer, for every file listed in /etc/logrotate.d/. Packages such as nginx install their own entry; for your app's files, you write one.

/etc/logrotate.d/stagedoorlogrotate

/var/log/stagedoor/*.log {
    daily
    rotate 7
    maxsize 100M
    compress
    delaycompress
    missingok
    notifempty
    create 0640 root adm
}

Read it as: for each .log file in that directory, rotate daily or as soon as it passes 100 MB at a daily check; keep seven old files; compress them, except the most recent (so it can still be read easily, and so a program that is still writing to it isn't compressed underneath); don't complain if there's no file; skip empty files; and create the new file as root:adm, mode 640.

The hard part of rotation isn't the schedule. It's the program that has the file open. Renaming a file doesn't change the program's file descriptor (Chapter 3): after stagedoor.log becomes stagedoor.log.1, the program carries on writing to .1, and the new stagedoor.log stays empty for ever. There are three ways out:

nginx's packaged entry, /etc/logrotate.d/nginx, is a good example of the first method:

/var/log/nginx/*.log {
	daily
	missingok
	rotate 14
	compress
	delaycompress
	notifempty
	create 0640 www-data adm
	sharedscripts
	prerotate
		if [ -d /etc/logrotate.d/httpd-prerotate ]; then \
			run-parts /etc/logrotate.d/httpd-prerotate; \
		fi \
	endscript
	postrotate
		invoke-rc.d nginx rotate >/dev/null 2>&1
	endscript
}

invoke-rc.d nginx rotate sends nginx SIGUSR1, which makes it reopen its log files (Chapter 5). sharedscripts runs that once for all the files, rather than once per file.

Test a new entry before you trust it: sudo logrotate --debug /etc/logrotate.d/stagedoor shows what it would do without doing it, and sudo logrotate --force /etc/logrotate.d/stagedoor rotates now. logrotate refuses to work in a directory that others may write to, and says so.

7.5Limits, levels and shipping

Rotation keeps files from growing for ever; three more habits keep logs useful and safe:

  • Cap the journal. By default journald uses at most 10% of the filesystem (and never more than 4 GB) and leaves 15% free. To set your own numbers, add a drop-in such as /etc/systemd/journald.conf.d/size.conf with [Journal], SystemMaxUse=2G and SystemKeepFree=10G, then sudo systemctl restart systemd-journald. sudo journalctl --vacuum-size=1G trims it now.
  • Log at INFO in production, and make the level something you can change at runtime or with a config reload, with a reminder to change it back. Debug logging is a tool for an investigation, not a setting.
  • Ship logs off the machine. A log on the server that crashed, or that an attacker controls, is the log you can't read when you need it. A shipper (Vector, Fluent Bit, the CloudWatch agent, Promtail) sends copies to a central service where they're kept, searchable and alertable. Ship what you'd search; don't ship secrets: check that passwords, tokens and card numbers never reach a log in the first place.

Key idea

Every log needs an owner, a level, a size limit and a destination. A log with none of these is a disk-full incident with a date not yet set.

7.6Writing logs worth reading

When you control the program, make its logs easy for the next person, who may be you at 10:04 on a Friday:

  • One event per line, with a level and the things you'll search by: order ID, event ID, request ID. checkout failed order=88213 event=4500 reason=payment_timeout beats a paragraph.
  • Structured where you can: key-value pairs or JSON lines make shippers and jq work without regular expressions.
  • Timestamps with a time zone, ideally UTC, so lines from three servers can be merged. journald adds its own; files need the program to.
  • Errors with context, once. Log an error where it's handled, not at every level it passes through, or one failure becomes six lines.

7.7Across distributions

Table 7.2 · Logs by distribution
Ubuntu, DebianRHEL familyAmazon Linux 2023
journald on diskyes (Debian 12+ and Ubuntu)yesyes
rsyslog text filesUbuntu: yes, /var/log/syslog, auth.log; Debian 12+: not installed by defaultyes: /var/log/messages, securenot installed by default: use journalctl
logrotateyes, logrotate.timeryesyes

On the cloud

Each provider has a log service and an agent to feed it: CloudWatch Logs with the CloudWatch agent on AWS, Azure Monitor Logs with the Azure Monitor agent, Cloud Logging with the Ops Agent on Google Cloud. They read the journal and files, ship them, and keep them for as long as you pay for. Handbook 7 covers logs, metrics and traces together; for now, know that “ship it off the box” is one agent and a config file away.

7.8Lab: fill a disk, free it, rotate it

Lab 7 · Stagedoor's 20 March, in eight megabytes

You'll write to the journal and filter it, fill a tiny filesystem with a chatty app's log, fail to free it by deleting, free it through /proc, then rotate the log twice: once the way that silently fails, once the way that works. You need an Ubuntu VM with systemd and sudo, Python 3. Cost: nothing. Clean-up: the last step. Outputs come from the same test system as Lab 6 (user tiago, host lab); times, sizes and PIDs will differ slightly.

  1. Write to the journal, then filter. logger sends a message through the syslog interface, with a priority and a tag: 
    logger -p user.warning -t stagedoor-lab "seat map slow: 2.4 s for event 4500"
logger -p user.err -t stagedoor-lab "payment provider timeout after 10 s"
logger -p user.info -t stagedoor-lab "checkout completed for order 88213"
sudo journalctl -t stagedoor-lab --no-pager -o short
    Nov 27 08:35:41 lab stagedoor-lab[915]: seat map slow: 2.4 s for event 4500
Nov 27 08:35:41 lab stagedoor-lab[916]: payment provider timeout after 10 s
Nov 27 08:35:41 lab stagedoor-lab[917]: checkout completed for order 88213
    Only warnings and worse: 
    sudo journalctl -t stagedoor-lab -p warning --no-pager -o short
    Nov 27 08:35:41 lab stagedoor-lab[915]: seat map slow: 2.4 s for event 4500
Nov 27 08:35:41 lab stagedoor-lab[916]: payment provider timeout after 10 s
    And the fields behind one entry: 
    sudo journalctl -t stagedoor-lab -p err -n 1 -o json-pretty --output-fields=PRIORITY,SYSLOG_IDENTIFIER,MESSAGE,_UID,_HOSTNAME --no-pager
    {
	"__REALTIME_TIMESTAMP" : "1795768541581881",
	"_HOSTNAME" : "lab",
	"MESSAGE" : "payment provider timeout after 10 s",
	"SYSLOG_IDENTIFIER" : "stagedoor-lab",
	"__SEQNUM" : "1462",
	"_BOOT_ID" : "3d62d2a080e14ef8bd22f5f52d74f296",
	"_UID" : "1001",
	"__CURSOR" : "s=ba20e08fdc0a4e1f8156e751eccaf79f;i=5b6;b=3d62d2a080e14ef8bd22f5f52d74f296;m=a912d3ec;t=6613df2051239;x=d611942abb387575",
	"__SEQNUM_ID" : "ba20e08fdc0a4e1f8156e751eccaf79f",
	"PRIORITY" : "3",
	"__MONOTONIC_TIMESTAMP" : "2836583404"
}
    Priority 3 is err. _UID (1001 here) was added by journald: the program couldn't have claimed to be someone else. The entries starting with __ are journald's bookkeeping. Check the journal's size and its limits: 
    sudo journalctl --disk-usage
grep -E "^#?(SystemMaxUse|SystemKeepFree|MaxRetentionSec)=" /etc/systemd/journald.conf
    Archived and active journals take up 16.0M in the file system.
#SystemMaxUse=
#SystemKeepFree=
#MaxRetentionSec=
    Commented out means “the default” (7.5).
  2. A small disk for the experiment. Mount an 8 MB in-memory filesystem (tmpfs) over the app's log directory, so you can fill it in seconds without endangering anything: 
    sudo mkdir -p /var/log/stagedoor
sudo mount -t tmpfs -o size=8M,mode=755 tmpfs /var/log/stagedoor
sudo chown "$USER": /var/log/stagedoor
df -h /var/log/stagedoor
    Filesystem      Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
tmpfs           8.0M     0  8.0M   0% /var/log/stagedoor
  3. Start the chatty app. Save it as chatty.py in your home directory; it writes a 1 KB debug line every couple of milliseconds and keeps the file open, like Stagedoor's app:

    chatty.pyPython 3.8+

    # chatty.py · an app that writes a debug line to its log file about every 2 ms, and keeps the file open
import sys, time

log = open(sys.argv[1], "a", buffering=1)          # line-buffered: each line goes to the file at once
n = 0
while True:
    n += 1
    try:
        log.write(f"{time.strftime('%H:%M:%S')} DEBUG checkout step {n}: " + "x" * 960 + "\n")
    except OSError as e:
        print(f"line {n}: write failed: {e}", file=sys.stderr, flush=True)
        time.sleep(1)
    time.sleep(0.002)
    Run it in the background, wait half a minute, and look: 
    python3 chatty.py /var/log/stagedoor/stagedoor.log 2> chatty.err &
sleep 30
df -h /var/log/stagedoor; ls -l /var/log/stagedoor; tail -2 chatty.err
    Filesystem      Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
tmpfs           8.0M  8.0M     0 100% /var/log/stagedoor
total 8192
-rw-r--r-- 1 tiago tiago 8388608 Nov 27 08:37 stagedoor.log
line 8441: write failed: [Errno 28] No space left on device
line 8442: write failed: [Errno 28] No space left on device
    Errno 28 is ENOSPC, “No space left on device”: the same error Stagedoor's checkout hit.
  4. Delete it, as Rafaela did. 
    rm /var/log/stagedoor/stagedoor.log
df -h /var/log/stagedoor
    Filesystem      Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
tmpfs           8.0M  8.0M     0 100% /var/log/stagedoor
    Still full. The name is gone; the file isn't: 
    pid=$(pgrep -f "^python3 chatty.py"); ls -l /proc/$pid/fd | grep deleted
    l-wx------ 1 tiago tiago 64 Nov 27 08:37 3 -> /var/log/stagedoor/stagedoor.log (deleted)
  5. Free it without stopping the app, by truncating the file through the app's own descriptor: 
    : > /proc/$pid/fd/3
df -h /var/log/stagedoor
    Filesystem      Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
tmpfs           8.0M     0  8.0M   0% /var/log/stagedoor
    All 8 MB back at once, and the app carries on (a couple of seconds later the filesystem is filling again: the cause is still there). Stop it with kill $pid and remove any leftover stagedoor.log* files.
  6. Rotate it, the way that fails. Save the logrotate entry from 7.4 as stagedoor-logrotate, start the app again, and force a rotation: 
    python3 chatty.py /var/log/stagedoor/stagedoor.log 2> chatty.err &
sleep 3
sudo install -m 644 stagedoor-logrotate /etc/logrotate.d/stagedoor
sudo logrotate --force /etc/logrotate.d/stagedoor
ls -l /var/log/stagedoor; sleep 2; ls -l /var/log/stagedoor
    total 1392
-rw-r----- 1 root  adm         0 Nov 27 08:37 stagedoor.log
-rw-r--r-- 1 tiago tiago 1423173 Nov 27 08:37 stagedoor.log.1
total 2300
-rw-r----- 1 root  adm         0 Nov 27 08:37 stagedoor.log
-rw-r--r-- 1 tiago tiago 2353437 Nov 27 08:37 stagedoor.log.1
    The new stagedoor.log stays at zero; stagedoor.log.1 keeps growing. The app is still writing through its old descriptor, and this app has no way to be told to reopen. With daily and rotate 7, in a week the growing file would be compressed and eventually deleted while still open: the disk would fill all the same, invisibly. (The new file is also root:adm, so this app couldn't have opened it anyway.)
  7. Rotate it with copytruncate. Stop the app, remove the files, start it again, switch the entry to copytruncate, and rotate: 
    kill %1; rm /var/log/stagedoor/stagedoor.log*
python3 chatty.py /var/log/stagedoor/stagedoor.log 2> chatty.err &
sleep 3
sudo sed -i "s/^    create 0640 root adm/    copytruncate/" /etc/logrotate.d/stagedoor
grep -n copytruncate /etc/logrotate.d/stagedoor
sudo logrotate --force /etc/logrotate.d/stagedoor
ls -l /var/log/stagedoor; sleep 2; ls -l /var/log/stagedoor
    9:    copytruncate
total 1400
-rw-r--r-- 1 tiago tiago    1992 Nov 27 08:37 stagedoor.log
-rw-r--r-- 1 tiago tiago 1427157 Nov 27 08:37 stagedoor.log.1
total 2308
-rw-r--r-- 1 tiago tiago  932256 Nov 27 08:37 stagedoor.log
-rw-r--r-- 1 tiago tiago 1427157 Nov 27 08:37 stagedoor.log.1
    Now .log.1 is a fixed snapshot and stagedoor.log grows from zero: rotation works for a program that knows nothing about it.
  8. Clean up. 
    kill %1
sudo umount /var/log/stagedoor
sudo rm /etc/logrotate.d/stagedoor
rm chatty.py chatty.err stagedoor-logrotate

Stretch goals. (1) Run chatty.py as a transient service instead, sudo systemd-run --unit=chatty -p User=$USER python3 -u -c 'import time; [print("debug", i, flush=True) or time.sleep(0.01) for i in range(10**9)]', and watch journalctl -u chatty -f: no file, nothing to rotate. Stop it with sudo systemctl stop chatty. (2) Use logrotate --debug on /etc/logrotate.d/nginx and read what it would do. (3) Write a drop-in that limits the journal to 200 MB, restart journald, and check journalctl --disk-usage after --vacuum-size.

At Stagedoor: 10:04 to 10:11

Tiago finds the deleted file in /proc while Rafaela watches checkout; one truncation through the app's file descriptor and door-2 has 131 GB free. Checkout recovers at 10:11. They do the same on door-3 before it fills. Seven minutes of failed checkouts, on a tour that sold out in forty.

The fixes, the following week: the app's log level goes back to INFO, and Sasha's team adds a runtime switch for DEBUG that turns itself off after an hour. The app stops writing its own file and logs to standard output, so its lines go to the journal with the unit's name, priority and time; a drop-in caps the journal at 4 GB with 20 GB always kept free. nginx's logs were already rotated by the package. A disk-usage alert at 80% goes to the operations channel. And a new line joins the wiki page of rules: every log has an owner, a level, a size limit and a destination.

7.9Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
An app writing its own file with no rotationIt grows until the disk is full.Log to stdout and the journal, or add a logrotate entry the day the file appears.
Deleting a big open logNo space is freed; the file is now hard to find.Truncate it; or truncate through /proc/<pid>/fd.
Rotating with create for a program that can't reopenThe program writes to the renamed file for ever.copytruncate, or a postrotate signal the program supports.
DEBUG in production, “for now”Volume multiplies; secrets and personal data leak into logs.INFO by default; time-limited debug switches.
Logs only on the serverLost with the server, or altered by whoever broke in.Ship them to a central service.
Reading the journal without sudoYou see only your own entries and think the rest is missing.sudo journalctl, or the adm group.

✓Chapter summary

  • Logs go to the journal (services' output, syslog, the kernel), to syslog files written by rsyslog, or to programs' own files.
  • journalctl filters by unit (-u), priority (-p), time (--since), boot (-b) and field; fields starting with _ are added by journald and trustworthy.
  • When a disk fills with logs: find it, truncate rather than delete, and if it's already deleted, truncate through /proc/<pid>/fd.
  • logrotate rotates files listed in /etc/logrotate.d/. A program holding the file open needs a signal to reopen it, or copytruncate.
  • Cap the journal, log at INFO, alert on disk usage, and ship logs off the machine.

Key terms

journald
systemd's logging service, which collects services' output, syslog messages and kernel messages into the journal.
Journal
journald's indexed, structured store of log entries, read with journalctl.
Syslog
The traditional Unix logging interface and protocol; on Ubuntu, rsyslog writes it to text files in /var/log.
Priority
A log entry's severity, from 0 (emerg) to 7 (debug).
Log rotation
Periodically moving a log aside, starting a new one, compressing and eventually deleting old ones.
copytruncate
A logrotate method that copies a log and truncates the original, so a program can keep writing to the same open file.
Log shipping
Sending copies of logs from a server to a central service where they are stored and searched.

Check your understanding

  1. Where would you look first for: why sudo was denied, what nginx served at 10:02, and why stagedoor.service restarted? recall

    Show answer

    sudo: /var/log/auth.log or journalctl -t sudo. nginx's requests: its own file, /var/log/nginx/access.log. The restart: journalctl -u stagedoor, which has both the app's output and systemd's messages about it.

  2. Write a journalctl command for errors or worse from stagedoor.service between 09:55 and 10:15 on 20 March 2026, newest first. apply

    Show answer

    sudo journalctl -u stagedoor -p err --since "2026-03-20 09:55" --until "2026-03-20 10:15" -r.

  3. Explain why, after a create-style rotation, a program can still be writing to app.log.1, and why this eventually fills the disk anyway. explain

    Show answer

    Renaming changes the directory entry, not the open file: the program's descriptor still refers to the same inode, now named app.log.1. Each later rotation renames and compresses it, and finally deletes it, but the program keeps it open, so its space is never freed and keeps growing until the program reopens or restarts.

  4. df says / is 100% full, but du -xsh / adds up to 40 GB on a 160 GB disk. What's the likely cause, and how do you confirm and fix it without a reboot? debug

    Show answer

    Deleted files still held open: du only counts files with names, df counts all used blocks. Confirm with sudo lsof +L1 or ls -l /proc/*/fd | grep deleted. Fix by truncating through /proc/<pid>/fd/<n>, or by restarting the program holding them, then stop the cause (rotation that the program supports).

  5. Design the logging for a new service on Stagedoor's servers so it can never fill a disk and its logs survive the server. design

    Show answer

    Log to standard output at INFO, one structured line per event, with order and event IDs; run as a systemd service so the journal stores it; cap the journal (SystemMaxUse, SystemKeepFree); ship the journal to a central log service with retention; alert on disk usage above 80%; and make DEBUG a time-limited runtime switch.

  6. Compare copytruncate with a postrotate signal. When would you choose each? compare

    Show answer

    A signal (or systemctl reload) loses nothing and needs no extra disk space, but works only if the program can reopen its log files; use it for nginx, PostgreSQL and other daemons that support it. copytruncate works with any program, but needs space for the copy and can lose lines written between the copy and the truncate; use it for programs you can't change.

Going further

  • man journalctl, man journald.conf, man systemd.journal-fields.
  • man logrotate, especially copytruncate, postrotate and sharedscripts.
  • nginx: Controlling nginx (rotating log files with USR1).
  • Python: the Logging HOWTO.

Part II · Processes and services

8

Packages and updates

Almost every program on a server arrived as a package, and almost every security problem on a server is fixed by a newer one. This chapter explains how packages get from a signed repository onto your disk, how to answer “what is this file, and where did it come from?”, and how to keep servers patched without letting updates restart things at the worst possible moment.

By the end you can

  • Explain what a package, a repository and a signing key are, and how apt and dpkg divide the work.
  • Find which package owns a file, what a package installed, and which version would be installed and from where.
  • Hold a package at its version, and add a third-party repository safely.
  • Configure automatic security updates, and know when an update needs a service restart or a reboot.
  • Plan for a release's end of support.

Before you start

  • Chapter 1: distributions and releases. Chapter 6: services and timers.
  • An Ubuntu machine with sudo and internet access (Appendix C).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
25 minutes, Ubuntu with sudo, free; nothing is upgraded
Main distribution
Ubuntu 24.04, with RHEL-family dnf in 8.7

At Stagedoor

On Tuesday 7 April 2026, Rafaela counts pending updates. door-2 and door-3 have about 140 each, dozens of them security fixes, some from the summer of 2025. Ubuntu installs security updates automatically by default, so somebody switched that off. Leonor remembers: in June 2025, an automatic update of a library restarted the app's Python processes in the middle of an on-sale, and she disabled it that evening. Nobody turned it back on.

door-1 is worse in a different way. It's been running for 500 days without a reboot, so the kernel fixes installed since November 2024 have never been loaded. And its release, Debian 11, stops receiving security updates on 31 August 2026, in less than five months. Rafaela needs a way to patch three servers that a ticketing company can live with, and a plan for the fourth date on the calendar.

8.1Packages, repositories and keys

A package is an archive of files (programs, libraries, configuration, documentation) plus metadata: its name and version, what it depends on, and scripts to run when it's installed or removed. On Ubuntu and Debian packages are .deb files; on the RHEL family, .rpm.

Packages come from a repository: a web server holding thousands of packages and an index that lists them. Ubuntu's repositories are split into pockets per release, and the names show up everywhere in apt's output:

  • noble: the release as it shipped (24.04 is “noble”), never changed afterwards;
  • noble-security: security fixes only;
  • noble-updates: security fixes plus other bug fixes (security fixes appear in both);
  • noble-backports: newer versions of a few programs, off unless you ask for them.

Every repository index is signed. Your machine holds the distribution's public signing key (in /usr/share/keyrings/), and apt refuses an index whose signature doesn't verify. That's what makes it safe to download packages over plain HTTP from a mirror you don't control: the index lists every package's checksum, and the index is signed by Ubuntu. Trusting a repository means trusting whoever holds its key, so add third-party repositories rarely and carefully (8.4).

Two programs share the work. dpkg is the low-level tool: it installs a single .deb file, removes packages and keeps the database of what's installed (/var/lib/dpkg/), but knows nothing about repositories or dependencies it would need to fetch. apt sits on top: it reads repositories, resolves dependencies, downloads, and calls dpkg. Use apt to install and upgrade, dpkg to ask questions.

8.2Everyday apt

Table 8.1 · apt and dpkg, by question
You want toCommand
refresh the indexes (do this first)sudo apt update
see what would be upgradedapt list --upgradable
upgrade, without removing anythingsudo apt upgrade
upgrade, allowing new and removed dependenciessudo apt full-upgrade
try any of these without doing itapt-get -s upgrade (-s: simulate)
install, remove, remove with configsudo apt install nginx, remove, purge
search, describeapt search logrotate, apt show nginx
which version, from whereapt-cache policy nginx
which package owns a filedpkg -S /bin/ss
which files a package installeddpkg -L iproute2
is it installed, which versiondpkg -l 'nginx*'
remove dependencies nobody needs any moresudo apt autoremove

Use apt at the keyboard; in scripts, use apt-get and apt-cache, whose output and options are promised to stay stable (apt warns about this if you pipe it). Install non-interactively with DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive apt-get install -y …, so a package that wants to ask a question doesn't hang your script.

Configuration files surviveWhen an upgrade brings a new version of a configuration file you've edited, dpkg asks what to do, or keeps yours and saves theirs as .dpkg-dist. remove leaves configuration in /etc; purge deletes it.

8.3Versions and holds

apt-cache policy answers the most useful question about any package: what's installed, what would be installed, and from where. Each source has a priority (500 for normal repositories, 100 for “what's already installed”), and apt picks the highest version among the highest-priority sources. A version string like 2.42-4ubuntu2.11 is the upstream version (2.42), then the distribution's revision: Ubuntu fixes security problems by patching the version it shipped, not by jumping to a newer upstream release, so a server's versions look old and are still patched.

Sometimes you need a package to stay exactly where it is for a while: a database whose upgrade you want to schedule, or a library a vendor tested against. Holding it stops apt upgrade from touching it: sudo apt-mark hold postgresql-16, apt-mark showhold, sudo apt-mark unhold. A hold is a promise to come back: write down why and until when, or it becomes a vulnerability nobody remembers choosing.

8.4Third-party repositories

Vendors such as Docker, PostgreSQL (the PGDG repository), nginx and Grafana publish their own repositories with newer versions than the distribution. Adding one is three things: download the vendor's signing key into /etc/apt/keyrings/, add a source file under /etc/apt/sources.list.d/ that names that key with Signed-By:, and apt update. The modern source format (“deb822”) looks like Ubuntu's own:

Types: deb
URIs: https://apt.postgresql.org/pub/repos/apt
Suites: noble-pgdg
Components: main
Signed-By: /etc/apt/keyrings/postgresql.asc

Watch out: a repository is root on your server

Packages run their install scripts as root. Whoever controls a repository you've added can run anything on your machines at the next upgrade. Add only vendors you'd trust with root, tie each key to its own repository with Signed-By: (never add keys to a global keyring, as old instructions with apt-key did), use HTTPS, and remove repositories you no longer need. The same thinking applies to curl … | sudo bash install scripts, which skip the signatures entirely.

8.5Staying patched

Ubuntu ships unattended-upgrades, which installs updates by itself every day. Two systemd timers drive it (Chapter 6): apt-daily.timer refreshes the indexes and apt-daily-upgrade.timer installs. Two files configure it. /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/20auto-upgrades switches it on or off:

APT::Periodic::Update-Package-Lists "1";
APT::Periodic::Unattended-Upgrade "1";

and /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/50unattended-upgrades says what it may install. By default, only the release and its security pocket, not -updates:

Unattended-Upgrade::Allowed-Origins {
	"${distro_id}:${distro_codename}";
	"${distro_id}:${distro_codename}-security";
	// Extended Security Maintenance; doesn't necessarily exist for
	// every release and this system may not have it installed, but if
	// available, the policy for updates is such that unattended-upgrades
	// should also install from here by default.
	"${distro_id}ESMApps:${distro_codename}-apps-security";
	"${distro_id}ESM:${distro_codename}-infra-security";
//Unattended-Upgrade::Remove-Unused-Dependencies "false";
//Unattended-Upgrade::Automatic-Reboot "false";
// when Unattended-Upgrade::Automatic-Reboot is set to true
//Unattended-Upgrade::Automatic-Reboot-WithUsers "true";
//Unattended-Upgrade::Automatic-Reboot-Time "02:00";

(// starts a comment.) Leaving -updates out keeps automatic changes to security fixes, which are small and carefully tested; the larger bug-fix updates are applied in your maintenance window, where you can watch them. Change settings in a file of your own, such as /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/52stagedoor, which wins over 50 because it's read later; package upgrades then leave your choices alone.

Installing a fix is half the job (Figure 8.1). After an update:

  • Services still run the old code until they restart. needrestart, installed on Ubuntu servers, checks after each upgrade which processes use replaced libraries and either lists, asks about or restarts them, depending on $nrconf{restart} ('l' list, 'i' interactive, 'a' automatic) in /etc/needrestart/conf.d/. sudo needrestart -r l lists them on demand.
  • The kernel (and a few core libraries) needs a reboot. Ubuntu creates /var/run/reboot-required and lists the packages in /var/run/reboot-required.pkgs. unattended-upgrades can reboot by itself (Unattended-Upgrade::Automatic-Reboot and …-Time), which suits a fleet of identical servers behind a load balancer and is risky for a single database server.

Key idea

Don't solve “an update restarted something at a bad time” by switching updates off. Separate installing from restarting, and put restarts in a window you control.

8.6Release upgrades and end of support

Within a release, updates are small and safe by design. Moving to the next release (Ubuntu 22.04 to 24.04, Debian 11 to 12) changes major versions of everything at once: Python, OpenSSL, PostgreSQL, configuration formats. You can upgrade in place (do-release-upgrade on Ubuntu), but on a server there's a better way: build a new server on the new release from your notes or scripts, test it, move the work to it, and keep the old one until you're sure. It's slower the first time and much safer, and it's the cattle approach of Chapter 1 in practice. It also tests the thing you most need to know: whether you could rebuild this server at all.

Whichever way, start long before the date. A release past its end of support (Chapter 1, Table 1.1) gets no fixes, and Ubuntu Pro or a paid extended support offer only buys time.

8.7Across distributions

Table 8.2 · apt and dnf side by side
TaskUbuntu, DebianRHEL family, Amazon Linux 2023
refresh, upgradeapt update, apt upgradednf check-update, dnf upgrade (refreshes by itself)
security fixes onlythe -security pocketdnf upgrade --security
owner of a filedpkg -Srpm -qf
files in a packagedpkg -Lrpm -ql
hold a versionapt-mark holddnf versionlock add (a plugin)
automatic updatesunattended-upgradesdnf-automatic and its timer
reboot needed?/var/run/reboot-requireddnf needs-restarting -r
repository files/etc/apt/sources.list.d//etc/yum.repos.d/

On Amazon Linux 2023

Amazon Linux 2023 locks each instance to a dated snapshot of its repository, so two instances launched a month apart may have different package versions, and dnf upgrade alone never moves to a newer snapshot. You move deliberately, with dnf upgrade --releasever=<version>, or by launching from a newer image. It's the update-in-place release model from Chapter 1.

8.8Lab: ask apt questions, change nothing

Lab 8 · Read a server's package state

You'll read the repository configuration, list pending updates, trace a package's versions and a file's owner, hold a package and see the consequence, and inspect the automatic-update setup, all without upgrading anything. You need an Ubuntu 24.04 machine with sudo and internet access. Cost: nothing. Clean-up: step 6 undoes the hold. Your package names and versions will differ: these outputs were captured on the test machine on 27 November 2026.

  1. Where do packages come from? 
    grep -v '^#' /etc/apt/sources.list.d/ubuntu.sources | grep -v '^$'
    Types: deb
URIs: http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu/
Suites: noble noble-updates noble-backports
Components: main universe restricted multiverse
Signed-By: /usr/share/keyrings/ubuntu-archive-keyring.gpg
Types: deb
URIs: http://security.ubuntu.com/ubuntu/
Suites: noble-security
Components: main universe restricted multiverse
Signed-By: /usr/share/keyrings/ubuntu-archive-keyring.gpg
    Two sources: the main archive with three pockets, and the security archive. Both are signed by the same Ubuntu key. (Older systems have /etc/apt/sources.list with one deb … line per pocket instead; same meaning.)
  2. What's waiting? 
    sudo apt update
apt list --upgradable 2>/dev/null | head -4
    Listing...
binutils-common/noble-updates,noble-security 2.42-4ubuntu2.11 amd64 [upgradable from: 2.42-4ubuntu2.10]
binutils-x86-64-linux-gnu/noble-updates,noble-security 2.42-4ubuntu2.11 amd64 [upgradable from: 2.42-4ubuntu2.10]
binutils/noble-updates,noble-security 2.42-4ubuntu2.11 amd64 [upgradable from: 2.42-4ubuntu2.10]
    noble-updates,noble-security: these are security fixes. How many in total, and what would upgrade do? 
    apt-get -s upgrade | grep -E '^[0-9]+ upgraded'
apt-get -s upgrade | grep '^Inst' | head -3
    46 upgraded, 0 newly installed, 0 to remove and 0 not upgraded.
Inst systemd-sysv [255.4-1ubuntu8.18] (255.4-1ubuntu8.19 Ubuntu:24.04/noble-updates [amd64]) []
Inst systemd-dev [255.4-1ubuntu8.18] (255.4-1ubuntu8.19 Ubuntu:24.04/noble-updates [all]) []
Inst libpam-systemd [255.4-1ubuntu8.18] (255.4-1ubuntu8.19 Ubuntu:24.04/noble-updates [amd64]) []
    -s only simulates: nothing was installed. Brackets show the installed version, parentheses the new one and its pocket.
  3. One package's versions. 
    apt-cache policy binutils
    binutils:
  Installed: 2.42-4ubuntu2.10
  Candidate: 2.42-4ubuntu2.11
  Version table:
     2.42-4ubuntu2.11 500
        500 http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu noble-updates/main amd64 Packages
        500 http://security.ubuntu.com/ubuntu noble-security/main amd64 Packages
 *** 2.42-4ubuntu2.10 100
        100 /var/lib/dpkg/status
     2.42-4ubuntu2 500
        500 http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu noble/main amd64 Packages
    *** marks the installed version (priority 100: it comes only from the local status file). The candidate is newer and comes from both -updates and -security at priority 500. The bottom line is the release as shipped.
  4. Who owns this file? Ask about ss, the socket tool from Chapter 1: 
    dpkg -S /usr/bin/ss; dpkg -S /bin/ss
    dpkg-query: no path found matching pattern /usr/bin/ss
iproute2: /bin/ss
    A surprise worth remembering. Since the /usr merge (Chapter 3), /bin is a symlink to /usr/bin, so both paths reach the same file, but iproute2 still registers it under /bin/ss, and dpkg -S matches the registered path literally. If one path finds nothing, try the other. And what else did iproute2 install? 
    dpkg -L iproute2 | grep -E 'bin/' | head -4
    /bin/ip
/bin/ss
/sbin/bridge
/sbin/dcb
  5. Hold a package, and see what that costs. 
    sudo apt-mark hold binutils
apt-mark showhold
apt-get -s upgrade | grep -A1 'kept back'
    binutils set on hold.
binutils
The following packages have been kept back:
  binutils binutils-common binutils-x86-64-linux-gnu libbinutils libctf0
    Holding one package kept back four more that must stay at matching versions: holds spread through dependencies, and so does the security debt.
  6. Undo the hold: 
    sudo apt-mark unhold binutils
    Canceled hold on binutils.
  7. How is this machine kept up to date? 
    cat /etc/apt/apt.conf.d/20auto-upgrades
systemctl list-timers 'apt-daily*' --no-pager | head -3
    APT::Periodic::Update-Package-Lists "1";
APT::Periodic::Unattended-Upgrade "1";
NEXT                        LEFT LAST PASSED UNIT                    ACTIVATES
Sat 2026-11-28 02:56:03 UTC  18h -         - apt-daily.timer         apt-daily.service
Sat 2026-11-28 06:11:00 UTC  21h -         - apt-daily-upgrade.timer apt-daily-upgrade.service
    Switched on, with the two timers from 8.5 (these come from the systemd test system of Lab 6). Then: ls /var/run/reboot-required* (is a reboot pending, and why?) and sudo needrestart -r l (which services run old code?).

Stretch goals. (1) apt show nginx: find its dependencies and its size. (2) zcat /usr/share/doc/openssl/changelog.Debian.gz | head -20: read what the latest security update fixed (the CVE numbers are there). (3) sudo unattended-upgrade --dry-run --debug 2>&1 | tail -20: see what tonight's run would install.

At Stagedoor: a patching policy

Rafaela writes Figure 8.2 into the wiki and sets it up the same week. On door-2 and door-3, unattended-upgrades is switched back on, for security updates only, with $nrconf{restart} = 'l' so nothing restarts by itself and no automatic reboots. Every Tuesday at 05:00 Lisbon time, unless an on-sale is booked that day, a maintenance script takes one web server out of the load balancer, applies all updates, restarts what needrestart lists, reboots if /var/run/reboot-required exists, waits for the health check, and moves to the next. The first run, on 14 April, installs about 140 updates per server and reboots both.

The same morning, with a maintenance notice sent to venues the week before, door-1 is rebooted for the first time in 500 days. PostgreSQL stops cleanly and is back in under a minute, on a kernel with seventeen months of fixes. For Debian 11's end of support, Leonor and Rafaela decide against an in-place upgrade: a new database server on Ubuntu 24.04 will be built from scratch by the end of August, and the data moved to it. (Handbook 6 tells the database side of that move.) It's the first server Stagedoor will build from a written checklist rather than from memory.

8.9Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Switching automatic updates off after one bad restartMonths of unpatched vulnerabilities, unnoticed.Keep security installs automatic; control restarts and reboots.
Installing updates and never restarting or rebootingThe fixes are on disk, not in memory.needrestart and reboot-required in a regular window.
Holds without an end dateA package, and its dependencies, frozen for years.Note why and until when; review holds monthly.
Third-party keys in a global keyringAny added key can sign packages for any repository.One key per repository, via Signed-By:.
curl … | sudo bash installersUnsigned code runs as root; nothing tracks what it installed.The vendor's signed repository, or a package you can inspect.
In-place release upgrades of a pet server, at the last minuteMany major changes at once, no way back.Build a new server on the new release, months ahead.

✓Chapter summary

  • A package is files plus metadata and scripts; a repository serves packages and a signed index. Trusting a repository means trusting its key holder with root.
  • apt fetches, resolves and upgrades; dpkg installs single packages and answers questions (-S, -L, -l).
  • Ubuntu splits each release into pockets: the release, -security, -updates, -backports. Fixes are backported, so versions look old and are patched.
  • unattended-upgrades installs security fixes daily. Installed isn't running: restart services (needrestart) and reboot for kernels, in a window you choose.
  • Replace a release before its end of support, preferably by building a new server.

Key terms

Package
An archive of files with metadata (name, version, dependencies) and install scripts, such as a .deb or .rpm.
Repository
A server that publishes packages and a signed index of them for one or more releases.
Pocket
One part of an Ubuntu release's repository: the release itself, -security, -updates or -backports.
Signing key
The public key a package manager uses to verify that a repository's index was published by its owner.
Hold
A mark that stops the package manager upgrading a package.
unattended-upgrades
Ubuntu and Debian's service that installs updates automatically, by default security updates only.
needrestart
A tool that finds processes still using replaced libraries after an upgrade, and lists or restarts them.

Check your understanding

  1. What does apt update change on the machine, and what does it not? recall

    Show answer

    It downloads and verifies the repositories' indexes, so apt knows which versions exist. It doesn't install or upgrade any package; apt upgrade does that.

  2. A server's openssl is version 3.0.13-0ubuntu3.16 and a scanner says “OpenSSL 3.0.13 is vulnerable to CVE-…, upgrade to 3.0.15”. How do you check whether you're actually affected? apply

    Show answer

    Ubuntu backports fixes into the version it shipped, so the upstream number alone says little. Check the package changelog (zcat /usr/share/doc/openssl/changelog.Debian.gz | grep CVE-…) or Ubuntu's security notice and CVE tracker for that CVE and release. If the fix is listed for your package revision or an earlier one, you're patched; if not, apt-cache policy openssl shows whether a fixed candidate is waiting.

  3. Explain why a server can be fully upgraded and still vulnerable to a bug in a library fixed last week. explain

    Show answer

    Processes that started before the upgrade still have the old library loaded in memory. Until they restart (or, for the kernel, until the machine reboots), the old code is what runs. needrestart and /var/run/reboot-required tell you what's still outstanding.

  4. apt upgrade says “The following packages have been kept back: postgresql-16 postgresql-client-16”. Give two possible reasons and how to tell them apart. debug

    Show answer

    Either a package is held (apt-mark showhold lists it), or the upgrade needs new packages installed or old ones removed, which apt upgrade won't do (apt-get -s full-upgrade shows the extra changes). Phased updates on Ubuntu can also keep packages back for a few days while they roll out gradually.

  5. Design an update policy for two identical web servers behind a load balancer and one database server. design

    Show answer

    Security updates installed daily on all three, with no automatic restarts or reboots. Web servers: in a weekly window, one at a time, out of the balancer, full updates, restarts, reboot if required, health check, back in; they could even reboot automatically, staggered. Database: a separate, announced monthly window (or a replica switchover once one exists), because restarting it is visible to everyone. A calendar of busy days when nothing is changed. A plan to replace each release months before its end of support.

  6. Compare upgrading a server to a new release in place with building a new server on the new release. compare

    Show answer

    In place is quicker and keeps everything, including the unknown hand changes, and is hard to roll back if it fails halfway. Building new takes longer the first time, but tests that you can rebuild the server, lets you compare old and new side by side, and leaves the old server as the rollback. For pets, building new is safer; for servers built from scripts, it's the normal way.

Going further

  • Ubuntu Server documentation: package management and automatic updates.
  • Ubuntu Security Notices and the Ubuntu CVE tracker.
  • man apt, man apt-get, man sources.list, man apt_preferences (pinning), man dpkg.
  • Debian: Instructions to connect to a third-party repository.

Part III · Resources

9

CPU, load and scheduling

“The load is 14!” is one of the most common alarms in operations, and one of the most misread. This chapter explains how Linux shares CPUs between processes, what load average actually counts, how to tell a machine that's busy computing from one that's stuck waiting, and how to tell the scheduler what matters more.

By the end you can

  • Explain what load average measures on Linux, and judge it against the number of CPUs.
  • Read the CPU breakdown in top and vmstat: user, system, idle, I/O wait, steal.
  • Tell CPU saturation from I/O waiting in two commands.
  • Lower a job's CPU and disk priority with nice, ionice and systemd settings.
  • Recognise cloud-specific CPU effects: vCPUs, steal time, burstable instances.

Before you start

  • Chapter 5: process states, especially R and D. Chapter 6: services and timers.
  • A Linux machine with at least 2 CPUs and a few GB of free disk (Appendix C).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
25 minutes, any Linux VM, free
Main distribution
any; the kernel's scheduler is the same everywhere

At Stagedoor

Friday 8 May 2026, 10:00. Tickets for a sold-out arena show's extra date go on sale, and at 10:02 the new monitoring pages Rafaela: door-1 load average 14.2. door-1 has 8 vCPUs. Checkout is slow; seat holds take three seconds instead of a fraction of one. In the team chat, Leonor writes what most people would: “The database server is overloaded. Can Atlantica give us a bigger one today?”

Rafaela opens top before answering. The CPUs are 30% busy. Most of the rest of the time, they're waiting.

9.1CPUs, cores and vCPUs

To Linux, a CPU is anything that can run one thread of instructions at a time. nproc counts them; lscpu describes them.

On physical hardware, a processor has several cores, and each core may run two hardware threads (Intel calls this Hyper-Threading, AMD SMT). Two threads on one core share its execution units, so they're worth less than two cores: perhaps 1.2 to 1.3 times one core for typical server work. Linux counts each hardware thread as a CPU.

In the cloud, you rent vCPUs. On most instance types one vCPU is one hardware thread of a physical core shared with other customers' VMs; on some, two vCPUs are the two threads of one dedicated core. Either way, nproc on an “8 vCPU” server says 8, and that's the number to compare load against. The lab machine for this chapter:

CPU(s):                                  4
Model name:                              Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz
Thread(s) per core:                      1
Core(s) per socket:                      4
Socket(s):                               1

9.2How the scheduler shares CPUs

At any moment, most processes are sleeping (S, Chapter 5): waiting for a network packet, a timer, a lock. The ones that could use a CPU right now are runnable (R). If there are more runnable threads than CPUs, the extras wait in a run queue, and the kernel's scheduler switches between them every few milliseconds so each gets a fair share. Each switch is a context switch; it costs a few microseconds, which is nothing until a machine does hundreds of thousands a second.

A third group neither runs nor waits for a CPU: threads in uninterruptible sleep (D), waiting inside the kernel for disk I/O (or a network filesystem) to finish. They can't use a CPU even if one is free; they're waiting for the disk.

9.3Load average

The load average is the three numbers at the start of uptime, top and /proc/loadavg: the average number of threads that were running, runnable or in uninterruptible sleep, over roughly the last 1, 5 and 15 minutes. They're exponentially smoothed, so a sudden change shows up gradually: in the lab, eight busy processes take more than a minute to push the 1-minute figure near 8.

Three rules make the numbers useful:

  1. Divide by the number of CPUs. A load of 8 is idle-ish on a 64-CPU machine and badly overloaded on a 2-CPU one. Below the CPU count, there's usually spare capacity; persistently above it, something is waiting.
  2. Compare the three numbers. 1-minute higher than 15-minute: getting worse. Lower: recovering. The trend matters more than the value.
  3. Find out what kind of waiting. On Linux (unlike most other Unix systems), load includes D-state threads. Look at the CPU breakdown (9.4) and the r and b columns of vmstat before deciding a machine needs more CPU.

Key idea

Load average says that things are waiting, not what they're waiting for. Before buying CPUs, check whether the waiting is for CPUs.

9.4Where the CPU time goes

The kernel accounts for every tick of every CPU in one of a few categories. top shows them on its %Cpu(s) line, and vmstat in its last columns:

Table 9.1 · CPU time categories
topvmstatMeansHigh usually means
usususer: running programs' own codeyour app or database is computing
sysysystem: running the kernel on programs' behalflots of system calls, network or disk work, context switches
ni(in us)user time of processes with a positive nice valuebackground jobs you deprioritised (9.5)
idididle, nothing to dospare capacity
wawaidle, but some thread is waiting for I/Othe disk is the bottleneck; look at b and Chapter 11
hi, si(in sy)handling hardware and software interruptsvery high packet rates
stststeal: the hypervisor ran someone else on your vCPUa busy host, or a burstable instance out of credits (9.6)

Two more columns of vmstat 1 complete the picture: r, the number of runnable threads (running plus queued), and b, the number blocked in D state. With these, the first question about a loaded machine takes one command:

  • r well above the CPU count, us + sy near 100: CPU saturation. Something is computing too much, or there's too little CPU.
  • b high, wa high, id non-zero: waiting for I/O. More CPUs won't help; find what's using the disk.

To find which process: top sorted by CPU (press P), ps -eo pid,stat,pcpu,comm --sort=-pcpu | head, and for D states ps -eo pid,stat,wchan:20,comm | awk '$2 ~ /D/' (the wchan column names the kernel function it's waiting in). The sysstat package adds mpstat -P ALL 1 (per CPU, to spot one CPU at 100% while the rest idle: a single-threaded bottleneck), pidstat 1 (per process, over time) and iostat -x 1 (per disk).

9.5Priorities: nice, ionice and systemd

The scheduler gives runnable threads CPU time in proportion to a weight, set by the process's nice value, from −20 (most favoured) to 19 (least). The default is 0. Each step changes the weight by about 25%, so the gaps are large: a nice-19 process competing for one CPU with a nice-0 one gets around 1.5% of it, as the lab shows. Nice only matters when CPUs are contended; on an idle machine, a nice-19 job runs at full speed.

  • nice -n 10 cmd starts a command with a lower priority; renice -n 10 -p 2187 changes a running one. Only root may raise priority (negative values).
  • ionice -c 3 cmd puts a process in the idle I/O class: it gets the disk only when nobody else wants it. -c 2 -n 7 is best-effort at the lowest priority. The effect depends on the disk's I/O scheduler, and is strongest on local disks.
  • For services and timers, put it in the unit: Nice=10, IOSchedulingClass=idle, or with cgroups, CPUWeight=20 (default 100) and CPUQuota=50% (a hard cap: half of one CPU).

Lowering the priority of what can wait is almost always better than raising the priority of what can't: a database at nice −10 can starve the very processes it depends on, such as its own log writer or sshd when you need to log in and fix it.

9.6CPU on a cloud VM

Three cloud effects appear in the numbers above:

  • Steal time (st) is time your vCPU was ready to run but the hypervisor ran another VM on that physical CPU. A few percent is normal on shared instances (the lab machine showed 1–3%); sustained double digits means you're not getting what you pay for, and moving to another host (stop and start the instance) or a different instance family helps.
  • Burstable instances (AWS T family, Azure B series, Google Cloud's shared-core types) give you a baseline, say 20% of a vCPU, plus credits saved while idle. When the credits run out, the vCPU is throttled to the baseline, often visible as high steal. They're good for small, spiky servers and a trap for anything with sustained load, like an on-sale.
  • vCPU counts are threads, not cores (9.1). A CPU-bound app that scales to 8 cores may not scale to 8 vCPUs on 4 cores.

9.7Across distributions

The scheduler, load average and /proc are the kernel's, identical everywhere. top, vmstat, ps and nice come from procps on every distribution in this book. mpstat, pidstat and iostat come from sysstat, which isn't installed by default on Ubuntu, Debian or Amazon Linux (apt install sysstat, dnf install sysstat); it also records history every ten minutes for sar once enabled, which is very useful for “what happened at 03:00?”.

On the cloud

Cloud consoles graph “CPU utilisation” from outside the VM, as busy time over all vCPUs. It doesn't show I/O wait or load, and it ignores steal from the VM's point of view, so a VM can look 30% busy in the console while its users wait. The provider's agent (CloudWatch agent, Azure Monitor agent, Ops Agent) can send the in-guest numbers too.

9.8Lab: busy, nice and blocked

Lab 9 · Three kinds of load

You'll overload the CPUs and watch the load climb, share one CPU between a nice and a not-nice process, then create load with the CPUs nearly idle by making processes wait for the disk. You need a Linux VM (not a laptop you're using for other things) with 2 or more CPUs and 2 GB of free disk. Cost: nothing. Clean-up: every step stops itself with timeout; delete the files at the end. Outputs come from a 4-vCPU test VM.

  1. A CPU burner. In ~/lab9, save this as burn.sh and chmod +x burn.sh:

    burn.shsh

    #!/bin/sh
# burn.sh · keep one CPU busy, doing nothing useful, until stopped
while :; do :; done
  2. Twice as many burners as CPUs. Start eight (on a 4-CPU machine; use twice nproc), each stopped after 100 seconds, wait 75 seconds, and look: 
    for i in 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8; do timeout 100 ./burn.sh & done
sleep 75
cat /proc/loadavg; vmstat 1 3; ps -o stat=,comm= -u "$USER" | sort | uniq -c
    6.78 3.54 1.47 10/171 12134
procs -----------memory---------- ---swap-- -----io---- -system-- -------cpu-------
 r  b   swpd   free   buff  cache   si   so    bi    bo   in   cs us sy id wa st gu
 8  0      0 15356720   5036 705048    0    0   826   460  478    1  6  0 93  0  0  0
 8  0      0 15356720   5036 705052    0    0     0     0 1037 1091 99  0  0  0  1  0
 8  0      0 15356720   5036 705052    0    0     0     0 1037 1123 99  0  0  0  1  0
      8 R    burn.sh
      1 R    ps
      1 S    bash
      1 S    sort
      8 S    timeout
      1 S    uniq
    The 1-minute load (6.78) is still climbing towards 8 after 75 seconds: smoothing. r is 8 (four running, four queued), us is 99 and id 0: CPU saturation. The first vmstat line is an average since boot; ignore it. And st 1: a little steal from the host.
  3. Nice in action. Pin two burners to the same CPU with taskset, one at the default priority and one at nice 19: 
    taskset -c 0 timeout 40 ./burn.sh &
taskset -c 0 nice -n 19 timeout 40 ./burn.sh &
sleep 20; ps -o pid,ni,psr,pcpu,comm -C burn.sh
      PID  NI PSR %CPU COMMAND
12155   0   0 96.8 burn.sh
12156  19   0  1.4 burn.sh
    Both on CPU 0 (PSR); the nice-19 one got about 1.5% of it. Run the nice one alone and it gets 100%: nice only matters under contention.
  4. Load without CPU. Three writers that bypass the page cache (oflag=direct, so every write waits for the disk): 
    for i in 1 2 3; do dd if=/dev/zero of=big$i bs=1M count=3000 oflag=direct status=none & done
sleep 5; vmstat 1 4; ps -o pid,stat,wchan:20,comm -C dd
    procs -----------memory---------- ---swap-- -----io---- -system-- -------cpu-------
 r  b   swpd   free   buff  cache   si   so    bi    bo   in   cs us sy id wa st gu
 0  3      0 15352800   5192 705672    0    0   809  1676  484    1  7  0 92  0  0  0
 1  2      0 15353680   5216 705728    0    0    24 819200 2489 3516  0  5 53 42  1  0
 1  2      0 15354896   5248 705824    0    0    32 947200 2844 4044  0  7 47 45  0  0
 0  3      0 15355548   5276 705880    0    0    28 1073152 3016 4518  0  7 48 46  0  0
  PID STAT WCHAN                COMMAND
12173 D    iomap_dio_rw         dd
12174 D    iomap_dio_rw         dd
12175 D    iomap_dio_rw         dd
    r is 0 or 1, b is 2 or 3, wa about 45 and id about 50: the CPUs are half idle and half “waiting for I/O”. The writers are in D, waiting in the kernel's direct-I/O path (wchan). Keep four writers going for a minute and read the load: 
    for i in 1 2 3 4; do timeout 75 sh -c "while :; do dd if=/dev/zero of=big$i bs=1M count=500 oflag=direct status=none; done" & done
sleep 65; cat /proc/loadavg; vmstat 1 2 | tail -1
    4.05 3.65 1.84 1/167 12303
 0  4      0 15358080   5768 707144    0    0     0 643072 2577 3166  0  6 23 71  0  0
    A load of 4 on a 4-CPU machine whose CPUs are 6% busy: exactly the reading that makes people order bigger servers. Delete the files: rm big1 big2 big3 big4.

Stretch goals. (1) Install sysstat and run step 2 again with mpstat -P ALL 1 3: every CPU at 100%. Then run a single burner: one CPU at 100%, the others idle, a load of 1. (2) Repeat step 4 with ionice -c 3 on the writers while a fourth, normal dd runs: does the normal one get more of the disk? (3) Run a burner as a transient service with a cap, sudo systemd-run --unit=capped -p CPUQuota=25% ./burn.sh (from the lab directory, with a full path), and watch it in top: 25%, however idle the machine.

At Stagedoor: the backup at the wrong time

vmstat 1 on door-1 shows r between 2 and 4, b around 10, and wa above 50. The processes in D are PostgreSQL's, plus pg_dump and gzip: a full backup is running. It's the new weekly full backup, added in April as a timer with OnCalendar=Fri 08:00, meant to finish long before any on-sale. door-1's clock is UTC; since the clocks changed in March, 08:00 UTC is 09:00 in Lisbon, and on an arena on-sale, with the database busy, the backup takes eighty minutes instead of forty. Exactly Chapter 6's warning about time zones.

Rafaela stops the backup at 10:05 (it will run again that night), and seat holds are fast again within a minute. Nobody buys a bigger server. The timer moves to OnCalendar=Sun 04:00 Europe/Lisbon, with Nice=10 and IOSchedulingClass=idle. The alert on load average is replaced by two: CPU busy (us + sy) above 80% for ten minutes, and I/O wait above 30%, each naming the machine and showing the top processes. Handbook 6 returns to this backup: taking it from a replica instead of the busy primary is the real fix.

9.9Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Reading load without the CPU countPanic over 8 on a 64-CPU machine; calm over 4 on a single CPU.Load ÷ nproc, and the trend of the three numbers.
Treating high load as “needs more CPU”A bigger server, the same slowness: the disk was the bottleneck.Check wa and b in vmstat first.
Trusting the first line of vmstatAverages since boot hide what's happening now.vmstat 1 and read from the second line.
Raising the priority of the important serviceIt starves helpers it depends on, even sshd.Lower the priority of what can wait.
Heavy batch jobs at the busiest timeUsers wait while a backup runs.Schedule in quiet hours with a time zone; Nice= and IOSchedulingClass=idle.
Burstable instances for sustained loadThrottled to the baseline when credits run out, mid-event.Fixed-performance instances for busy servers; watch the credit balance.

✓Chapter summary

  • Linux counts every hardware thread, and every cloud vCPU, as a CPU; nproc gives the number to compare against.
  • The scheduler shares CPUs between runnable threads; extras wait in the run queue.
  • Load average is the smoothed number of threads running, waiting for a CPU, or in D state waiting for the disk, over 1, 5 and 15 minutes.
  • CPU time splits into user, system, nice, idle, I/O wait, interrupts and steal. r and us high: CPU saturation. b and wa high: waiting for I/O.
  • nice, ionice and systemd's Nice=, IOSchedulingClass=, CPUWeight= and CPUQuota= decide who gets contended resources.
  • In the cloud, watch for steal time and burstable instances that run out of credits.

Key terms

vCPU
A virtual CPU rented in the cloud, usually one hardware thread of a physical core.
Scheduler
The part of the kernel that decides which runnable thread runs on which CPU, and for how long.
Run queue
The runnable threads waiting for a CPU.
Context switch
The CPU switching from running one thread to running another.
Load average
The average number of threads running, runnable or in uninterruptible sleep over the last 1, 5 and 15 minutes.
I/O wait
CPU time that was idle while at least one thread was waiting for I/O to complete.
Steal time
Time a virtual CPU was ready to run but the hypervisor was running something else.
Nice value
A process's scheduling priority, from −20 (most favoured) to 19 (least); 0 by default.

Check your understanding

  1. A 4-vCPU server shows a load average of 3.1 5.6 7.9. What do the three numbers tell you? recall

    Show answer

    Over the last 15 minutes the load averaged about 7.9, almost twice the CPU count; over the last 5 minutes 5.6, and over the last minute 3.1, now below the CPU count. Something was overloaded and is recovering.

  2. vmstat 1 shows r 1 b 9 us 4 sy 3 id 41 wa 52 on an 8-vCPU database server. What's the bottleneck, and what would you look at next? apply

    Show answer

    The disk: nine threads blocked in I/O, half the CPU time spent waiting for I/O, CPUs mostly idle. Next: ps -eo pid,stat,wchan:20,comm | awk '$2 ~ /D/' for which processes, iostat -x 1 or pidstat -d 1 for which disk and who's using it (Chapter 11).

  3. Explain why a nice-19 backup job still runs at full speed on an idle server, and why that can still hurt the database at night. explain

    Show answer

    Nice only changes the share of CPU when CPUs are contended; with free CPUs, the job gets as much as it wants. And nice affects CPU, not the disk: a backup's I/O can still slow the database. Add ionice -c 3 or IOSchedulingClass=idle, and schedule it when the database is quiet.

  4. A web server's console graph shows 35% CPU, but its users report slowness and top inside the VM shows st at 40%. What's happening? debug

    Show answer

    The hypervisor isn't giving the VM the CPU time it wants: either a noisy neighbour on a shared host, or (very often) a burstable instance that has run out of CPU credits and is throttled to its baseline. Check the credit balance; move to a fixed-performance instance type, or stop and start to land on another host.

  5. Design monitoring alerts for CPU on Stagedoor's servers that would have pointed at the real problem on 8 May. design

    Show answer

    Alert on CPU busy (user + system) above about 80% for several minutes, per server, and separately on I/O wait above about 30%, each with the top processes by CPU and the processes in D attached. Graph load per CPU for context rather than alerting on it. Add steal time for cloud VMs.

  6. Compare Nice=10 and CPUQuota=50% for a reporting service. compare

    Show answer

    Nice=10 lowers its share only when CPUs are contended; when the machine is idle it can use every CPU. CPUQuota=50% is a hard ceiling of half a CPU, even on an idle machine. Use nice (or CPUWeight=) to be polite; use a quota when the job must never take more than a fixed amount.

Going further

  • Brendan Gregg, Linux Load Averages: Solving the Mystery.
  • man 5 proc (/proc/loadavg, /proc/stat), man vmstat, man 7 sched, man ionice.
  • man systemd.resource-control (CPUWeight=, CPUQuota=, IOWeight=).
  • AWS: Burstable performance instances.

Part III · Resources

10

Memory, swap and the OOM killer

Linux uses memory differently from how most people expect: it fills “free” memory with cache on purpose, promises programs more than it has, and, when the promises can't all be kept, picks a process and kills it. This chapter explains each of those, how to read memory numbers without alarm, and how to make sure that when something must die, it isn't your database.

By the end you can

  • Read free correctly, and say why “available” matters more than “free”.
  • Tell virtual size from resident memory, and find the processes really using memory.
  • Explain what swap is for, and when a server should have it.
  • Explain how the OOM killer chooses a victim, find its traces, and protect a process from it.
  • Contain a memory-hungry job with a cgroup limit.

Before you start

  • Chapter 5: processes, signals, exit status 137. Chapter 6: units and systemd-run.
  • A Linux VM with systemd, sudo, Python 3 and 3 GB of free disk (Appendix C).

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
25 minutes, a VM with systemd, free
Main distribution
any; memory management is the kernel's

At Stagedoor

Friday 5 June 2026, 10:12, twelve minutes into a summer festival's on-sale: for forty seconds, every request to the database fails, on both web servers at once. Then everything recovers. PostgreSQL's log on door-1 tells the story in three lines: one server process “was terminated by signal 9: Killed”; the database then “terminating any other active server processes”; then “database system was not properly shut down; automatic recovery in progress”.

Nobody sent signal 9. The kernel did. journalctl -k has the matching line: Out of memory: Killed process 31877 (postgres), with 19 GB of resident memory. One PostgreSQL process, on a 32 GB machine, had grown to 19 GB. Rafaela and Sasha set out to find out which query it was running, and why the kernel chose it.

10.1Virtual memory

Every process sees its own private, contiguous range of memory addresses, its virtual address space. The kernel maps pieces of it, in pages of 4 KB, onto real memory (RAM) only when the process actually touches them.

This explains the two memory columns in ps and top:

  • VSZ (virtual size, VIRT in top): everything mapped, used or not. It's often huge and almost meaningless: a Java or Go program can map gigabytes it never touches.
  • RSS (resident set size, RES): pages actually in RAM. This is the number to watch, with one caveat: shared pages (libraries, a database's shared buffers) are counted in the RSS of every process that uses them, so adding up RSS over-counts. smem or /proc/<pid>/smaps_rollup gives PSS, which divides shared pages fairly.

Because pages are handed out only when touched, the kernel lets programs reserve more memory in total than the machine has. This is overcommit, and it's usually right: most reservations are never fully used. When they are, the kernel has a problem it can only solve by taking memory back from someone (10.4).

10.2Reading free

               total        used        free      shared  buff/cache   available
Mem:            15Gi       673Mi        14Gi        12Mi       739Mi        15Gi
Swap:             0B          0B          0B
Table 10.1 · The columns of free
ColumnMeans
usedmemory programs and the kernel are using, not counting cache
freememory nobody is using for anything, not even cache
buff/cachethe page cache and kernel buffers: copies of files, kept in case they're read again
availablean estimate of how much could be given to programs right now without swapping: free plus cache that can be dropped

Linux uses otherwise idle RAM to cache files, because reading from RAM is thousands of times faster than from disk, and gives that memory back the moment a program needs it. On a healthy server that has been up for a while, free is small and buff/cache is large, and that's a good sign, not a warning. The lab reads a 2 GB file and watches buff/cache grow by 2 GB while available doesn't move.

Key idea

Watch available, not free. Free memory is memory doing nothing; cache is memory doing something useful until it's needed elsewhere.

10.3Swap

Swap is disk space the kernel can move memory pages to when RAM is short: a swap partition, or a swap file. Pages of program memory that haven't been used for a while are written out (“swapped out”) and read back when touched again. It isn't a substitute for RAM, since disk is far slower, but it does two useful things: it lets the kernel move genuinely idle memory out of the way to make room for cache and active work, and it turns a sudden memory shortage into a gradual slowdown that a person or alert can notice, instead of an instant kill.

free shows swap's size and use; vmstat 1's si and so columns show pages swapped in and out each second. Swap used isn't a problem; continuous swapping in and out (“thrashing”) is, and makes a machine crawl. vm.swappiness (0–200, default 60) tunes how readily the kernel swaps program memory rather than dropping cache.

Swap on cloud VMsMost cloud images, including Ubuntu's, ship with no swap at all, as did the lab machine. Adding a swap file is a few commands (Chapter 11); whether to is a judgement: for a database server, a small amount buys warning time; for a container host, many teams prefer no swap and strict limits.

10.4When memory runs out

When a program touches a page and the kernel can't find RAM for it, the kernel works down a ladder:

The OOM killer (out of memory) chooses by a score, /proc/<pid>/oom_score: mostly how much memory the process uses, so the biggest process is usually the victim. Each process also has /proc/<pid>/oom_score_adj, from −1000 to +1000, added to its score; −1000 exempts it entirely. In a unit file it's OOMScoreAdjust=. The victim gets SIGKILL (exit status 137, Chapter 5), and the kernel log records it: journalctl -k | grep -i "out of memory" or dmesg, with the process's memory use at the moment of death.

Watch out: the biggest process is often the most important one

On a database server, the database is the biggest process by far, so it's the OOM killer's favourite target, even when a careless report or a leaky script caused the shortage. For PostgreSQL, killing even one backend with SIGKILL makes the whole server restart all connections and run crash recovery, because shared memory might be corrupt: a few seconds to minutes of outage for everyone. Protect the database's main process, cap what other jobs on the machine may use, and keep memory headroom.

10.5Limits with cgroups

A cgroup (control group) is a group of processes that the kernel accounts and limits together. systemd puts every service in its own cgroup (Chapter 6), so memory limits are one line in a unit:

  • MemoryMax=2G: a hard limit. If the service's processes need more and nothing can be reclaimed within the cgroup, the OOM killer kills one of them, not something else on the machine.
  • MemoryHigh=1.5G: a soft limit. Above it, the kernel reclaims the cgroup's memory aggressively and slows it down, which is often enough to keep it from reaching MemoryMax.
  • MemorySwapMax=0: no swap for this service.

For a one-off job, sudo systemd-run --unit=report -p MemoryMax=4G -p User=$USER command runs it in its own limited cgroup, which is what the lab does. Containers use the same mechanism: docker run --memory=2g, and Kubernetes' memory limits (Handbook 4), so a container killed with 137 and OOMKilled: true hit its own limit, not the machine's.

10.6Who is using the memory?

ps -eo pid,rss,vsz,comm --sort=-rss | head        # biggest resident first (KB)
top                                               # press M to sort by memory
grep -E 'VmRSS|VmSwap' /proc/2187/status          # one process: resident and swapped
systemd-cgtop                                     # memory per service (cgroup), live
grep -E 'MemAvailable|Dirty|Shmem' /proc/meminfo  # the whole machine, in detail

Two traps when the numbers don't add up: shared memory is counted in every process that maps it (10.1), and memory used by the kernel itself (slab caches, page tables) appears in no process at all; /proc/meminfo and slabtop show it.

10.7Across distributions

Memory management, the OOM killer and cgroups are the kernel's, so they behave the same everywhere. What differs is the plumbing around them:

On newer systems: systemd-oomd

Ubuntu Desktop (since 22.04) and Fedora run systemd-oomd, a user-space killer that acts earlier than the kernel, when memory pressure (time spent waiting for memory, from /proc/pressure/memory) stays high, and kills a whole cgroup rather than one process. It's configured per slice with ManagedOOMMemoryPressure=; on servers it's often not installed or not active, so check systemctl status systemd-oomd. If something was killed and the kernel log has no OOM line, check journalctl -u systemd-oomd. RHEL and Amazon Linux don't enable it by default.

10.8Lab: cache, residence and a kill

Lab 10 · Watch memory being cached, reserved and taken back

You'll see the page cache fill without hurting available, compare a process's virtual and resident size, change its OOM score, then run a memory hog inside a 200 MB limit and find the kernel's record of killing it. You need a Linux VM with systemd, sudo, Python 3 and 3 GB of free disk. Cost: nothing. Clean-up: delete big.bin and hog.py. Steps 1–3 ran on a 16 GB test VM, step 4 on the systemd test system of Lab 6.

  1. The page cache. Make a 2 GB file, empty the cache (harmless: the kernel just forgets cached copies), then read the file and compare: 
    mkdir -p ~/lab10 && cd ~/lab10
head -c 2G /dev/urandom > big.bin
sync; echo 3 | sudo tee /proc/sys/vm/drop_caches > /dev/null
free -h | head -2; cat big.bin > /dev/null; free -h | head -2
                   total        used        free      shared  buff/cache   available
Mem:            15Gi       616Mi        15Gi        12Mi       319Mi        15Gi
               total        used        free      shared  buff/cache   available
Mem:            15Gi       618Mi        13Gi        12Mi       2.3Gi        15Gi
    buff/cache grew by 2 GB and free shrank by 2 GB, but available stayed at 15 GiB: that cache can be given back at any time. Read the file again and time it (time cat big.bin > /dev/null): from cache, it's much faster than the first read. Never use drop_caches on a production server to “free memory”; you'd only make the next reads slow.
  2. Virtual and resident. Start a Python process that maps 4 GB without touching it and fills 300 MB, and look at it: 
    python3 -c "import mmap, time; m = mmap.mmap(-1, 4 << 30); b = bytearray(300 << 20); time.sleep(20)" &
sleep 2; ps -o pid,vsz,rss,comm -p $!; grep -E "VmSize|VmRSS" /proc/$!/status
      PID    VSZ   RSS COMMAND
12866 4537064 317596 python3
VmSize:	 4537064 kB
VmRSS:	  317596 kB
    4.3 GB virtual, 310 MB resident. The untouched 4 GB costs nothing until it's written.
  3. The OOM score of the same process, before and after making it a preferred victim (no sudo needed to raise your own; lowering it below 0 needs root): 
    cat /proc/$!/oom_score; echo 500 > /proc/$!/oom_score_adj; cat /proc/$!/oom_score
    679
1012
    The adjustment moved it up the list. A process with oom_score_adj of −1000 is never chosen; setting a negative value needs root (and some sandboxed machines refuse even that). Stop it with kill $!.
  4. A kill, contained. Save this hog as hog.py:

    hog.pyPython 3.8+

    # hog.py · take memory 10 MB at a time, say so every 50 MB, and never give it back
import time

held = []
for n in range(1, 1001):
    held.append(bytearray(10 * 1024 * 1024))   # bytearray fills its memory with zeros, so it's really used
    if n % 5 == 0:
        print(f"holding {n * 10} MB", flush=True)
    time.sleep(0.02)
print("finished: holding 10 GB")
    Run it as a transient service with a 200 MB memory limit and no swap, and wait for it to finish: 
    sudo systemd-run --wait --unit=hog -p User=$USER -p MemoryMax=200M -p MemorySwapMax=0 /usr/bin/python3 $HOME/lab10/hog.py
    Running as unit: hog.service; invocation ID: 9d1ab0c9e70e433babfc971be1cfd8e4
Finished with result: signal
Main processes terminated with: code=killed/status=KILL
Service runtime: 524ms
    Killed by signal 9 after half a second. Its output and what systemd saw: 
    sudo journalctl -u hog --no-pager -o cat | tail -6
    Started hog.service - /usr/bin/python3 /home/tiago/hog.py.
holding 50 MB
holding 100 MB
holding 150 MB
hog.service: Main process exited, code=killed, status=9/KILL
hog.service: Failed with result 'signal'.
    It reached 150 MB and died before 200. The kernel's side: 
    sudo dmesg | grep -i "killed process" | tail -1
    [ 3881.215523] Memory cgroup out of memory: Killed process 12758 (python3) total-vm:220000kB, anon-rss:204288kB, file-rss:6456kB, shmem-rss:0kB, UID:1001 pgtables:456kB oom_score_adj:0
    “Memory cgroup out of memory”: the limit was the cgroup's, so the rest of the machine never noticed. anon-rss (about 200 MB) is the memory it had written to; UID:1001 is the user it ran as. On a system using cgroup v2 throughout, systemd may also report the result as oom-kill rather than signal.
  5. Clean up: sudo systemctl reset-failed hog; rm ~/lab10/big.bin ~/lab10/hog.py.

Stretch goals. (1) Run the hog again with -p MemoryHigh=150M -p MemoryMax=200M and watch it with systemd-cgtop: does it slow down before it dies? (2) Run it with no limit at all on a small VM (1 GB) and find which process the global OOM killer chose; were you surprised? (3) Read /proc/pressure/memory during step 4 of a run with MemoryHigh=.

At Stagedoor: the 19 GB query

PostgreSQL's log shows what process 31877 was doing: a settlement report from an analyst's dashboard, grouping three years of ticket rows by venue and seat category. The analyst's session had raised work_mem, the memory each sort or hash step may use, to 4 GB “so the report would finish”. A single query can use work_mem several times over, and this one used it five times. With 8 GB of shared buffers and two hundred checkout connections on top, a 32 GB server with no swap ran out, and the biggest process on it was that one PostgreSQL backend.

Sasha and Rafaela change four things. Reports get their own database role with work_mem capped at 64 MB and a statement timeout; they run on a schedule outside on-sales. door-1 gets an 8 GB swap file, to turn the next shortage into a slowdown with an alert instead of a restart.
