PostgreSQL's main process (the postmaster) gets OOMScoreAdjust=-1000 in a drop-in, with the environment settings PostgreSQL's documentation describes so that its child processes stay killable, so if anything must be killed, it isn't the process whose death restarts everything. And an alert fires when MemAvailable stays below 10%. The real fix, a read-only replica for reports, belongs to Handbook 6.

10.9Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Alerting on low freeConstant false alarms on every healthy server.Alert on MemAvailable, and on swapping in and out.
Judging a process by VSZHuge numbers that use no memory.RSS (or PSS for shared memory).
Dropping caches to “free memory”Every following read goes to disk; nothing is gained.Leave the cache alone; it's given back when needed.
Running reports and batch jobs uncapped on the database serverThe OOM killer picks the database.Limits (MemoryMax=, per-role work_mem), another machine, or a replica.
oom_score_adj=-1000 on everything importantThe killer has nothing left to choose, and the whole machine can hang.Protect one or two processes; limit the rest.
Missing the kernel's message“It just died with 137” stays a mystery.journalctl -k or dmesg for “Killed process”.

✓Chapter summary

  • Each process has a virtual address space; pages use RAM only when touched. VSZ is mapped, RSS is resident.
  • The kernel fills idle RAM with the page cache and gives it back on demand; available is the number that matters.
  • Swap moves idle pages to disk, buying time and turning sudden shortages into slowdowns; continuous swapping is thrashing.
  • When memory runs out, the kernel reclaims cache, swaps, then the OOM killer sends SIGKILL to the process with the highest oom_score, adjusted by oom_score_adj, and logs it.
  • cgroup limits (MemoryMax=, MemoryHigh=, container limits) contain a hungry job so the OOM killer chooses only within it.

Key terms

Virtual address space
The private range of memory addresses a process sees, mapped by the kernel onto RAM page by page.
Page
The unit of memory the kernel manages, usually 4 KB.
RSS
Resident set size: the part of a process's memory currently in RAM.
Page cache
RAM holding copies of file contents, kept for fast re-reading and released when programs need memory.
Overcommit
The kernel allowing programs to reserve more memory in total than the machine has.
Swap
Disk space used to hold memory pages moved out of RAM.
OOM killer
The kernel mechanism that kills a process to free memory when none can otherwise be found.
oom_score_adj
A per-process value from −1000 to 1000 added to its OOM score; −1000 means never kill.
cgroup
A control group: a set of processes the kernel accounts for and limits together.

Check your understanding

  1. free -h shows total 32Gi, used 6Gi, free 400Mi, buff/cache 25Gi, available 25Gi. Is the server short of memory? recall

    Show answer

    No. Programs use about 6 GB; most of the rest is page cache, which the kernel gives back on demand, so about 25 GB is available. Low free on a long-running server is normal.

  2. A container exits with 137. How do you find out whether it was out of memory, and whose limit it hit? apply

    Show answer

    docker inspect shows OOMKilled: true if the container hit its own memory limit. The kernel log (dmesg or journalctl -k) has “Memory cgroup out of memory: Killed process …” for a cgroup limit, or “Out of memory: Killed process …” for a machine-wide shortage. If neither appears, something else sent SIGKILL, such as a stop timeout.

  3. Explain why adding up the RSS of all PostgreSQL processes gives more than the machine's RAM. explain

    Show answer

    PostgreSQL's shared buffers are shared memory mapped by every backend. Each backend's RSS includes the shared pages it has touched, so the same pages are counted many times. PSS (from smem or smaps_rollup) splits shared pages between the processes using them.

  4. A server becomes extremely slow for minutes at a time but nothing is killed. vmstat 1 shows si and so in the thousands. What's happening, and what would you do? debug

    Show answer

    Thrashing: memory demand exceeds RAM, and pages are continuously swapped out and back in. Find the processes with the largest resident and swapped memory (VmSwap in /proc/<pid>/status), reduce or limit them (a cgroup MemoryMax=), or add RAM. Swap bought time; the shortage is real.

  5. Design memory protection for a server that runs PostgreSQL and, at night, a Python export job that sometimes uses a lot of memory. design

    Show answer

    Run the export as a systemd service or timer with MemoryHigh= and MemoryMax= well below what PostgreSQL needs, so it's killed first and alone. Give the postmaster OOMScoreAdjust=-1000. Size PostgreSQL's memory settings (shared buffers, work_mem per connection) with headroom. Add a little swap for warning time and alert on MemAvailable. Better still, run the export elsewhere, against a replica.

  6. Compare MemoryHigh= and MemoryMax=. compare

    Show answer

    MemoryHigh= is soft: above it, the kernel reclaims the cgroup's memory hard and throttles it, slowing it down, but nothing is killed. MemoryMax= is hard: the cgroup can't exceed it, and if reclaim fails the OOM killer kills one of its processes. Use High as a brake a little below Max.

Going further

  • The kernel documentation: Concepts overview (memory management) and Control Group v2 (memory controller).
  • man 5 proc (meminfo, oom_score_adj), man free, man systemd.resource-control.
  • PostgreSQL: Managing kernel resources, section “Linux Memory Overcommit”.

Part III · Resources

11

Disks, filesystems and inodes

Between a cloud volume and a file you can open sit three layers: a partition, a filesystem and a mount. This chapter takes them apart, shows how to find what filled a disk (including the case where the disk isn't full at all), and how to give a server more space without stopping it.

By the end you can

  • Describe the layers from block device to mounted filesystem, and inspect each one.
  • Find what's using space with df and du, and explain when they disagree.
  • Diagnose and fix inode exhaustion.
  • Write a safe /etc/fstab entry and check it before rebooting.
  • Grow a cloud volume, its partition and its filesystem, and explain what LVM adds.

Before you start

  • Chapter 3: inodes, mount points. Chapter 7: deleted-but-open files.
  • A Linux VM with sudo where you may create loop devices (Appendix C); sudo apt install cloud-guest-utils parted.

This chapter

Reading
about 45 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, a VM with sudo, free; works on a file, not a real disk
Main distribution
Ubuntu, with others in 11.8

At Stagedoor

Thursday 25 June 2026, 14:20. Venues report that uploading a poster fails, and so, on door-2, does the app's attempt to write a ticket PDF: No space left on device. Tiago, remembering March (Chapter 7), runs df -h. The root filesystem is 61% full: 62 GB free. He checks for deleted open files: none. He runs df -h again, as if it might change its mind.

Rafaela asks him to try one more letter: df -i. The same filesystem is at 100%.

11.1From block device to file

A block device is storage the kernel reads and writes in fixed-size blocks: a physical disk, a cloud volume, a partition of either, or a file pretending to be a disk. Files only appear once a filesystem is laid over it and mounted into the tree.

lsblk shows the tree of block devices and partitions with their sizes and mount points; it's the first command on any unfamiliar machine. Device names depend on how the disk is attached: /dev/sda (SCSI and SATA), /dev/vda (virtio, common on KVM hosts such as Atlantica's), /dev/nvme0n1 (NVMe, how AWS attaches EBS volumes to current instances), with partitions numbered sda1, vda1, nvme0n1p1. Names can change between boots when disks are added, which is why fstab uses UUIDs (11.4).

11.2Partitions and filesystems

A partition table divides a disk into ranges; GPT is the modern format (the older MBR is limited to 2 TB). Cloud data volumes are often used whole, with no partition at all; boot disks always have one. parted, fdisk or sfdisk edit them.

A filesystem organises a block device into files and directories, with an inode table (Chapter 3) and data blocks. Two dominate on servers:

Table 11.1 · ext4 and XFS
ext4XFS
Default onUbuntu, DebianRHEL family, Amazon Linux
Inodesfixed when created (one per 16 KB by default); can run outallocated as needed; practically never runs out
Grow while mountedyes, resize2fsyes, xfs_growfs
Shrinkyes, unmounted onlyno
Check and repaire2fsck, unmountedxfs_repair, unmounted
Good atgeneral use, small fileslarge files, many parallel writers

mkfs.ext4 /dev/vdb1 or mkfs.xfs /dev/vdb1 creates one, erasing whatever was there. A mount attaches it to a directory: sudo mount /dev/vdb1 /var/lib/stagedoor. Whatever was in that directory before is hidden, not deleted, until you unmount, a classic source of “where did my files go?”.

11.3What filled the disk?

Three commands answer it, and they answer different questions:

  • df -h asks the filesystem how many blocks are in use. It's the truth about space, including space held by deleted files still open (Chapter 7) and blocks reserved for root (ext4 keeps 5% by default so root can still log in and clean up).
  • du -xh --max-depth=1 /var | sort -h walks the directory tree and adds up the files it can see. -x stays on one filesystem. Repeat it one level down until you find the culprit; ncdu does the same interactively.
  • df -i asks how many inodes are in use. Every file and directory needs one, and on ext4 their number is fixed when the filesystem is created.

When df and du disagree, there are three usual reasons: deleted-but-open files (sudo lsof +L1), files hidden under a mount point, or a different filesystem than you think (du without -x wanders into other mounts). And when writes fail with “No space left on device” while df -h shows space, check df -i: an inode exhaustion. Millions of tiny files (caches, sessions, mail queues, thumbnails) can use every inode while using little space. du --inodes -x /var | sort -n | tail finds where they are.

Key idea

A filesystem can run out of two things: blocks and inodes. “No space left” with free space means df -i.

11.4Mounting at boot: /etc/fstab

Mounts you make by hand disappear at reboot. /etc/fstab lists the filesystems to mount at boot, one per line, six fields:

UUID=9024b159-9dd4-4d75-8f1e-7a99a652b68a  /var/lib/stagedoor  ext4  defaults,noatime,nofail  0  2
# what (by UUID)                           where               type  options                  dump  fsck order
  • Identify by UUID (from blkid or lsblk -f), never by device name: /dev/vdb today can be /dev/vdc after the next disk is attached.
  • nofail for anything that isn't needed to boot: if that volume is missing or broken, the machine still comes up and you can still log in. Without it, a detached data volume can leave a cloud server stuck in emergency mode, reachable only through the provider's serial console.
  • noatime stops a write on every read to update access times; a small, free win.
  • The last field orders boot-time checks: 1 for /, 2 for others, 0 for none (XFS ignores it).

Watch out: test fstab before you reboot

A typo in /etc/fstab is found at the next boot, when it's hardest to fix. After editing, run sudo findmnt --verify and sudo mount -a (which mounts everything listed that isn't mounted yet) and fix any error while you're still logged in. systemd turns each fstab line into a .mount unit, so also sudo systemctl daemon-reload.

11.5Growing a volume

On a cloud provider, making a disk bigger is a console click or an API call, and nowadays the instance doesn't even need to stop. But the click only grows the bottom layer of Figure 11.1. Each layer above needs its own step, all of them possible while the filesystem is mounted and in use:

Shrinking is another matter: XFS can't shrink at all, ext4 only unmounted, and cloud volumes generally can't be made smaller. To shrink, create a smaller volume and copy the data. Size data volumes with room to grow, and grow them before they're full rather than during an incident.

What LVM adds

The Logical Volume Manager (LVM) puts a flexible layer between partitions and filesystems. Disks or partitions become physical volumes; they're pooled into a volume group; and logical volumes, which you format and mount, are carved out of the pool. A logical volume can span several disks, be grown into free space in the group with lvextend --resizefs in one command, and be snapshotted. On physical servers like Stagedoor's at Atlantica, LVM is very useful: adding a second disk to a full volume group is how you grow without moving data. In the cloud, where volumes can be resized and snapshotted directly, many teams skip it for data volumes and use one filesystem per volume. Ubuntu Server's installer puts the root filesystem on LVM by default (/dev/ubuntu-vg/ubuntu-lv); sudo pvs, vgs and lvs show the layers.

11.6Swap files and other small jobs

Two more tasks you'll meet. A swap file (Chapter 10): sudo fallocate -l 8G /swapfile, sudo chmod 600 /swapfile, sudo mkswap /swapfile, sudo swapon /swapfile, then a line /swapfile none swap sw 0 0 in fstab. And disk health and speed: iostat -x 1 (from sysstat) shows each device's utilisation, queue and latency (%util near 100 and await in the tens of milliseconds mean the disk is the bottleneck, Chapter 9), and journalctl -k | grep -iE 'I/O error|ext4|xfs' shows errors the kernel saw.

11.7Cloud volumes

Cloud block storage (AWS EBS, Azure Managed Disks, Google Cloud Persistent Disk) is a network service that looks like a local disk. Three properties matter day to day. Performance is bought, not found: IOPS and throughput depend on the volume type and size (on AWS gp3, 3,000 IOPS baseline unless you pay for more, at the time of writing), so a slow disk may be a small or cheap one. Snapshots are cheap and incremental; take one before any resize or risky change. And instance-store or “local SSD” disks are fast but lose their data when the instance stops: use them only for things you can rebuild.

11.8Across distributions

On the RHEL family and Amazon Linux

The default filesystem is XFS, so inode exhaustion is rare and growing uses xfs_growfs /mount/point (it takes the mount point, not the device). RHEL's installer uses LVM for the root filesystem, as Ubuntu's does; Amazon Linux images don't. growpart comes from cloud-utils-growpart. On Ubuntu, growpart is in cloud-guest-utils, and cloud images grow their root partition automatically at first boot (cloud-init, Chapter 1), but not after later resizes.

11.9Lab: a disk in a file

Lab 11 · Partition, fill with inodes, grow

You'll make a disk out of a file (a loop device), partition it, create an ext4 filesystem with very few inodes, run out of inodes while most of the space is free, then grow the disk, partition and filesystem as you would a cloud volume, and check an fstab file without touching the real one. You need a Linux VM with sudo, parted and growpart (sudo apt install parted cloud-guest-utils); not a container. Cost: nothing. Clean-up: the last step. The UUIDs and device numbers on your machine will differ.

  1. Make a disk and partition it. A 200 MB file, attached as a block device (--partscan makes the kernel look for partitions on it), with one 99 MB partition and an ext4 filesystem limited to 2,048 inodes (-N), labelled thumbs: 
    mkdir -p ~/lab11 && cd ~/lab11
truncate -s 200M disk.img
dev=$(sudo losetup --find --show --partscan disk.img); echo "attached as $dev"
sudo parted -s "$dev" mklabel gpt mkpart data ext4 1MiB 100MiB
sudo mkfs.ext4 -q -N 2048 -L thumbs "${dev}p1"
sudo mkdir -p /mnt/lab11
sudo mount "${dev}p1" /mnt/lab11
    attached as /dev/loop0
    Now look at every layer: 
    lsblk -o NAME,SIZE,TYPE,MOUNTPOINTS "$dev"; sudo blkid "${dev}p1"; df -h /mnt/lab11; df -i /mnt/lab11
    NAME       SIZE TYPE MOUNTPOINTS
loop0      200M loop 
`-loop0p1   99M part /mnt/lab11
/dev/loop0p1: LABEL="thumbs" UUID="9024b159-9dd4-4d75-8f1e-7a99a652b68a" BLOCK_SIZE="4096" TYPE="ext4" PARTLABEL="data" PARTUUID="4c4538ad-eafe-43e6-8eb6-562a595dc65e"
Filesystem      Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
/dev/loop0p1     95M   24K   88M   1% /mnt/lab11
Filesystem     Inodes IUsed IFree IUse% Mounted on
/dev/loop0p1     2048    11  2037    1% /mnt/lab11
    The disk (200M), its partition (99M), the filesystem's type, label and UUID, its usable space (95M after ext4's own structures) and its inodes (2,048, 11 already used by the filesystem itself).
  2. Run out of inodes. Write up to 3,000 tiny “thumbnails”, stopping at the first failure: 
    sudo mkdir /mnt/lab11/thumbs; sudo chown "$USER": /mnt/lab11/thumbs
for i in $(seq 1 3000); do echo "thumbnail $i" > /mnt/lab11/thumbs/t$i.jpg || { echo "stopped at file $i"; break; }; done
df -h /mnt/lab11; df -i /mnt/lab11
    bash: line 2: /mnt/lab11/thumbs/t2037.jpg: No space left on device
stopped at file 2037
Filesystem      Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
/dev/loop0p1     95M  8.1M   80M  10% /mnt/lab11
Filesystem     Inodes IUsed IFree IUse% Mounted on
/dev/loop0p1     2048  2048     0  100% /mnt/lab11
    “No space left on device” with 80 MB available. The inodes are gone. Find where: 
    sudo du --inodes -x /mnt/lab11 | sort -n | tail -3; du -sh /mnt/lab11/thumbs
    1	/mnt/lab11/lost+found
2037	/mnt/lab11/thumbs
2039	/mnt/lab11
8.1M	/mnt/lab11/thumbs
    2,037 files in one directory, using only 8 MB. Clear them: rm -r /mnt/lab11/thumbs.
  3. Enlarge the disk, as a provider would, and tell the kernel: 
    truncate -s 400M disk.img
sudo losetup --set-capacity "$dev"
lsblk -o NAME,SIZE,TYPE,MOUNTPOINTS "$dev"
    NAME       SIZE TYPE MOUNTPOINTS
loop0      400M loop 
`-loop0p1   99M part /mnt/lab11
    The disk is 400M; the partition is still 99M (Figure 11.2, step 1).
  4. Grow the partition (step 2): 
    sudo growpart "$dev" 1
    CHANGED: partition=1 start=2048 old: size=202752 end=204799 new: size=817119 end=819166
  5. Grow the filesystem (step 4). On a cloud server you'd do it while mounted, and normally that works: 
    sudo resize2fs "${dev}p1"
    On the test machine for this book, a sandboxed VM, the kernel refused the online resize (resize2fs: Permission denied to resize filesystem), so the filesystem was grown unmounted instead, which works everywhere: 
    sudo umount /mnt/lab11
sudo e2fsck -f -p "${dev}p1"
sudo resize2fs "${dev}p1"
sudo mount "${dev}p1" /mnt/lab11
df -h /mnt/lab11; df -i /mnt/lab11
    thumbs: 11/2048 files (9.1% non-contiguous), 1174/25344 blocks
resize2fs 1.47.0 (5-Feb-2023)
Resizing the filesystem on /dev/loop0p1 to 102139 (4k) blocks.
The filesystem on /dev/loop0p1 is now 102139 (4k) blocks long.

Filesystem      Size  Used Avail Use% Mounted on
/dev/loop0p1    393M   24K  365M   1% /mnt/lab11
Filesystem     Inodes IUsed IFree IUse% Mounted on
/dev/loop0p1     8192    11  8181    1% /mnt/lab11
    393 MB, and four times the inodes: ext4 keeps its inodes-per-space ratio when it grows.
  6. Check an fstab before trusting it. Write a test file with one good line (this filesystem, by UUID) and one bad one, and verify it with findmnt, which never touches /etc/fstab when given --tab-file: 
    uuid=$(sudo blkid -s UUID -o value "${dev}p1")
printf 'UUID=%s  /mnt/lab11  ext4  defaults,noatime,nofail  0  2\n' "$uuid" > fstab.test
printf 'UUID=00000000-1111-2222-3333-444444444444  /mnt/other  ext4  defaults  0  2\n' >> fstab.test
findmnt --verify --tab-file fstab.test; echo "exit status: $?"
    /mnt/other
   [E] unreachable on boot required target: No such file or directory
   [E] unreachable on boot required source: UUID=00000000-1111-2222-3333-444444444444

0 parse errors, 2 errors, 0 warnings
exit status: 1
    The good line passes. The bad one names a filesystem that doesn't exist and a mount point that doesn't exist, without nofail: in a real fstab, that's a server that doesn't finish booting.
  7. Clean up. 
    sudo umount /mnt/lab11
sudo losetup -d "$dev"
rm disk.img fstab.test

Stretch goals. (1) Repeat step 1 with mkfs.xfs instead (no -N), and run step 2 with 30,000 files: XFS allocates inodes as it goes. Grow it with sudo xfs_growfs /mnt/lab11. (2) Before step 4, run du -sh and df -h while a process holds a deleted file open on /mnt/lab11 (Chapter 3's Python one-liner), and see them disagree. (3) Run sudo tune2fs -l "${dev}p1" | grep -iE 'inode count|reserved block' and find ext4's 5% reserve for root.

At Stagedoor: 9.8 million thumbnails

sudo du --inodes -x /var | sort -n | tail points at /var/cache/stagedoor/thumbs: 9.8 million small files. For every poster, the app generates a resized copy for each screen size and device it meets, and has never deleted one; the ext4 root filesystem, made with default settings for a 160 GB disk, has about 10 million inodes in total. Tiago deletes thumbnails older than 30 days with find /var/cache/stagedoor/thumbs -type f -mtime +30 -delete, after running it once without -delete to check, and uploads work again at 15:05.

The thumbnails are a cache, so they're allowed to disappear: a nightly timer now deletes any not used for 30 days, and Sasha's team changes the app to generate three standard sizes instead of one per device. An alert on inode usage above 80% joins the disk-space one. And the same week, door-1's database volume, at 78%, gets room ahead of the autumn: Atlantica enlarges its virtual disk from 1 TB to 1.5 TB on a Tuesday afternoon, and Rafaela grows the partition and the ext4 filesystem with growpart and resize2fs while PostgreSQL keeps running. For the new database server being built for August (Chapter 8), the data goes on its own volume, separate from the operating system, so filling one can never stop the other.

11.10Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Checking only df -hInode exhaustion looks like a mystery.df -i too; alert on both.
Device names in fstabA new disk renames the old ones; the wrong disk is mounted, or none.UUIDs or labels.
No nofail on data volumesA missing volume stops the boot; the server is unreachable.nofail for anything not needed to boot.
Rebooting to test fstabErrors appear when you can't fix them.findmnt --verify and mount -a first.
Resizing the volume and stopping thereThe partition and filesystem stay the same size.growpart, then resize2fs or xfs_growfs.
Data and the operating system on one filesystemFilling one stops the other; logs fill the database's disk.Separate volumes for data that grows.
Resizing without a snapshotA mistake in the partition step loses the volume.Snapshot first; it's cheap.

✓Chapter summary

  • A block device is divided by a partition table, optionally pooled by LVM, formatted with a filesystem and mounted into the tree. lsblk shows the layers.
  • ext4 is Ubuntu's default with a fixed number of inodes; XFS is RHEL's default and allocates inodes as needed.
  • df counts used blocks, du adds up visible files, df -i counts inodes. Each answers a different question.
  • /etc/fstab mounts at boot: use UUIDs, nofail for data volumes, and verify before rebooting.
  • Growing a cloud volume is four steps: the provider, the partition (growpart), LVM if used, the filesystem (resize2fs, xfs_growfs). Snapshot first.

Key terms

Block device
Storage the kernel reads and writes in fixed-size blocks: a disk, a partition, a cloud volume or a loop device.
Partition table
The structure at the start of a disk that divides it into partitions; GPT is the modern format.
Filesystem
The on-disk structure of inodes, directories and data blocks that turns a block device into files.
Mount
Attaching a filesystem to a directory in the tree.
fstab
The file /etc/fstab, listing filesystems to mount at boot and their options.
Inode exhaustion
A filesystem running out of inodes, so no new files can be created although space is free.
LVM
The Logical Volume Manager, which pools disks into volume groups and divides them into resizable logical volumes.
Loop device
A block device backed by a file, used to treat a file as a disk.

Check your understanding

  1. Which command shows, in one view, the disks, their partitions, their sizes and where each is mounted? recall

    Show answer

    lsblk (add -f for filesystem types, labels and UUIDs).

  2. You've enlarged an EBS volume from 100 GB to 200 GB. lsblk shows nvme1n1 200G and nvme1n1p1 100G mounted at /data, which is XFS. Write the remaining commands. apply

    Show answer

    sudo growpart /dev/nvme1n1 1, then sudo xfs_growfs /data (XFS takes the mount point), then df -h /data to confirm. Both work while mounted.

  3. Explain why du -sh / can report much less than df -h /. Give two causes. explain

    Show answer

    du only adds up files it can reach by name; df counts every used block. Deleted files still held open by a process use blocks but have no name; files hidden underneath a mount point also can't be reached. (Ext4's reserved blocks and du skipping unreadable directories without sudo also contribute.)

  4. After adding a second data volume, a cloud server doesn't come back from a reboot, and the serial console shows “You are in emergency mode”. What's the likely cause, and how do you prevent it? debug

    Show answer

    An fstab line that can't be satisfied: a device name that changed (the new volume took the old one's name) or a volume that isn't attached, without nofail. Fix from the console (or by attaching the disk to another instance), then use UUIDs, add nofail for non-essential volumes, and verify with findmnt --verify and mount -a before every reboot.

  5. Design the disk layout for Stagedoor's new database server in the cloud. design

    Show answer

    A small root volume for the operating system. A separate data volume for PostgreSQL, mounted at /var/lib/postgresql by UUID with noatime, sized with room to grow and a volume type with enough IOPS; XFS or ext4 with enough inodes. Optionally a separate volume for write-ahead logs. Snapshots scheduled, and alerts on space and inodes for each filesystem.

  6. Compare using LVM and using whole cloud volumes directly for data. compare

    Show answer

    LVM can span disks, grow a logical volume and its filesystem in one step, and take LVM snapshots, which is valuable on physical servers where disks can't be resized. In the cloud, volumes can already be resized and snapshotted by the provider, so a filesystem directly on a volume is simpler, with one fewer layer to grow and debug. Use LVM where it solves a problem you have.

Going further

  • man lsblk, man fstab, man findmnt, man resize2fs, man xfs_growfs, man lvm.
  • AWS: Extend the file system after resizing an EBS volume.
  • Ubuntu Server documentation: storage, including LVM.
  • The kernel documentation on ext4.

Part III · Resources

12

Limits and kernel settings

Some of the resources that run out on a busy server aren't CPU, memory or disk, but numbers: how many files a process may open, how many connections may wait to be accepted, how many ports there are to connect from. This chapter shows where each limit is set, how to see which one you've hit, and how to raise the right one without copying a page of “tuning” from the internet.

By the end you can

  • Explain file descriptor limits at the system, process and application level, and read a process's limits.
  • Diagnose “Too many open files” and raise the limit for a systemd service.
  • Explain the listen backlog and spot a full accept queue with ss and nstat.
  • Read and set kernel parameters with sysctl, and make them persistent.
  • Decide which settings are worth changing, and say why.

Before you start

  • Chapter 2: file descriptors. Chapter 6: drop-ins. Chapter 5: /proc/<pid>.
  • A Linux VM with Python 3 and iproute2; for the nginx step, a VM with systemd and nginx (Appendix C).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
30 minutes, a Linux VM, free
Main distribution
Ubuntu, with others in 12.7

At Stagedoor

Friday 10 July 2026 is the biggest on-sale in Stagedoor's history: a stadium date for a band that last played Portugal ten years ago. At 10:00:38, fans start seeing 502 Bad Gateway. On door-2, nginx's error log fills with two kinds of line: accept4() failed (24: Too many open files) and 768 worker_connections are not enough.

The CPUs are at 40%, memory is fine, the disks are idle. Nothing in the last six chapters has run out. Tiago reads the error number aloud: 24. Rafaela knows it from a hundred incidents at her old job: EMFILE, the process has hit its limit on open files.

12.1File descriptors, and why servers need many

A file descriptor is the small number a process uses for anything it has open (Chapter 2): a file, a pipe, and, on a server, every network connection. A web proxy like nginx uses two per request it forwards: one for the fan's connection and one for its connection to the app.

Each process can have only so many open at once, and the limit is enforced in three layers:

The middle layer is the one most often hit. Its soft limit is what's enforced; the hard limit is the ceiling a process may raise its own soft limit to. A soft limit of 1024 is a historical default that's still common, and it's far too low for a busy proxy: 1024 file descriptors, minus a few for logs and listening sockets, is about 500 proxied requests per nginx worker.

To see a running process's limits and usage, use /proc, not your own shell's ulimit (which shows only your shell's):

grep -i "open files" /proc/2187/limits          # soft and hard limit of process 2187
ls /proc/2187/fd | wc -l                        # how many it has open right now
sudo ls -l /proc/2187/fd | grep -c socket       # how many of those are sockets

12.2Raising the limit, in the right place

Limits are inherited from parent to child (Chapter 5), so where you set one decides who gets it:

  • For a service, in its unit or a drop-in: LimitNOFILE=65536 sets soft and hard; LimitNOFILE=4096:65536 sets them separately. This is the one that matters for servers, because services don't start from a login shell.
  • For one shell and the programs it starts: ulimit -n 4096 (up to the hard limit; raising the hard limit needs root).
  • For login sessions: /etc/security/limits.conf or a file in /etc/security/limits.d/, read by PAM at login. It has no effect on services, which is the most common reason “I raised the limit and nothing changed”.

Then the application's own setting, the innermost layer. nginx has two: worker_connections, the connections each worker may hold, and worker_rlimit_nofile, which raises the worker's own file limit. A useful rule: worker_rlimit_nofile at least twice worker_connections, and LimitNOFILE= at least that.

Key idea

After changing a limit, check the running process's /proc/<pid>/limits. Not your shell's, not the config file's: the process's.

12.3The listen backlog

Before a server's code sees a connection, the kernel has already done the TCP handshake (Handbook 1, Chapter 3) and put the finished connection in a queue for that listening socket, the accept queue. The program takes connections from it with accept(). The queue's length is the listen backlog: the number the program passes to listen(), capped by the kernel setting net.core.somaxconn.

Two consequences surprise people. First, clients can connect successfully to a program that will never serve them: the lab's crowd reports every connection as made while the server is stuck. Second, the symptom of a full queue is delay (clients retransmit their handshake after one second, then two, then four), not an error message anywhere. ss -lnt shows the queue for every listening socket, and nstat -az TcpExtListenOverflows counts drops since boot.

Since Linux 5.4, net.core.somaxconn defaults to 4096, which is enough for most servers; older kernels (and old “tuning” guides) used 128. The program's own value is usually the limiting one: nginx asks for 511 unless you write listen 443 ssl backlog=4096;.

12.4Kernel parameters with sysctl

The kernel exposes hundreds of settings as files under /proc/sys/: net.core.somaxconn is /proc/sys/net/core/somaxconn. sysctl reads and writes them:

sysctl net.core.somaxconn                          # read one
sudo sysctl -w net.core.somaxconn=8192             # change it now; lost at reboot
echo "net.core.somaxconn = 8192" | sudo tee /etc/sysctl.d/60-stagedoor.conf   # persist
sudo sysctl --system                               # reload every file in /etc/sysctl.d/

Files in /etc/sysctl.d/ are read in name order at boot, later ones winning. Put each setting in a file named for your purpose, with a comment saying why: the next person to see vm.swappiness = 10 deserves to know who decided that, and on what evidence.

Table 12.1 · Kernel settings worth knowing (defaults from Ubuntu 24.04's kernel, at the time of writing)
SettingDefaultControlsChange it when
net.core.somaxconn4096maximum listen backloga server needs a larger backlog and asks for it
net.ipv4.tcp_max_syn_backlogdepends on memoryhalf-open connections per listenerunder SYN floods (with SYN cookies on)
net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range32768 60999source ports for outgoing connectionsa proxy makes tens of thousands of connections to the same backend
fs.file-maxvery largeopen files on the whole systemalmost never on modern kernels
fs.nr_open1048576highest per-process file limita process needs more than a million
vm.swappiness60preference for swapping vs dropping cache (Chapter 10)with evidence, e.g. a database server
vm.overcommit_memory0how freely memory is promised (Chapter 10)a dedicated database server, per its docs

Watch out: copied tuning

The internet is full of “Linux performance tuning” lists with forty sysctl lines. Many were written for kernels from 2010, some are harmful today (net.ipv4.tcp_tw_recycle broke clients behind NAT and was removed from the kernel), and almost none come with a measurement. Change a setting when you can name the limit you hit, show it in a counter or a log, and check afterwards that the counter stopped growing.

12.5Other limits you'll meet

  • Processes and threads: LimitNPROC= and TasksMax= per service (systemd's default task limit is a percentage of the system's), kernel.pid_max for the whole system. Hitting them shows as “Resource temporarily unavailable” when starting threads.
  • Ephemeral ports: every outgoing connection from one address to one destination address and port needs its own source port, and there are about 28,000 in the default range. A proxy that opens a new connection to its backend for every request, and closes it, can run out, helped by the 60 seconds each closed connection spends in TIME_WAIT. Keep-alive connections to the backend (nginx's keepalive in an upstream block) fix it far better than widening the range. ss -s shows the counts.
  • Memory locked, message queues, core file size: other Limit…= settings in man systemd.exec, needed rarely and by specific software, which documents them.

12.6Finding which limit you hit

Errors name limits by their errno numbers and names, and a few cover almost everything in this chapter:

Table 12.2 · Error numbers that mean “a limit”
errnoNameMessageUsually
24EMFILEToo many open filesthis process's RLIMIT_NOFILE
23ENFILEToo many open files in systemfs.file-max
11EAGAINResource temporarily unavailableprocess or thread limits; also normal for non-blocking sockets
99EADDRNOTAVAILCannot assign requested addressout of ephemeral ports
28ENOSPCNo space left on deviceblocks or inodes (Chapter 11)
12ENOMEMCannot allocate memorymemory, or a memory-related limit (Chapter 10)

With the number in hand: read the process's /proc/<pid>/limits, count what it has open in /proc/<pid>/fd, look at the listening socket with ss -lnt, and check the kernel's counters with nstat.

12.7Across distributions

The limits and sysctl settings are the kernel's and systemd's, so they work the same everywhere; defaults differ a little by kernel version. Two practical differences:

On older releases and other packages

Kernels before 5.4 (Ubuntu 18.04, RHEL 7 and 8, Amazon Linux 2's original kernel) default somaxconn to 128, a real limit for busy servers. Distribution nginx packages differ too: Ubuntu's sets worker_connections 768, nginx.org's packages 1024, and neither raises worker_rlimit_nofile. Whatever the defaults, read them on your server with sysctl and /proc/<pid>/limits rather than trusting a book or a blog, this one included.

12.8Lab: run out of file descriptors

Lab 12 · Too many open files, on purpose

You'll run a server that holds every connection open, give it a small file limit, send it a crowd, and watch it fail with errno 24 while the crowd believes it's connected; then fill its accept queue, raise its limit, and finally raise nginx's limits the systemd way. You need a Linux VM with Python 3; for the last step, systemd and nginx (sudo apt install nginx). Cost: nothing. Clean-up: stop the programs, remove the nginx drop-in and config change. Outputs come from the test VM (user tiago) and, for nginx, the systemd test system of Lab 6.

  1. Your own limits, and the kernel's. 
    ulimit -n; ulimit -Hn; grep -i "open files" /proc/self/limits
sysctl net.core.somaxconn fs.file-max fs.nr_open net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range
    1024
20000
Max open files            1024                 20000                files     
net.core.somaxconn = 4096
fs.file-max = 9223372036854775807
fs.nr_open = 1048576
net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range = 32768	60999
    Soft 1024; the hard limit here was 20,000 (on a standard Ubuntu VM you'll more often see 1048576).
  2. The server and the crowd. In ~/lab12, save:

    holdserver.pyPython 3.8+

    # holdserver.py · accept connections and keep every one open, like a proxy waiting on slow checkouts
import os, socket, time

srv = socket.socket()
srv.setsockopt(socket.SOL_SOCKET, socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)
srv.bind(("127.0.0.1", 9000))
srv.listen(1024)
print(f"pid {os.getpid()} listening on 127.0.0.1:9000", flush=True)

held, errors = [], 0
while True:
    try:
        conn, _ = srv.accept()
        held.append(conn)                       # each open connection is one file descriptor
        if len(held) % 100 == 0:
            print(f"holding {len(held)} connections", flush=True)
    except OSError as e:
        errors += 1
        if errors in (1, 100, 1000):            # don't print the same error thousands of times
            print(f"accept failed ({errors}x) with {len(held)} held: {e}", flush=True)
        time.sleep(0.01)

    crowd.pyPython 3.8+

    # crowd.py N · open N connections to 127.0.0.1:9000, hold them 10 seconds, report how many got in
import socket, sys, time

wanted, conns, refused = int(sys.argv[1]), [], 0
for i in range(wanted):
    s = socket.socket()
    s.settimeout(2)
    try:
        s.connect(("127.0.0.1", 9000))
        conns.append(s)
    except OSError:
        refused += 1
print(f"connected {len(conns)} of {wanted}, failed {refused}", flush=True)
time.sleep(10)
  3. A small limit. Start the server in a subshell with a limit of 256 (exec so the limit applies to Python itself, Chapter 5), send 400 connections, and look at the server's output and its file descriptors: 
    (ulimit -n 256; exec python3 holdserver.py) > server.out 2>&1 & srv=$!
sleep 0.5; python3 crowd.py 400 > crowd.out & sleep 4
cat server.out; ls /proc/$srv/fd | wc -l; grep -i "open files" /proc/$srv/limits
wait %2; cat crowd.out; kill $srv
    pid 13799 listening on 127.0.0.1:9000
holding 100 connections
holding 200 connections
accept failed (1x) with 252 held: [Errno 24] Too many open files
accept failed (100x) with 252 held: [Errno 24] Too many open files
256
Max open files            256                  256                  files     
connected 400 of 400, failed 0
    The server stopped accepting at 252 connections: 252 sockets plus standard input, output, error and the listening socket make 256, the limit exactly. Errno 24 is EMFILE. And the crowd reports all 400 as connected: the other 148 completed their handshake into the accept queue and are waiting for a server that can't take them. That's what fans saw on 10 July: not a refusal, a page that never loaded, then a 502 from the load balancer.
  4. Fill the accept queue. Same server, a much bigger crowd (with its own limit raised, so the crowd itself doesn't run out). After a few seconds, look at the listening socket and the kernel's counters: 
    nstat -n
(ulimit -n 256; exec python3 holdserver.py) > server.out 2>&1 & srv=$!
sleep 0.5; (ulimit -n 4096; exec python3 crowd.py 1500) > crowd.out & sleep 12
ss -lnt "sport = :9000"; nstat -az TcpExtListenOverflows TcpExtListenDrops
    State  Recv-Q Send-Q Local Address:Port Peer Address:PortProcess
LISTEN 1025   1024       127.0.0.1:9000      0.0.0.0:*          
#kernel
TcpExtListenOverflows           12                 0.0
TcpExtListenDrops               12                 0.0
    Send-Q 1024 is the backlog the server asked for (under somaxconn 4096); Recv-Q shows the queue full; every handshake beyond it is dropped and counted as an overflow, and those clients wait and retry. nstat -n at the start reset the counters' baseline. The crowd will now take many minutes to give up on the rest, two seconds per attempt: stop both with kill %1 %2.
  5. A big enough limit. The same 400 connections, with a limit of 4096: 
    (ulimit -n 4096; exec python3 holdserver.py) > server.out 2>&1 & srv=$!
sleep 0.5; python3 crowd.py 400 > crowd.out & sleep 4
cat server.out; ls /proc/$srv/fd | wc -l
wait %2; cat crowd.out; kill $srv
    pid 13817 listening on 127.0.0.1:9000
holding 100 connections
holding 200 connections
holding 300 connections
holding 400 connections
404
connected 400 of 400, failed 0
    All 400 accepted; 404 descriptors open.
  6. nginx, the systemd way. On a VM with systemd and nginx, look at the unit's limit, a worker's real limit, and nginx's own settings: 
    systemctl show nginx -p LimitNOFILE -p LimitNOFILESoft
grep -i "open files" /proc/$(pgrep -n -u www-data nginx)/limits
grep -E "worker_(processes|connections|rlimit)" /etc/nginx/nginx.conf
    LimitNOFILE=524288
LimitNOFILESoft=1024
Max open files            1024                 20000                files     
worker_processes auto;
	worker_connections 768;
    A worker gets soft 1024 (hard 20,000 was the test system's own ceiling; usually it's 524288, as the unit says), and Ubuntu's nginx allows 768 connections per worker: this was door-2 on 10 July. Raise all three layers: the unit (a drop-in), nginx's file limit and its connections, then check the worker again: 
    sudo mkdir -p /etc/systemd/system/nginx.service.d
printf "[Service]\nLimitNOFILE=16384\n" | sudo tee /etc/systemd/system/nginx.service.d/limits.conf > /dev/null
sudo sed -i "s/^worker_processes auto;/worker_processes auto;\nworker_rlimit_nofile 16384;/; s/worker_connections 768;/worker_connections 4096;/" /etc/nginx/nginx.conf
sudo nginx -t
sudo systemctl daemon-reload; sudo systemctl restart nginx
systemctl show nginx -p LimitNOFILE -p LimitNOFILESoft
grep -i "open files" /proc/$(pgrep -n -u www-data nginx)/limits
    nginx: the configuration file /etc/nginx/nginx.conf syntax is ok
nginx: configuration file /etc/nginx/nginx.conf test is successful
LimitNOFILE=16384
LimitNOFILESoft=16384
Max open files            16384                16384                files
    16,384 here because the test system couldn't go higher; on a normal VM, 65536 is a common choice. Undo afterwards: delete the drop-in, reverse the two edits, daemon-reload and restart.

Stretch goals. (1) Run holdserver.py as a transient service with sudo systemd-run --unit=hold -p User=$USER -p LimitNOFILE=256 /usr/bin/python3 $HOME/lab12/holdserver.py and confirm the limit with /proc; notice that your shell's ulimit made no difference. (2) Change srv.listen(1024) to srv.listen(16) and repeat step 4: what does Send-Q show? (3) ss -s before and after step 5: count the established connections.

At Stagedoor: three layers on both web servers

At 10:04, Rafaela raises the limits on door-2 and then door-3, in the order of Figure 12.1, and restarts nginx on each in turn: by 10:07 the 502s have stopped. Afterwards she writes the change into both servers' configuration properly. A drop-in sets LimitNOFILE=65536 for nginx and for stagedoor.service; nginx gets worker_rlimit_nofile 65536, worker_connections 8192, listen … backlog=4096, and keep-alive connections to the app so each fan no longer costs a new connection and a port. An alert now watches TcpExtListenOverflows and the number of open descriptors per nginx worker.

The on-sale sells out at 10:31, despite the seven bad minutes. In the retrospective, Joana asks the question this whole Part has been answering: how would we know before an on-sale? Rafaela's answer is a load test the week before every big one, against a copy of the servers, watching CPU, memory, disk, inodes and the limits in this chapter. Building that copy needs servers that can be rebuilt from a script, which is where Parts IV and V are heading.

12.9Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Raising limits in limits.conf for a serviceNothing changes: services don't use PAM login sessions.LimitNOFILE= in a drop-in.
Checking ulimit -n in your own shellYou see your shell's limit, not the service's./proc/<pid>/limits of the running process.
Raising one layer onlyThe next layer in is still the bottleneck.System, process and application, in that order (Figure 12.1).
Raising somaxconn but not the program's backlogThe program still asks for 511 or 128.Set the program's backlog (nginx listen … backlog=) too.
Copying tuning listsObsolete or harmful settings nobody can explain.Change what a counter or log proves you hit; comment why.
A new backend connection per requestEphemeral ports and TIME_WAIT pile up.Keep-alive connection pools.

✓Chapter summary

  • Every open file and network connection is a file descriptor; a proxy uses two per forwarded request.
  • File limits come in three layers: the system (fs.file-max, fs.nr_open), the process (RLIMIT_NOFILE, soft and hard) and the application (worker_connections, pool sizes). The smallest wins.
  • For services, set LimitNOFILE= in the unit; check the running process's /proc/<pid>/limits.
  • Finished handshakes wait in the accept queue, sized by the program's backlog capped by somaxconn. A full queue drops handshakes and looks like slowness; ss -lnt and nstat show it.
  • sysctl reads and sets kernel parameters; persist them in /etc/sysctl.d/, and change only what a measurement justifies.

Key terms

RLIMIT_NOFILE
The per-process limit on open file descriptors, with a soft (enforced) and a hard (ceiling) value.
Soft and hard limits
The enforced value of a resource limit, and the ceiling to which a non-root process may raise it.
EMFILE
Error 24, “Too many open files”: a process has reached its file descriptor limit.
Accept queue
The kernel's queue of fully established connections waiting for a program to call accept().
Listen backlog
The length of a listening socket's accept queue: the program's value, capped by net.core.somaxconn.
sysctl
The interface, and the command, for reading and setting kernel parameters under /proc/sys.
Ephemeral port
A temporary local port the kernel assigns to an outgoing connection.

Check your understanding

  1. Where do you look to find the open-file limit of the running PostgreSQL postmaster, and how many files it has open? recall

    Show answer

    /proc/<pid>/limits (the “Max open files” line) and ls /proc/<pid>/fd | wc -l, with the PID from systemctl show -p MainPID postgresql@16-main or pgrep. Not ulimit -n in your shell.

  2. Write a drop-in that gives stagedoor.service a soft limit of 8192 and a hard limit of 65536 open files. apply

    Show answer

    /etc/systemd/system/stagedoor.service.d/limits.conf with [Service] and LimitNOFILE=8192:65536; then sudo systemctl daemon-reload, sudo systemctl restart stagedoor, and check /proc/<pid>/limits.

  3. Explain why clients can report a successful connection to a server that's stuck with “Too many open files”. explain

    Show answer

    The kernel completes the TCP handshake and puts the connection in the listening socket's accept queue without involving the program. The client sees an established connection. Only when the program calls accept() does the connection need a file descriptor, and that's the step that fails, so the connection waits in the queue until the client gives up.

  4. After raising nofile in /etc/security/limits.conf and rebooting, nginx still logs “Too many open files”. Why, and what's the fix? debug

    Show answer

    limits.conf applies to login sessions through PAM; nginx is started by systemd, which uses the unit's LimitNOFILE=. Add a drop-in with LimitNOFILE=65536, set worker_rlimit_nofile (and enough worker_connections) in nginx's config, restart, and confirm in a worker's /proc/<pid>/limits.

  5. Design the checks for a load test before a big on-sale, so that limit problems show up a week early. design

    Show answer

    Generate more than the expected peak against a copy of the servers, and watch: nginx's error log for errno 24 and “worker_connections are not enough”; open descriptors per worker against its limit; TcpExtListenOverflows and ss -lnt queues; ss -s for TIME_WAIT and ephemeral port use; plus CPU, memory, disk and inode usage from Chapters 9–11. Fix each limit reached, then test again.

  6. Compare raising net.ipv4.ip_local_port_range with using keep-alive connections to the backend, for a proxy running out of ephemeral ports. compare

    Show answer

    Widening the range adds ports (at most about 64,000 per destination) and delays the problem. Keep-alive reuses a small pool of connections for many requests, so ports and TIME_WAIT entries barely grow with traffic, and it also saves handshakes and latency. Keep-alive fixes the cause; widening the range treats the symptom.

Going further

  • man 2 getrlimit, man 5 proc (/proc/<pid>/limits), man systemd.exec (LimitNOFILE=), man sysctl.d.
  • nginx: worker_rlimit_nofile, worker_connections and the backlog parameter of listen.
  • The kernel documentation: IP sysctl (every net.ipv4 setting explained).
  • Cloudflare's blog: SYN packet handling in the wild, on the two queues.

Part IV · Networking from the host

13

Network configuration and tools

Every network problem eventually lands on one machine, and on that machine four things decide where a packet goes: its addresses, its routes, the sockets that are listening, and how it turns names into addresses. This chapter shows each one with the tool that reads it, and builds a small network of pretend servers on your own machine to practise on.

By the end you can

  • Read a server's interfaces, addresses and routes with ip, and ask which route a packet would take.
  • List listening sockets with ss, and spot a service listening only on localhost.
  • Explain how a name becomes an address on Linux, and why getent and dig can disagree.
  • Read an Ubuntu netplan file and apply a change safely.
  • Build a test network from network namespaces.

Before you start

  • Handbook 1, Chapters 2–5 (addresses and CIDR, TCP and ports, routing, DNS) help a lot; this chapter recaps what it needs.
  • A Linux VM with sudo and these packages: iproute2 iputils-ping netcat-openbsd dnsutils dnsmasq-base (Appendix C).

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
35 minutes, a Linux VM with sudo, free; everything stays on your machine
Main distribution
Ubuntu 24.04, with others in 13.7

At Stagedoor

The new database server promised in Chapter 8 is ready on Monday 24 August 2026: door-4, Ubuntu 24.04, address 10.40.0.14, built by Rafaela and Tiago from a written checklist. The move from door-1 is planned for Thursday night; Monday afternoon is the dry run. The app's settings on both web servers now say db.stagedoor.internal, and the internal DNS record points at 10.40.0.14.

Two things go wrong in ten minutes. On door-2, the app's test connection fails at once: connection refused. On door-3, it succeeds, which is worse, because its log shows it connected to 10.40.0.11: the old server.

13.1The network as one host sees it

From inside a server, “the network” is four tables the kernel keeps. Nearly every connection problem is one of them being different from what you assumed.

One tool reads most of them. ip, from the iproute2 package, replaced the older ifconfig, route and arp (still found in old guides and on old servers, from net-tools): use ip, which shows everything, including what the old tools can't.

13.2Interfaces, addresses and routes

Table 13.1 · The ip commands you'll use
You wantCommand
interfaces and addresses, one line eachip -br address (ip -br a)
everything about one interfaceip address show dev eth0
link state, MTU, MACip -br link
the routing tableip route
which route a packet to X would takeip route get 10.40.0.14
the neighbour (ARP) tableip neigh
add an address or route now (until reboot)sudo ip addr add 10.40.0.20/24 dev eth0, sudo ip route add 10.50.0.0/16 via 10.40.0.1

A server's routing table is short. Usually there's one route per directly connected network, added automatically from the interface's address (10.40.0.0/24 dev eth0 proto kernel scope link: anything in this range is on the wire, reachable directly), and a default route for everything else (default via 10.40.0.1: send it to this gateway). The kernel picks the most specific matching route, as Handbook 1, Chapter 4 explains. ip route get asks the kernel to make that choice for one address and shows the answer, including the source address it would use: the quickest way to settle “which interface does this go out of?” on a machine with several.

For addresses on the same network, the kernel needs the neighbour's hardware (MAC) address, which it learns with ARP and keeps in the neighbour table (ip neigh). An entry stuck in FAILED or INCOMPLETE means nothing answered: the other machine is down, on another network, or a firewall drops ARP.

Changes with ip don't lastAddresses and routes added with ip vanish at reboot. They're perfect for testing; the permanent configuration lives in netplan on Ubuntu (13.5).

13.3Who is listening?

A server program accepts connections on a listening socket: an address and a port. ss (which replaced netstat) lists them:

sudo ss -tlnp      # -t TCP, -l listening, -n numbers not names, -p the process (needs sudo for others' processes)
sudo ss -ulnp      # the same for UDP
ss -tn state established '( dport = :5432 )'   # established connections to port 5432

The address in Local Address:Port decides who can connect, and it's the cause of a classic failure:

  • 0.0.0.0:5432 (IPv4) or [::]:5432 (IPv6, often both): every address on the machine. Reachable from anywhere a route and firewall allow.
  • 10.40.0.14:5432: only that address.
  • 127.0.0.1:5432: only loopback, the machine talking to itself. Unreachable from any other machine, whatever the firewall says. Many services listen here by default to be safe: PostgreSQL (listen_addresses = 'localhost'), Redis, many admin interfaces.

What a client sees tells you which case you're in. Connection refused means the packet reached the machine and nothing was listening on that address and port, so the kernel answered with a reset. A timeout means nothing answered at all: wrong address, no route, or a firewall dropping the packet (Chapter 15).

Key idea

“Refused” means you reached the machine and nobody was listening there. “Timed out” means you didn't get an answer at all. They point at different layers.

13.4From a name to an address

When a program connects to db.stagedoor.internal, it calls the C library's resolver (getaddrinfo), which follows the hosts: line in /etc/nsswitch.conf. On a server that line is usually files dns: look in /etc/hosts first, and only if the name isn't there, ask DNS.

  • /etc/hosts is a plain list of addresses and names. It's handy for one machine's own name and for testing, and dangerous for anything else: an entry there wins over DNS on that machine, silently and for ever, long after everyone has forgotten it.
  • /etc/resolv.conf names the DNS servers. On Ubuntu it points at 127.0.0.53, a local stub run by systemd-resolved, which forwards queries to the real servers (from DHCP or netplan) and caches the answers. resolvectl status shows the real servers per interface; resolvectl query name resolves through it; resolvectl flush-caches empties its cache.
  • dig asks a DNS server directly and shows the whole answer, with its TTL (how long it may be cached, Handbook 1, Chapter 5). dig @10.40.0.1 name asks a particular server; dig +short name prints only the answer.

Watch out: “DNS is fine, I checked with dig”

dig proves what DNS says, not what your program gets. Applications also cache addresses themselves (connection pools, the JVM's DNS cache, long-lived connections that never look the name up again). To know what the app will do, use getent hosts on the same machine, and look at its real connections with ss -tnp.

13.5Making it permanent: netplan

On Ubuntu servers, the network configuration is written in YAML under /etc/netplan/, and netplan turns it into configuration for systemd-networkd (on servers) or NetworkManager (on desktops). Cloud images ship a file written by cloud-init that asks for an address by DHCP; servers on a private network like Atlantica's usually have static addresses:

/etc/netplan/60-stagedoor.yamlnetplan (an illustration of door-4's configuration)

network:
  version: 2
  ethernets:
    eth0:
      addresses: [10.40.0.14/24]
      routes:
        - to: default
          via: 10.40.0.1
      nameservers:
        addresses: [10.40.0.1]
        search: [stagedoor.internal]

Apply changes with sudo netplan try, not apply: it applies the new configuration and rolls it back after 120 seconds unless you confirm. If the change cuts off your SSH session, you can't confirm, and the old configuration returns by itself. On a remote server, that's the difference between a typo and a trip to the provider's console. netplan get shows the merged configuration; networkctl status shows what systemd-networkd did with it. File permissions matter too: netplan warns if a file is readable by others, since it may hold Wi-Fi or VPN secrets.

13.6A lab network from namespaces

To practise on several servers without renting any, this book uses network namespaces: the kernel can give a group of processes its own complete network stack, with its own interfaces, addresses, routes, firewall and sockets. Containers are built on exactly this (Chapter 19). A veth pair is a virtual cable with one end in each namespace; a bridge is a virtual switch. ip netns exec door-2 COMMAND runs a command “on” door-2: it sees only that namespace's network, while sharing the rest of the machine (files, users, processes).

The lab's script builds three pretend servers on one switch, with your machine as 10.40.0.1, their gateway and DNS server. Chapters 14–16 reuse it.

13.7Across distributions

Table 13.2 · Where network configuration lives
UbuntuDebianRHEL family, Amazon Linux 2023
Configurationnetplan YAML in /etc/netplan//etc/network/interfaces (ifupdown) on servers; netplan on cloud imagesNetworkManager (nmcli, files in /etc/NetworkManager/); Amazon Linux: systemd-networkd
Resolversystemd-resolved, stub 127.0.0.53usually plain /etc/resolv.confNetworkManager writes /etc/resolv.conf; AL2023 uses systemd-resolved
Toolsip, ss, dig (dnsutils / bind-utils) everywhere

On the cloud

Cloud VMs get their address by DHCP from the provider, along with the provider's DNS resolver (on AWS, the VPC's resolver at the network's base address plus two, Handbook 1, Chapter 5). You rarely configure addresses on the VM itself: you configure the network around it (subnets, route tables, security groups) and the VM picks it up. The host-side tools in this chapter are how you check that what you configured is what the VM actually got.

13.8Lab: Stagedoor's dry run, in miniature

Lab 13 · Three servers, one switch, two bugs

You'll build a pretend Stagedoor network from network namespaces, look at it with ip, reproduce the dry run's two failures (a service listening on localhost, and a stale /etc/hosts entry beating DNS) and fix both. You need a Linux VM with sudo (not a container) and sudo apt install iproute2 iputils-ping netcat-openbsd dnsutils dnsmasq-base. Cost: nothing; nothing leaves your machine. Clean-up: the last step. MAC addresses, interface numbers and times will differ.

  1. Build the network. Save the script as netlab.sh, make it executable, and run it:

    netlab.shbash

    #!/usr/bin/env bash
# netlab.sh up|down · a pretend Stagedoor network on one Linux machine, built from network namespaces.
# Three "servers" (door-2, door-3, door-4) share a switch (a Linux bridge) with your machine, which is 10.40.0.1.
set -euo pipefail
hosts=(door-2:10.40.0.12 door-3:10.40.0.13 door-4:10.40.0.14)

up() {
  ip link add br-lab type bridge
  ip addr add 10.40.0.1/24 dev br-lab
  ip link set br-lab up
  for h in "${hosts[@]}"; do
    name=${h%%:*} addr=${h##*:} n=${name#door-}
    ip netns add "$name"
    ip link add "veth$n" type veth peer name eth0 netns "$name"   # a cable: one end on the switch, one in the server
    ip link set "veth$n" master br-lab up
    ip -n "$name" addr add "$addr/24" dev eth0
    ip -n "$name" link set eth0 up
    ip -n "$name" link set lo up
    ip -n "$name" route add default via 10.40.0.1
    mkdir -p "/etc/netns/$name"                                   # ip netns exec mounts these over /etc
    echo "nameserver 10.40.0.1" > "/etc/netns/$name/resolv.conf"
  done
  if iptables -L DOCKER-USER -n >/dev/null 2>&1; then              # Docker's firewall drops bridged traffic
    iptables -I DOCKER-USER -i br-lab -o br-lab -j ACCEPT           # by default; let the lab's own through
  fi
  echo "lab network up: 10.40.0.0/24 on br-lab"
}

down() {
  for h in "${hosts[@]}"; do
    name=${h%%:*}
    ip netns del "$name" 2>/dev/null || true
    rm -rf "/etc/netns/$name"
  done
  ip link del br-lab 2>/dev/null || true
  iptables -D DOCKER-USER -i br-lab -o br-lab -j ACCEPT 2>/dev/null || true
  echo "lab network removed"
}

case "${1:-}" in
  up) up ;;
  down) down ;;
  *) echo "usage: sudo $0 up|down" >&2; exit 2 ;;
esac
    chmod +x netlab.sh
sudo ./netlab.sh up
    It prints lab network up: 10.40.0.0/24 on br-lab. The DOCKER-USER lines matter only on machines with Docker installed, whose firewall otherwise drops traffic across other bridges: the test machine for this book was one, which is how this line got into the script (Chapter 15 explains why).
  2. Look at door-2. Its interfaces and routes, then a ping to door-4 and the neighbour entry it leaves: 
    sudo ip netns exec door-2 ip -br address
sudo ip netns exec door-2 ip route
    lo               UNKNOWN        127.0.0.1/8 
eth0@if13        UP             10.40.0.12/24 
default via 10.40.0.1 dev eth0 
10.40.0.0/24 dev eth0 proto kernel scope link src 10.40.0.12 
    sudo ip netns exec door-2 ping -c 2 10.40.0.14
sudo ip netns exec door-2 ip neigh
    PING 10.40.0.14 (10.40.0.14) 56(84) bytes of data.
64 bytes from 10.40.0.14: icmp_seq=1 ttl=64 time=0.051 ms
64 bytes from 10.40.0.14: icmp_seq=2 ttl=64 time=0.069 ms

--- 10.40.0.14 ping statistics ---
2 packets transmitted, 2 received, 0% packet loss, time 1002ms
rtt min/avg/max/mdev = 0.051/0.060/0.069/0.009 ms
10.40.0.14 dev eth0 lladdr a2:73:bf:e5:6a:98 REACHABLE
    Ask the kernel which route it would use for a neighbour and for an outside address: 
    sudo ip netns exec door-2 ip route get 10.40.0.14
sudo ip netns exec door-2 ip route get 203.0.113.40
    10.40.0.14 dev eth0 src 10.40.0.12 uid 0 
    cache 
203.0.113.40 via 10.40.0.1 dev eth0 src 10.40.0.12 uid 0 
    cache
    Directly on eth0 for the neighbour; via 10.40.0.1, the default route, for everything else.
  3. A database that listens on localhost. Start a stand-in on door-4 (nc -lk listens and keeps listening) exactly as PostgreSQL does by default, then try to reach it from door-2: 
    sudo ip netns exec door-4 nc -lk 127.0.0.1 5432 &
sudo ip netns exec door-4 ss -tlnp
    State  Recv-Q Send-Q Local Address:Port Peer Address:PortProcess
LISTEN 0      1          127.0.0.1:5432      0.0.0.0:*    users:(("nc",pid=1431,fd=3))
    sudo ip netns exec door-2 nc -zv -w 2 10.40.0.14 5432; echo "exit status: $?"
    nc: connect to 10.40.0.14 port 5432 (tcp) failed: Connection refused
exit status: 1
    Refused, instantly: door-4 got the packet, and nothing listens on 10.40.0.14:5432. That was door-2's error in the dry run.
  4. Fix it: stop the stand-in (sudo pkill -f "nc -lk 127.0.0.1 5432") and listen on the server's own address, as listen_addresses = '10.40.0.14' would: 
    sudo ip netns exec door-4 nc -lk 10.40.0.14 5432 &
sudo ip netns exec door-4 ss -tlnp
sudo ip netns exec door-2 nc -zv -w 2 10.40.0.14 5432
    State  Recv-Q Send-Q Local Address:Port Peer Address:PortProcess
LISTEN 0      1         10.40.0.14:5432      0.0.0.0:*    users:(("nc",pid=1444,fd=3))
Connection to 10.40.0.14 5432 port [tcp/postgresql] succeeded!
  5. A DNS server for the lab. Start dnsmasq on 10.40.0.1 with two records, answering only on the lab network: 
    sudo dnsmasq --listen-address=10.40.0.1 --bind-interfaces --no-resolv --no-hosts --port=53 \
  --host-record=db.stagedoor.internal,10.40.0.14 --host-record=door-1.stagedoor.internal,10.40.0.11 \
  --local-ttl=300 --pid-file=/run/lab-dnsmasq.pid
sudo ip netns exec door-2 dig db.stagedoor.internal
    
; <<>> DiG 9.18.39-0ubuntu0.24.04.9-Ubuntu <<>> db.stagedoor.internal
;; global options: +cmd
;; Got answer:
;; ->>HEADER<<- opcode: QUERY, status: NOERROR, id: 44357
;; flags: qr aa rd ra; QUERY: 1, ANSWER: 1, AUTHORITY: 0, ADDITIONAL: 1

;; OPT PSEUDOSECTION:
; EDNS: version: 0, flags:; udp: 1232
;; QUESTION SECTION:
;db.stagedoor.internal.		IN	A

;; ANSWER SECTION:
db.stagedoor.internal.	300	IN	A	10.40.0.14

;; Query time: 0 msec
;; SERVER: 10.40.0.1#53(10.40.0.1) (UDP)
;; WHEN: Fri Nov 27 09:33:47 UTC 2026
;; MSG SIZE  rcvd: 66
    status: NOERROR, one answer, 10.40.0.14 with a TTL of 300 seconds, from SERVER: 10.40.0.1#53. The aa flag means the answer is authoritative: dnsmasq owns this name.
  6. The stale /etc/hosts line. Give door-3 its own hosts file (ip netns exec mounts files from /etc/netns/door-3/ over /etc) with the entry Leonor added in 2025, then compare what a program gets with what DNS says: 
    echo "10.40.0.11  db.stagedoor.internal" | sudo tee /etc/netns/door-3/hosts
sudo ip netns exec door-3 getent hosts db.stagedoor.internal
sudo ip netns exec door-3 dig +short db.stagedoor.internal
grep '^hosts:' /etc/nsswitch.conf
    10.40.0.11      db.stagedoor.internal
10.40.0.14
hosts:          files dns
    The app on door-3 would connect to the old server, while dig reports the new one: files comes before dns. Delete the line (sudo rm /etc/netns/door-3/hosts) and run getent again: 10.40.0.14.
  7. Clean up. 
    sudo pkill -f "nc -lk 10.40.0.14 5432"
sudo kill "$(cat /run/lab-dnsmasq.pid)"
sudo ./netlab.sh down
    Keep netlab.sh: Chapters 14 to 16 use it again.

Stretch goals. (1) In door-2, delete the default route (sudo ip -n door-2 route del default) and run ip route get 203.0.113.40: what error do you get, and what would an app see? (2) Run sudo ip netns exec door-2 ss -tn while nc 10.40.0.14 5432 is connected: find both ends of the connection. (3) Run dig +trace on your own machine for a public name, if your network allows DNS out, and follow the delegation (Handbook 1, Chapter 5).

At Stagedoor: Thursday night

sudo ss -tlnp on door-4 shows PostgreSQL on 127.0.0.1:5432: the checklist installed it but never set listen_addresses. It becomes 'localhost, 10.40.0.14', with pg_hba.conf allowing only the two web servers' addresses (Handbook 6 covers the database side). On door-3, getent hosts db.stagedoor.internal answers 10.40.0.11 while dig answers 10.40.0.14; /etc/hosts holds a line Leonor added during a DNS outage in 2025. It's removed, and a new checklist item says: internal names live in DNS, and nowhere else.

The move happens on Thursday 27 August between 23:00 and 01:30, with the app in maintenance mode. By Friday morning the web servers talk to door-4. door-1 stays read-only as a fallback until Monday 31 August, the last day of Debian 11's security support, and is then switched off.

13.9Common mistakes

MistakeWhat happensInstead
Opening the firewall when the service listens on 127.0.0.1Still refused; time wasted on the wrong layer.ss -tlnp on the server first.
Checking names only with dig/etc/hosts and app caches go unnoticed.getent hosts on the same machine as the app.
Internal names in /etc/hosts on many serversOne forgotten copy points somewhere old for years.One internal DNS zone.
netplan apply over SSHA typo cuts you off; only the console helps.netplan try, which rolls back by itself.
Using ifconfig and netstatMissing information (several addresses per interface, policy routes), and absent on new systems.ip and ss.
Treating “refused” and “timed out” as the sameDebugging the wrong layer.Refused: nothing listening. Timeout: no route, or a firewall drops.

✓Chapter summary

  • On one host, a connection depends on four things: the name, the route, the interface, and a listening socket at the far end.
  • ip -br address, ip route, ip route get and ip neigh show addresses, routes, the chosen route and neighbours.
  • ss -tlnp lists listening sockets. A service on 127.0.0.1 can't be reached from other machines. Refused means nothing listening; a timeout means no answer.
  • Programs resolve names through nsswitch.conf: /etc/hosts first, then DNS (on Ubuntu via systemd-resolved at 127.0.0.53). getent hosts shows what a program gets; dig shows what DNS says.
  • Permanent configuration on Ubuntu is netplan; change it with netplan try.
  • Network namespaces give a process its own network stack: a cheap lab, and the basis of containers.

Key terms

Routing table
The kernel's list of destination ranges and where to send packets for each: directly on an interface or via a gateway.
Default route
The route used for any destination no more specific route matches, usually via a gateway.
Listening socket
An address and port on which a program accepts incoming connections.
Loopback
The interface (lo, 127.0.0.1) through which a machine talks to itself; unreachable from other machines.
nsswitch.conf
The file that tells the C library which sources to use, in which order, for names, users and other lookups.
systemd-resolved
The local DNS stub resolver and cache on Ubuntu, listening on 127.0.0.53.
netplan
Ubuntu's YAML network configuration, rendered for systemd-networkd or NetworkManager.
Network namespace
An isolated copy of the network stack (interfaces, routes, firewall, sockets) for a group of processes.

Check your understanding

  1. Which command tells you which interface and source address the kernel would use to reach 10.50.3.7? recall

    Show answer

    ip route get 10.50.3.7. It prints the chosen route, the outgoing device, the gateway if any, and the source address.

  2. ss -tlnp on a server shows 127.0.0.1:6379 for Redis. A web server on another machine needs it. What do you change, and what else must you check before you do? apply

    Show answer

    Make Redis listen on the server's private address (bind 10.x.y.z in its configuration) and restart it. Before that: require authentication, and make sure a firewall or security group allows only the web servers to reach the port, because Redis on a reachable address with no password is a well-known way to be compromised.

  3. Explain why dig and the app on the same server can disagree about a name's address. explain

    Show answer

    dig asks a DNS server directly. The app resolves through the C library, which follows nsswitch.conf and usually checks /etc/hosts first; it may also go through a local cache (systemd-resolved) or cache addresses itself. A stale /etc/hosts entry or cache gives the app a different answer from DNS.

  4. From a web server, nc -zv 10.40.0.14 5432 hangs for its whole timeout instead of failing at once. List three causes, and the command that checks each. debug

    Show answer

    No route or wrong gateway (ip route get 10.40.0.14); the machine is down or not on that network (ip neigh showing FAILED, no ping reply); a firewall dropping the packets on either host or in between (Chapter 15: nft list ruleset, the cloud's security groups). A refused connection would have meant the packet arrived and nothing listened.

  5. Design how Stagedoor's servers should find each other by name. design

    Show answer

    One internal DNS zone (stagedoor.internal) served by the provider's private DNS or a small resolver, with records per service (db, scan-api) rather than per machine, so a move changes one record; short TTLs (a few minutes) on records that move; no internal names in /etc/hosts; netplan pointing each server at that resolver with search: [stagedoor.internal].

  6. Compare netplan apply and netplan try for a change on a remote server. compare

    Show answer

    apply makes the change at once and permanently; if it breaks connectivity, you need a console to fix it. try applies it and reverts after a timeout unless you confirm, so a change that cuts your SSH session undoes itself. Use try on anything you reach over the network.

Going further

  • man ip-address, man ip-route, man ss, man nsswitch.conf, man resolv.conf, man ip-netns.
  • Netplan documentation, especially the YAML reference.
  • man systemd-resolved and man resolvectl.
  • Handbook 1: Chapters 2 (addresses and CIDR), 4 (routing) and 5 (DNS).

Part IV · Networking from the host

14

Debugging connections

“It can't connect” is the most common sentence in operations, and the least informative. This chapter gives you a method: five questions, asked in order, each answered by one tool, from the name to the application. Then it takes apart TLS, the layer that fails most often for reasons that have nothing to do with the network, and shows how to watch the packets themselves when nothing else explains what's happening.

By the end you can

  • Locate a connection failure layer by layer: name, route, port, TLS, application.
  • Read curl -v output and curl's exit codes.
  • Inspect a server's certificate, chain and expiry with openssl.
  • Capture and read the packets of one connection with tcpdump.
  • Set up certificate renewal and expiry monitoring so certificates don't expire by surprise.

Before you start

  • Chapter 13: addresses, routes, sockets, name resolution, and its netlab.sh.
  • Handbook 1, Chapter 6 (HTTP, TLS and certificates) helps.
  • A Linux VM with sudo, and sudo apt install curl openssl tcpdump dnsutils netcat-openbsd dnsmasq-base.

This chapter

Reading
about 40 minutes
Lab
35 minutes, a Linux VM with sudo, free
Main distribution
any; the tools are the same everywhere

At Stagedoor

Sunday 13 September 2026, 18:40. Doors have opened for an early show at a Lisbon venue, and the door staff's scanning app shows one message for every ticket: Can't connect securely to Stagedoor. At a second venue, the same. Staff start checking names against printed lists, and the queues reach the street.

Rafaela is on call. The rest of Stagedoor works. The scanners talk to one API, scan.stagedoor.example, served by nginx on the two web servers. From her laptop, the API's address resolves, the port answers, and her browser shows a full-page warning. She has the cause within five minutes, because she asks the questions in order.

14.1A method: five questions in order

Every connection passes through the same layers, and each one can fail independently. Start at the bottom and stop at the first layer that fails: everything above it is a symptom.

  1. Name. getent hosts scan.stagedoor.example on the machine that fails (Chapter 13), and dig to see what DNS says. Is it the address you expect?
  2. Route. ip route get ADDRESS; ping if ICMP is allowed (many cloud firewalls block it, so a failed ping proves little); traceroute -T -p 443 ADDRESS to see how far packets get, using TCP so firewalls treat it like real traffic.
  3. Port. nc -zv -w 3 ADDRESS 443: “succeeded”, “Connection refused” (nothing listening) or a timeout (dropped on the way). The difference between the last two is the most useful fact in network debugging (Chapter 13).
  4. TLS. openssl s_client -connect ADDRESS:443 -servername NAME (14.3).
  5. Application. curl -v (14.2), and the server's own logs for the same request.

Run the checks from the machine that fails, or as close to it as you can. From your laptop, a different resolver, different routes and different firewall rules may give different answers, which is useful information in itself: if it works from here and not from there, the difference is in what's between.

14.2curl, verbosely

curl -v narrates every layer of one request: the address it resolved, the TCP connection, the TLS handshake and certificate, the HTTP request and response headers. Lines starting with * are curl's own commentary, > what it sent, < what it received. A few options make it a precise instrument:

curl -v https://scan.stagedoor.example/health                     # the whole story
curl -sS -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code} %{time_total}s\n' URL     # just status and time
curl --resolve scan.stagedoor.example:443:10.40.0.12 URL          # this name, but at this address (test one server)
curl --connect-timeout 3 --max-time 10 URL                        # fail fast instead of hanging
curl -H 'Host: scan.stagedoor.example' http://10.40.0.12/          # a virtual host by IP (plain HTTP)

--resolve deserves a special mention: it connects to an address you choose while keeping the real name for TLS and HTTP, so you can test one server behind a load balancer, or a new server before DNS points at it, with exactly the request a client would make. And curl's exit status names the layer that failed:

Table 14.1 · curl exit codes you'll see
CodeMeansLayer
6couldn't resolve hostname
7failed to connect (refused, or no route)route or port
28operation timed outroute, port (dropped), or a slow server
35TLS handshake failedTLS (protocol or cipher mismatch)
60certificate problem: expired, untrusted, wrong nameTLS (trust)
22HTTP status 400 or above, with --failapplication
52empty reply from serverapplication crashed or closed the connection (Chapter 5)

Watch out: curl -k

-k (--insecure) skips certificate checks. It's fine for one look at what a server is sending; it never belongs in a script, a health check or application code, because it turns a broken certificate (or an attacker's) into a working connection. If a script needs a private CA, give it --cacert.

14.3TLS and certificates from the command line

A TLS client checks four things about the server's certificate (Handbook 1, Chapter 6): it's within its validity dates; it's issued for the name the client asked for (in the Subject Alternative Name); it chains, through any intermediate certificates the server sends, to a root CA the client trusts; and it hasn't been revoked. Most failures are the first three:

Table 14.2 · Certificate errors and what they mean
Error (curl / openssl)MeansUsual fix
certificate has expired / num=10now is after notAfterrenew; automate renewal
no alternative certificate subject name matches / num=62wrong name: a certificate for another hostright certificate for the name, or the right server block in nginx
unable to get local issuer certificate / num=20the client can't chain it to a root it trustssend the intermediate (fullchain.pem), or install a private CA on clients
self-signed certificate / num=18nobody vouches for it but itselfa certificate from a CA
